Gromov’s dihedral rigidity conjecture in dimension three
Jinmin Wang, Zhizhang Xie, Guoliang Yu

Abstract
. In this article, we present a self-contained proof of Gromov’s dihedral rigidity conjecture on scalar curvature in the three-dimensional case. The proof avoids many of the technical complications that arise in higher dimensions, while still illustrating the essential ideas of the general approach developed in [ 31 ] and [ 30 ] . It is significantly shorter than the proof of the general case and is intended to be more accessible.



1.
Introduction

Gromov’s dihedral rigidity conjecture states that

Conjecture 1.1

(
[
15
,
16
]
)
.

Let
P
be a convex polyhedron in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
g_{0}
the Euclidean metric on
P
. If
g
is a smooth Riemannian metric on
P
such that the scalar curvature, mean curvature, and dihedral angles satisfy

(1)

\mathrm{Sc}(g)\geq\mathrm{Sc}(g_{0})=0
,

(2)

H_{g}(F_{i})\geq H_{g_{0}}(F_{i})=0
for each codimension one face
F_{i}
of
P
, and

(3)

\theta_{ij}(g)\leq\theta_{ij}(g_{0})
on
F_{ij}=F_{i}\cap F_{j}
for each pair of adjacent codimension one faces
F_{i}
and
F_{j}
,

then we have

\mathrm{Sc}(g)=0,H_{g}(F_{i})=0\textup{ and }\theta_{ij}(g)=\theta_{ij}(g_{0})

for all
i
and all
j\neq i
. Moreover,
(P,g)
is flat.

Here a pair of codimension one faces of
P
are called adjacent if their intersection is a nonempty codimension two face of
P
.

In dimension three, Li proved the dihedral rigidity conjecture for cone-type or prism type polyhedra under additional angle restrictions
[
24
]
. We also would like to point out Lott established an analogous rigidity theorem for even-dimensional manifolds with smooth boundaries (in which case dihedral angles do not appear)
[
26
]
.

In
[
31
]
and
[
30
]
, the authors proved the above conjecture–and in fact, established stronger generalizations of the conjecture–in all dimensions. The proof in the higher-dimensional case is rather technical. Following Gromov’s suggestions, we present in this paper a self-contained and significantly shorter proof of Gromov’s dihedral rigidity conjecture in the three-dimensional case. By restricting our attention to the three-dimensional case, we bypass many of the technical complications arising in the general setting, while still illustrating the essential ideas of the general approach.

More precisely, in this paper we give a self-contained proof of the following strengthened version of Gromov’s dihedral rigidity conjecture in dimension
3
.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
(M,g)
be a convex polyhedron in the Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, where
g
is the Euclidean metric. Let
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g})
be a connected spin polyhedral manifold of dimension three and
f\colon\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}\to M
be a polyhedral map with non-zero degree, such that the scalar curvature, mean curvature, and dihedral angles satisfy

(1)

\mathrm{Sc}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g})\geq\mathrm{Sc}(g)=0
,

(2)

H_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{i})\geq 0
for each codimension one face
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{i}
of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
, and

(3)

\theta_{ij}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g})\leq f^{\ast}\theta_{ij}(g)
on
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{ij}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{i}\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{j}
for each pair of adjacent codimension one faces
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{i}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{j}
,

then
\mathrm{Sc}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}}=0
,
H_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}}=0
, and
\theta_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}}=f^{*}\theta_{g}
. Moreover,
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g})
is flat.

The precise definitions of polyhedral manifolds and polyhedral maps are given in Section
2.1
. In this setting, the map
f
induces a homomorphism between the top relative homology groups:

f_{*}\colon H_{n}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},\partial\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M};\mathbb{Z})\to H_{n}(M,\partial M;\mathbb{Z}).

The degree of
f
is defined as the unique integer
\deg(f)
such that

f_{*}([\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},\partial\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}])=\deg(f)[M,\partial M].

Our strategy for proving Theorem
1.2
utilizes twisted Dirac operators equipped with suitable elliptic boundary conditions. A key step is to construct a solution to an appropriate twisted Dirac operator, subject to a natural boundary condition determined by the geometric setup, so that the classical Bochner-Lichnerowicz-Weitzenböck formula can be applied to obtain estimates for the scalar curvature and mean curvature (cf. Proposition
2.16
). A further computation using this solution also yields estimates for the dihedral angles (cf. Lemma
2.17
).
However, the presence of dihedral angles and higher-codimensional singularities introduces substantial analytical difficulties. In particular, these singularities make it considerably more challenging to formulate the relevant index theory rigorously and to carry out the associated index computations. Overcoming these difficulties constitutes the main contribution of our work.

Let us give a brief overview of the paper. Since
f\colon\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}\to M
is a spin map, the bundle
T\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}\oplus f^{\ast}TM
admits a spinor bundle
E=S(T\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}\oplus f^{\ast}TM)
. Let
D
be the (twisted) Dirac operator associated with the bundle
E
. There is a natural boundary condition
B
(cf. Definition
2.13
) determined by the unit inner normal vectors of the codimension-one faces of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
and their corresponding codimension-one faces in
M
.

A main ingredient of our proof is developing an index theory for this type of Dirac operator
D_{B}
subject to these boundary conditions.

(1).
First, we prove that the Dirac operator under consideration is Fredholm, provided that the dihedral angles of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
are strictly less than
\pi
and satisfy the prescribed comparison condition (Theorem
3.15
). We begin by establishing essential self-adjointness. Because
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
has polyhedral corners, we study
D_{B}
within Cheeger’s framework for conic-type operators (cf.
[
8
,
9
,
10
]
). A standard partition-of-unity argument shows that it is enough to prove that
D_{B}
is locally essentially self-adjoint near every point of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
(cf. Definition
3.2
). At points in the interior of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
or in the interior of a codimension-one face, local essential self-adjointness follows from classical elliptic regularity theory.

At points on higher-codimension faces, we establish a key reduction theorem. Under the relevant comparison condition on the dihedral angles,
D_{B}
is locally essentially self-adjoint near a point
x\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
if and only if the corresponding model operator on the tangent cone at
x
, equipped with the induced boundary condition, is essentially self-adjoint (Theorem
3.5
). Thus, the general problem reduces to model problems on Euclidean polyhedral cones.

If
x
lies in the interior of a codimension-two face (that is, an edge) of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
, Theorem
3.5
reduces the analysis to two-dimensional sectors in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
whose angles satisfy the required inequality. The relevant essential self-adjointness results are established in Lemmas
3.9
and
3.10
. Similarly, if
x
lies in a codimension-three face (that is, a vertex) of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
, the problem reduces to three-dimensional polyhedral cones in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. This case is treated in Lemmas
3.11
and
3.12
and Corollary
3.14
.

Once
D_{B}
is known to be essentially self-adjoint, its domain is the Sobolev space
H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E;B)
of
H^{1}
sections of
E
over
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
satisfying the boundary condition
B
. By the Rellich lemma, the inclusion
H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E;B)\hookrightarrow L^{2}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E)
is compact. A standard argument then shows that
D_{B}
is Fredholm.

(2).
Second, we compute the Fredholm index
\textup{Ind}(D_{B})
(Theorem
6.4
). A direct computation on
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
is difficult because of its singularities. Instead, we use a cutting-and-pasting argument to reduce the problem to manifolds with smooth boundary, where the index computation is classical
[
1
]
; see also
[
26
]
.

Figure 1.
Deformation of small neighborhoods of the vertices of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
that makes both the metric and the adjacent faces flat.

We first deform the boundary conditions (Proposition
6.3
) and the metric in small neighborhoods of the vertices of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
so that both the metric and the faces become flat near each vertex (see Figure
1
), without changing the Fredholm index. For technical reasons, we work with boundary conditions more general than those naturally induced by the unit inner normal vector fields on the codimension-one faces of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
and
M
(cf. Lemma
6.2
and Proposition
6.3
). We then apply the gluing formula of Theorem
4.1
. Suppose that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
is cut into two pieces,
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{2}
, along a hypersurface
\Sigma
that meets every codimension-one face it intersects orthogonally, and that the corresponding boundary condition is imposed along
\Sigma
. The formula states that the Fredholm index of
D_{B}
on
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
is the sum of the Fredholm indices of the corresponding Dirac operators on
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{2}
. It therefore allows us to cut off a small polyhedral neighborhood of each vertex along a spherical link (see Figure
2
).
1
1

1

More precisely, we cut along a convex hypersurface that is a slight modification of the spherical link; see Example
5.5
.
A crucial point is to show that the Fredholm index on each cut-off piece vanishes. We prove this by combining a deformation argument with the approximation result in Lemma
5.2
and Corollary
5.3
.

Figure 2.
Cutting off a small neighborhood of each vertex using the gluing formula (Theorem
4.1
). In the right-hand figure, some hidden dashed edges are omitted.

Applying this procedure at every vertex of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
yields a new polyhedral manifold
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}^{\prime}
(see Figure
3
). Removing a small neighborhood of a vertex
v\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
creates new vertices and edges. To distinguish the original edges from the newly created ones, let
\mathcal{E}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}
denote the set of edges of the original manifold
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
. By a slight abuse of notation, after the truncation we continue to use
\mathcal{E}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}
for the remaining segments of those edges. Thus, the new edges of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}^{\prime}
created by the cutting procedure are not included in
\mathcal{E}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}
.

Let
\Gamma
be an edge of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}^{\prime}
so that
\Gamma\in\mathcal{E}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}
. We deform the boundary conditions and the metric near
\Gamma
so that the metric and the adjacent faces become flat (see Figure
4
), and then excise a small neighborhood by cutting along a surface
\Sigma_{\Gamma}
(see Figure
5
). Here
\Sigma_{\Gamma}
is the product of
\Gamma
with a circular arc of small radius. Geometrically, the cut-off region is the product
\mathbb{G}\times I
of a sector
\mathbb{G}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
and an interval
I
(see the left-hand figure of Figure
6
). We show that the Fredholm index of the associated Dirac operator on this region vanishes by combining a deformation argument with the approximation result in Lemma
5.2
and Corollary
5.3
.

.

The boundary of the sector
\mathbb{G}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
consists of three pieces: two radial line segments emanating from the origin and a circular arc of small radius
\varepsilon
(see the left-hand figure of Figure
7
). We replace the two radial segments by a smooth convex curve that meets the circular arc orthogonally at both endpoints, thereby obtaining a convex region
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
(see the right-hand figure of Figure
7
). We then form the product
U\times I
(see the right-hand figure of Figure
6
). We likewise show that the Fredholm index of the associated Dirac operator on
U\times I
, equipped with the corresponding boundary condition, is zero. Gluing
U\times I
to
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}^{\prime}
along
\Sigma_{\Gamma}
preserves the Fredholm index by Theorem
4.1
(see Figure
8
). Geometrically, this operation smooths the edge
\Gamma
; in particular, it simultaneously smooths the vertices at the two endpoints of
\Gamma
.

U\times I
to
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}^{\prime}
along
\Sigma_{\Gamma}
.

Repeating this operation for every edge in
\mathcal{E}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}
produces a polyhedral manifold
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}^{\prime\prime}
with no vertices (see Figure
9
), although its still has codimension-two faces consists of closed smooth curves. Applying the same cutting-and-pasting construction to small neighborhoods of these curves removes the remaining codimension-two singularities and yields a manifold with smooth boundary (see Figure
10
), while preserving the Fredholm index. Thus, the index problem on a polyhedral manifold is reduced to the classical smooth boundary case
[
1
]
; see also
[
26
]
.

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}^{\prime\prime}
obtained after smoothing every edge in
\mathcal{E}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}
.

Figure 10.
The resulting manifold with smooth boundary.

(3).
Finally, we remove the strict comparison hypothesis. Theorem
6.4
computes the Fredholm index only under the stronger hypothesis that the comparison inequalities for the dihedral angles—or, equivalently, for the inner products of adjacent vectors—are strict (see condition
6.2
). It therefore does not directly establish Gromov’s dihedral rigidity conjecture. To pass to the nonstrict case, we approximate the geometric data in Theorem
1.2
by a sequence of geometric data and associated boundary conditions satisfying the strict comparison condition
6.2
. For each approximation, Theorem
6.4
gives a nonzero index; hence, the corresponding Dirac operator has a nontrivial solution
\varphi_{n}
. The approximation lemma (Lemma
5.2
) then yields a nontrivial solution
\varphi\in H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E;B)
for the Dirac operator associated with the original geometric data and boundary condition. Applying the Bochner–Lichnerowicz–Weitzenböck formula to
\varphi
gives the required equalities for the scalar and mean curvatures (see Lemma
2.17
). Equality of the dihedral angles follows from a separate elementary computation using the boundary condition; see also
[
30
]
.

After our papers
[
31
,
30
]
appeared on arXiv, Brendle proved a case of Gromov’s dihedral rigidity conjecture via a different approach under the additional assumption that all corresponding angles (not only dihedral angles) are equal
[
5
]
.
Subsequently, Brendle and Wang proved another case of Gromov’s dihedral rigidity conjecture under the assumption that all angles are acute, that is, all angles are
\leq\pi/2

[
6
]
.

The authors thank Gromov for many stimulating discussions and for suggesting that we write up a self-contained proof of the dihedral rigidity conjecture in the three-dimensional case.

2.
Geometric setup of an index problem

In this section, we establish the geometric setup for our index problem. This includes defining the relevant vector bundles, elliptic operators, and boundary conditions, as well as clarifying their relationship to Theorem
1.2
. Our focus will primarily be on the three-dimensional case.

2.1.
Polyhedral manifolds and twisted Dirac operators

In this subsection, we describe the basic setup of the index problem and provide some key geometric estimates.

The geometric objects investigated in this paper are manifolds with polyhedral boundaries, which we will henceforth refer to as
polyhedral manifolds
. As the current paper is concerned with the three-dimensional case of Gromov’s dihedral rigidity conjecture, we shall focus on the dimension three case for simplicity.

First, we introduce the following notion of polyhedral cones. A one-dimensional polyhedral cone in
\mathbb{R}^{1}
is simply the ray
[0,\infty)\subset\mathbb{R}^{1}
.

Definition 2.1
.

A two dimensional polyhedral cone
C
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
is a cone (i.e., for any
v\in C
and
\lambda\geq 0
,
\lambda v\in C
) such that its link, which is defined to be
C\cap\mathbb{S}^{1}
, is a connected arc.

To define three-dimensional polyhedral cones in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, we first recall the definition of spherical polygons.

Definition 2.2
.

Let
\mathbb{S}^{2}
be the unit sphere in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. A spherical polygon
P\subset\mathbb{S}^{2}
is a two-dimensional submanifold with boundary such that its boundary
\partial P
is piecewise smooth and consists of a finite union of geodesic arcs.

Definition 2.3
.

A three-dimensional polyhedral cone
C
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
is a cone (i.e., for any
v\in C
and
\lambda\geq 0
, we have
\lambda v\in C
) whose link, defined by
C\cap\mathbb{S}^{2}
, is a connected two-dimensional spherical polygon. In this case, the codimension-one faces of
C
are precisely the intersections of
C
with its bounding planes.

Let
(X,h)
be an open, oriented,
3
-dimensional Riemannian manifold and let
M\subset X
be an
3
-dimensional topological manifold with boundary.

Definition 2.4
.

The tangent cone
\mathcal{C}_{x}M\subset T_{x}X
at a point
x\in M
is defined as the set of vectors
v\in T_{x}X
such that there exists a smooth curve
\gamma:[0,\epsilon)\to M
with
\gamma(0)=x
and
\dot{\gamma}(0)=v
.

We adopt the following definition of polyhedral manifolds.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
(X,h)
be an open, oriented,
3
-dimensional Riemannian manifold and let
M\subset X
be a compact region. We say that
(M,g)
, with
g\coloneqq h|_{M}
, is a
polyhedral manifold
if
M
is a
3
-dimensional topological manifold with boundary and there exists a finite collection of pairwise transversal smooth open hypersurfaces
\mathcal{H}=\{H_{1},\dots,H_{p}\}
in
X
such that:

(1)

The boundary
\partial M
is contained in the union of these hypersurfaces:

\partial M\subset\bigcup_{i=1}^{p}H_{i}

(2)

For any point
x\in\partial M
, the tangent cone
\mathcal{C}_{x}M\subset T_{x}X
is a three dimensional polyhedral cone (identifying
T_{x}X
with
\mathbb{R}^{3}
) in the sense of Definition
2.3
.

(3)

Furthermore, the set of bounding planes of the polyhedral cone
\mathcal{C}_{x}M
is exactly the tangent planes
\{T_{x}H_{j}\mid H_{j}\in\mathcal{H}\text{ and }x\in H_{j}\}
.

We say a function on
M
is
smooth
on
M
if it is the restriction to
M
of some smooth function on
X
. Similarly, smooth vector bundles on
M
are restrictions to
M
of smooth vector bundles on
X
, and smooth sections of a smooth vector bundle over
M
are restrictions of smooth sections of smooth vector bundles on
X
.

Let
M
be a
3
-dimensional polyhedral manifold. Let
\mathcal{S}_{k}
be the set of points
x\in M
for which
k
is the largest integer such that the tangent cone
\mathcal{C}_{x}M
splits as
T_{x}M\cong\mathbb{R}^{k}\times C^{\prime}_{x}
, where
C^{\prime}_{x}
is a polyhedral cone in
\mathbb{R}^{3-k}
. The definition of a polyhedral manifold ensures that
\mathcal{S}_{k}
is an open manifold of dimension
k
.

Definition 2.6
.

Let
\mathring{F}
be a connected component of
\mathcal{S}_{k}
, and let
F
denote its closure in
M
. We call
F
a codimension
(3-k)
face (or equivalently, a dimension
k
face) of
M
.

This collection of faces, ordered by inclusion, endows
M
with a natural stratification.

Definition 2.7
.

Let
F
be a codimension
(3-k)
face of
M
, as introduced in Definition
2.6
. For each
x\in\mathring{F}
, we define the normal cone
\mathcal{C}^{\perp}_{x}F
of
F
at
x
to be

\mathcal{C}^{\perp}_{x}F=\mathcal{C}_{x}M\cap(T_{x}F)^{\perp},

where
(T_{x}F)^{\perp}
denotes the orthogonal complement of the tangent space
T_{x}F
within the ambient tangent space
T_{x}X
.

We now discuss the types of maps between three dimensional polyhedral manifolds that are relevant for this paper.

Definition 2.8
.

Let
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g})
and
(M,g)
be three dimensional polyhedral manifolds. A map
f\colon\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}\to M
is called a polyhedral map, if

(1)

f
is Lipschitz;

(2)

f
is smooth away from the vertices;

(3)

f
maps each codimension
k
face
{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}}^{k}_{i}
in
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
to a codimension
k
face
F^{k}_{\varphi(i)}
of
M
, where
\varphi
is a map from the collection of faces of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
to the collection of faces of
M
such that each pair of adjacent
2
2

2

A pair of codimension one faces of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
are called adjacent if their intersection is a nonempty codimension two face of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
. Since
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
has dimension three, this simply means a pair of faces of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
are adjacent if their intersection is an edge of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
.
codimension one faces of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
are mapped to a pair of adjacent codimension one faces of
M
.

Remark 2.9
.

Condition (3) requires the existence of a specific assignment
i\mapsto\varphi(i)
that maps each face of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
to a face of
M
of the same codimension. This data is crucial for the subsequent analysis of dihedral angle comparisons.

It is important to note that
f
is allowed to be “degenerate” on some faces, meaning the image
f(\overline{F}^{k}_{i})
may lie in a subset of
F^{k}_{\varphi(i)}
with strictly lower dimension. For instance, if
f
maps a 2-dimensional face
\overline{F}
of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
to a 2-dimensional face
F
of
M
, the image
f(\overline{F})
might be contained entirely within an edge
e\subset F
, or even a vertex. In such a case, the image
f(\overline{F})
is contained in more than one 2-dimensional faces of
M
(i.e. those
2
-dimensional faces containing
e
). Since the dihedral angles are defined between two specific adjacent faces, the map
\varphi
resolves this ambiguity by explicitly specifying which target faces are being compared.

We emphasize that a polyhedral map
f\colon\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}\to M
between
3
-dimensional polyhedral manifolds is not required to be smooth at the vertices. For example, for two polyhedra
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
of the same combinatorial type, one can construct a polyhedral map from
P_{1}
to
P_{2}
by defining a smooth
f
on the interior, the
2
-dimensional faces, and the edges, but excluding small neighborhoods of the vertices, and then extending
f
radially toward the vertices within these neighborhoods. If there are four or more 2-dimensional faces meeting at a vertex, such a map generally is not smooth at that vertex.

Definition 2.10
.

Let
f\colon(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g})\to(M,g)
be a polyhedral map between Riemannian polyhedral manifolds of dimension three. Denote by
T\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
and
TM
the tangent bundles of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
and
M
, respectively. The map
f
is called spin if the bundle
T\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}\oplus f^{\ast}TM
admits a spin structure.

Now suppose
f\colon(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g})\to(M,g)
is a spin polyhedral map between Riemannian polyhedral manifolds of dimension three. We consider the spinor bundle associated with the vector bundle

:

E\coloneqq S(T\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}\oplus f^{*}TM)

equipped with the spinorial connection

and

on
T\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
and

, respectively. Denote by
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}

and
\hat{c}

of

. Since
T\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}\oplus f^{\ast}TM
has even rank, there exists a natural
\mathbb{Z}/2
grading operator
\mathscr{E}=i^{3}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e}_{1})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e}_{2})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e}_{3})\hat{c}(e_{1})\hat{c}(e_{2})\hat{c}(e_{3})
on

, where
\{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e}_{i}\}
and
\{e_{i}\}
are oriented local orthonormal basis of
T\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
and
f^{\ast}TM
. Let us define

c\coloneqq i\mathscr{E}\hat{c}.

Note that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})
and
c(u)
commute with each other for all
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}\in T\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
and
u\in f^{\ast}TM
. We extend these to Clifford actions of
2
-forms by setting

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}\wedge\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{v})=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{v})~\mathrm{and}~c(u\wedge v)=c(u)c(v),

whenever
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{u}\perp\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{v}
and

, for

and

.

Definition 2.11
.

We define

to be the space of smooth sections of

over

that vanish near all codimension two faces.

Consider the Dirac operator

D=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e}_{i})\nabla_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e}_{i}}

acting on

. For any
\sigma\in C^{\infty}_{00}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E)
, the Stokes formula reads:

(2.1)

\int_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}|D\sigma|^{2}=\int_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}|\nabla\sigma|^{2}+\int_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}\langle\mathscr{R}\sigma,\sigma\rangle\\
+\sum_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}}\int_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}}\langle D^{\partial}\sigma,\sigma\rangle,

where
\{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}\}
is the collection of codimension one faces of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}

(2.2)

\mathscr{R}=\frac{\mathrm{Sc}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}}}{4}-\frac{1}{4}\sum_{i\neq j}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e}_{i})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e}_{j})c(R^{g}(f_{*}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e}_{i}\wedge f_{*}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e}_{j})),

with

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}
and
R^{g}
the curvature operator
3
3

3

Our convention is that the curvature operator of the unit round sphere is the identity operator.
acting on
\mathord{\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{$\bigwedge$}}}}}}}^{2}TM
. The boundary operator is given as

(2.3)

D^{\partial}=\sum_{\mu=1}^{n-1}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e}_{\mu})\nabla_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e}_{\mu}},

where
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k}
is the unit inner normal vector of

, and
\{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e}_{\mu}\}
is a local orthonormal frame of

We have the following lemma estimating the curvature term
\mathscr{R}
; see
[

, Section 1.1]
.

Lemma 2.12
.

If the curvature operator of
(M,g)
is non-negative, then

(2.4)

\mathscr{R}\geq\frac{\mathrm{Sc}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}}}{4}-\|\wedge^{2}f_{*}\|\frac{f^{*}\mathrm{Sc}_{g}}{4}.

Proof.

At any point
x\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
, consider the singular value decomposition of the map

R^{g}\circ\wedge^{2}f_{*}\colon\mathord{\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{$\bigwedge$}}}}}}}^{2}T_{x}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}\to\mathord{\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{$\bigwedge$}}}}}}}^{2}T_{f(x)}M,

that is,

R^{g}\circ\wedge^{2}f_{*}(\alpha_{i})=\lambda_{i}\beta_{i},~\forall 1\leq i\leq n(n-1)/2,

for some orthonormal bases
\{\alpha_{i}\}
of
\mathord{\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{$\bigwedge$}}}}}}}^{2}T_{x}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
and
\{\beta_{i}\}
of
\mathord{\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{$\bigwedge$}}}}}}}^{2}T_{f(x)}M
, and
\lambda_{i}\geq 0
.
Then

(2.5)

\begin{split}&-\frac{1}{4}\sum_{i\neq j}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e}_{i})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e}_{j})c(R^{g}(f_{*}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e}_{i}\wedge f_{*}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e}_{j}))=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\alpha_{i})c(\beta_{i})\lambda_{i}\\
\geq&-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}=-\frac{1}{2}\|R^{g}\circ\wedge^{2}f_{*}\|_{1}\\
\geq&-\frac{1}{2}\|R^{g}\|_{1}\cdot\|\wedge^{2}f_{*}\|=-\frac{1}{2}\|\wedge^{2}f_{*}\|\cdot\mathrm{tr}(R^{g})=-\|\wedge^{2}f_{*}\|\frac{f^{*}\mathrm{Sc}_{g}}{4}.\end{split}

∎

2.2.
Local boundary conditions

In this subsection, we describe a local boundary condition for sections of
E=S(T\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}\oplus f^{\ast}TM)
over
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
and discuss the relationship between the associated boundary value problem and Theorem
1.2
.

Definition 2.13
.

Let
B
denote the following boundary condition for sections of
E
over
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
: a smooth section

,

\mathscr{E}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k})c(f^{*}\nu_{k})\sigma=-\sigma,

where
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k}
is the unit inner normal vector of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
and
\nu_{k}
is the unit inner normal vector of its corresponding
4
4

4

See Definition

for the precise definition of the correspondence between codimension one faces of

and

under the map
f
.
codimension one face

of

. The space of all such sections is denoted by

. For convenience, we define the operator

(2.6)

\gamma_{k}\coloneqq\mathscr{E}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k})c(f^{*}\nu_{k}).

It is straightforward to verify that the Dirac operator

is symmetric with respect to the boundary condition

\sigma,\tau\in C^{\infty}_{00}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E;B)

\int_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}\langle D\sigma,\tau\rangle=\int_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}\langle\sigma,D\tau\rangle.

Example 2.14
.

Consider the special case where
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}=M
and
f
is the identity map. In this case, the bundle
E=S(T\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}\oplus T\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M})
is naturally identified with the exterior algebra bundle
\Lambda^{*}T\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
. Under this identification, the Dirac operator
D
on
E
becomes the de Rham operator of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
. The restriction of a section
\sigma\in C^{\infty}_{00}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},\Lambda^{*}T\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M})
to a codimension one face
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
admits a decomposition
\sigma=\alpha+\nu_{k}^{\ast}\wedge\beta
, where
\nu_{k}^{\ast}
is the 1-form dual to the unit normal vector
\nu_{k}
, and
\alpha,\beta
are tangential forms (i.e., their contraction with
\nu_{k}
vanishes). In this special case, the section
\sigma
satisfies the boundary condition
B
on
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
if and only if
\beta=0
on
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
. This is referred to as the
absolute boundary condition
for the de Rham operator in the literature.

Let
\gamma_{k}
be the self-adjoint involution from line (
2.6
). The super-commutator

\mathscr{A}\coloneqq-\frac{1}{2}(D^{\partial}\gamma_{k}+\gamma_{k}D^{\partial})

defines a bundle endomorphism of

. Therefore, if
\sigma\in C^{\infty}_{00}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E;B)
, then it follows from
\gamma_{k}\sigma=-\sigma
that line (

) becomes

(2.7)

\int_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}|D\sigma|^{2}=\int_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}|\nabla\sigma|^{2}+\int_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}\langle\mathscr{R}\sigma,\sigma\rangle\\
+\sum_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}}\int_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}}\langle\mathscr{A}\sigma,\sigma\rangle,

A direct computation yields

(2.8)

\begin{split}\mathscr{A}=&\frac{H_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}}}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mu=1}^{n-1}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e}_{\mu})c(\nu_{k})c(\nabla^{M}_{f_{*}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e}_{\mu}}\nu_{k})\\
=&\frac{H_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}}}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mu=1}^{n-1}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e}_{\mu})c(\nu_{k})c(A^{g}f_{*}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e}_{\mu}),\end{split}

where
H_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}}
is the mean curvature of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
and

denotes the second fundamental form
5
5

5

Our convention is that the second fundamental form of the unit sphere as the boundary of the unit ball is the identity operator.
of

.
We now estimate the endomorphism

(cf.

Lemma 2.15
.

If the second fundamental form

of each codimension one face

(2.9)

\mathscr{A}\geq\frac{H_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}}}{2}-\|f_{*}^{\partial}\|\cdot\frac{f^{*}H_{g}}{2},

\partial\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}

Proof.

The argument is similar to the proof of Lemma
2.12
.
For any point
x\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
, consider the singular value decomposition of the map

A^{g}\circ f^{\partial}_{*}\colon T_{p}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}\to T_{f(p)}F_{k},

that is,

A^{g}\circ f^{\partial}_{*}(\alpha_{\mu})=\lambda_{\mu}\beta_{\mu},~\forall 1\leq\mu\leq n-1,

for some orthonormal bases
\{\alpha_{\mu}\}
of
T_{p}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
and
\{\beta_{\mu}\}
of
T_{f(p)}F_{k}
, and
\lambda_{\mu}\geq 0
.
Then we have

(2.10)

\begin{split}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mu=1}^{n-1}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e}_{\mu})c(\nu_{k})c(A^{g}f_{*}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e}_{\mu})=&-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mu=1}^{n-1}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\alpha_{\mu})c(\nu_{k})c(\beta_{\mu})\lambda_{\mu}\\
\geq&-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mu=1}^{n-1}\lambda_{\mu}=-\frac{1}{2}\|A^{g}\circ f^{\partial}_{*}\|_{1}\\
\geq&-\frac{1}{2}\|A^{g}\|_{1}\cdot\|f^{\partial}_{*}\|\\
=&-\frac{1}{2}\|f^{\partial}_{*}\|\cdot\mathrm{tr}(A^{g})=-\|f^{\partial}_{*}\|\frac{f^{*}H_{g}}{2}.\end{split}

∎

Since
f\colon\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}\to M
is a Lipschitz map, the pullback bundle
f^{*}TM
is a Lipschitz vector bundle. Consequently, the Sobolev space of sections
H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E)
is well-defined. By (
2.7
) and the Sobolev trace theorem, there exist constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that

C_{1}\|\sigma\|_{1}\leq\|\sigma\|_{D}\leq C_{2}\|\sigma\|_{1}

for all
\sigma\in C^{\infty}_{00}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E;B)
, where
\|\cdot\|_{1}
denotes the standard
H^{1}
-norm

\|\sigma\|_{1}^{2}=\|\sigma\|^{2}+\|\nabla\sigma\|^{2}

and
\|\cdot\|_{D}
denotes the graph norm
\|\sigma\|_{D}^{2}=\|\sigma\|^{2}+\|D\sigma\|^{2}
. Thus, the two norms are equivalent on this subspace.

We define
H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E;B)
to be the closure of
C^{\infty}_{00}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E;B)
with respect to the
H^{1}
-norm. It is a standard fact that subsets of codimension two have vanishing
H^{1}
-capacity; therefore,
H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E;B)
coincides with the closed subspace of
H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E)
consisting of sections whose traces on the codimension-one faces satisfy the boundary condition
B
. In particular, the identity (
2.7
) extends to all
\sigma\in H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E;B)
.

To summarize the preceding discussion, we obtain the following proposition.

Proposition 2.16
.

With the notation as above, assume that
M
has a non-negative curvature operator and that each codimension-one face of
M
has a non-negative second fundamental form. Then, for any
\sigma\in C^{\infty}_{00}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E;B)
, the following inequality holds:

(2.11)

\begin{split}\int_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}|D\sigma|^{2}\geq&\int_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}|\nabla\sigma|^{2}+\int_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}\frac{\mathrm{Sc}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}}-\|\wedge^{2}f_{*}\|\cdot f^{*}\mathrm{Sc}_{g}}{4}|\sigma|^{2}\\
&+\sum_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}}\int_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}}\frac{H_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}}-\|f^{\partial}_{*}\|\cdot f^{*}H_{g}}{2}|\sigma|^{2},\end{split}

where the sum runs over all codimension-one faces
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
. Moreover, the inequality (
2.11
) extends to any
\sigma\in H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E;B)
.

The following lemma shows how the boundary value problem for the Dirac operator
D
is applied to resolve Gromov’s dihedral rigidity conjecture in dimension three.

Lemma 2.17
.

Suppose
(M,g)
is a three dimensional convex Euclidean polyhedron, where
g
is the Euclidean metric. Let
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g})
be a three dimensional connected polyhedral manifold such that

\mathrm{Sc}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}}\geq 0
and
H_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}}\geq 0.
Let
f\colon(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g})\to(M,g)
be a spin polyhedral map and
E=S(T\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}\oplus f^{\ast}TM)
.
If

•

there exists a non-zero
\sigma\in H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E;B)
satisfying
D\sigma=0
,

then
\mathrm{Sc}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}}=0
,
H_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}}=0
, and
\theta_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}}=f^{*}\theta_{g}
. Moreover,
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g})
is flat.

Proof.

Let
\sigma\in H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E;B)
be a non-zero section satisfying
D\sigma=0
. By hypothesis, the scalar curvature
\mathrm{Sc}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}}
and the mean curvature
H_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}}
are non-negative. Applying the inequality (
2.11
) from Proposition
2.16
, the vanishing of
D\sigma
combined with the non-negativity of the geometric terms forces the inequality to become an equality. In particular, we deduce that
\nabla\sigma=0
; that is,
\sigma
is a parallel section. On a connected manifold, a non-zero parallel section is nowhere vanishing.
6
6

6

In the current setting, since
(M,g)
is Euclidean, the bundle
TM
is a trivial bundle equipped with a flat connection. Consequently,
E=S(T\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}\oplus f^{*}TM)\cong S(T\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}\oplus\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{3})
is a smooth vector bundle over
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
, where
\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{3}=f^{*}TM
is a trivial flat bundle. Standard regularity theory implies that a parallel section is smooth.
Consequently, the inequality (
2.11
) implies that
\mathrm{Sc}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}}=0
and
H_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}}=0
.

Since
\sigma
is parallel, the Ricci identity for spinors implies that

0=\sum_{j=1}^{3}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e}_{j})\left(\nabla_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e}_{i}}\nabla_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e}_{j}}-\nabla_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e}_{j}}\nabla_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e}_{i}}-\nabla_{[\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e}_{i},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e}_{j}]}\right)\sigma=-\frac{1}{2}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\textup{Ric}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e}_{i}))\sigma

for each vector
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e}_{i}
, where
\{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e}_{j}\}
is a local orthonormal frame of
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g})
. This implies that
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g})
is Ricci-flat. Since
\dim\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}=3
, the Riemann curvature tensor is determined by the Ricci tensor; thus,
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g})
is flat.

Finally, consider two adjacent codimension one faces
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{i}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{j}
, and suppose
x\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{i}\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{j}
. Since
\sigma
satisfies the boundary condition
B
(see Definition
2.13
) on each face, we have:

\mathscr{E}c(\nu_{i,f(x)})\sigma_{x}=-\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{i,x})\sigma_{x}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathscr{E}c(\nu_{j,f(x)})\sigma_{x}=-\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{j,x})\sigma_{x}.

where
\sigma_{x}
is the value of
\sigma
at
x
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{i,x}
is the value of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{i}
at
x
.
By linearity, for any
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
,

\mathscr{E}c\big(a\nu_{i,f(x)}+b\nu_{j,f(x)}\big)\sigma_{x}=-\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}\big(a\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{i,x}+b\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{j,x}\big)\sigma_{x}.

Taking the squared norm of both sides and dividing by
|\sigma_{x}|^{2}
(which is non-zero), we obtain

|a\nu_{i,f(x)}+b\nu_{j,f(x)}|^{2}=|a\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{i,x}+b\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{j,x}|^{2}.

Expanding the squares and using the fact that the normal vectors are chosen to have unit length, we conclude that

\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{i,x},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{j,x}\rangle=\langle\nu_{i,f(x)},\nu_{j,f(x)}\rangle.

It follows that the dihedral angles satisfy
\theta_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}}(x)=\theta_{g}(f(x))
.
∎

3.
Essential self-adjointness of twisted Dirac operators on polyhedral manifolds

In this section, we show that the twisted Dirac operator
D
associated with the bundle
E=S(T\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}\oplus f^{*}TM)
(introduced in Section
2
) is essentially self-adjoint subject to the boundary condition
B
described in Definition
2.13
, under suitable assumptions on the comparison of dihedral angles. As a consequence, we conclude that such an operator
D_{B}
is Fredholm.

We begin by recalling the notion of essential self-adjointness for unbounded symmetric operators.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
D
be the Dirac operator with initial domain
C^{\infty}_{00}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E;B)
as in Section
2
. The minimal domain of
D
is the closure of
C^{\infty}_{00}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E;B)
with respect to the Sobolev norm
\|\cdot\|_{1}
, which is
H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E;B)
.
We define the maximal domain
\mathcal{D}_{\max}(D)
of
D
as the space of sections
\xi\in L^{2}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E)
such that the linear functional

\sigma\in C^{\infty}_{00}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E;B)\mapsto\int_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}\langle\xi,D\sigma\rangle\in\mathbb{C}

extends to a bounded linear functional on
L^{2}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E)
.

We say that
D
is
essentially self-adjoint
if the minimal and maximal domains coincide, i.e.,
\mathcal{D}_{\max}(D)=H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E;B)
.

If a section lies in
\mathcal{D}_{\max}(D)
but not in
H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E;B)
, it must fail to be an
H^{1}
-section near some point of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
. Therefore, by a standard partition-of-unity argument, the question of essential self-adjointness can be studied locally in arbitrarily small neighborhoods of each point.

Definition 3.2
.

Let
D
be the twisted Dirac operator associated with the bundle
E=S(T\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}\oplus f^{*}TM)
, subject to the boundary condition
B
, as above. We say that
D
is
locally essentially self-adjoint
at a point
x\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
if there exists an open neighborhood
U
of
x
such that every section
\xi\in\mathcal{D}_{\max}(D)
with support contained in
U
belongs to
H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E;B)
.

Consequently,
D
is (globally) essentially self-adjoint if and only if it is locally essentially self-adjoint at every point
x\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
.

3.1.
Reduction to standard model cases

In this subsection, we discuss how to reduce the verification of the essential self-adjointness of
D_{B}
to certain standard model cases.

We begin by describing two typical geometric situations that will play a central role in the reduction process.

Example 3.3
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a region whose boundary, near the origin, consists of two smooth surfaces
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
meeting transversely along a curve
\Gamma
. Assume that
\Gamma
passes through the origin and is tangent to the
x
-axis at the origin. Let
\theta\in(0,\pi)
denote the dihedral angle between
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
at the origin.

Suppose the tangent planes
T_{0}\Sigma_{1}
and
T_{0}\Sigma_{2}
of
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
at the origin. We shall construct a local diffeomorphism near the origin of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
such that it maps
\Sigma_{i}
to
T_{0}\Sigma_{i}
.

By performing a rotation if necessary, we assume without loss of generality that
T_{0}\Sigma_{1}
is
\Pi_{1}=\{(x,y,z)\mid z=0\}
and
T_{0}\Sigma_{2}
is
\Pi_{2}=\{(x,y,z)\mid y\sin\theta-z\cos\theta=0\}
.

Consider smooth defining functions
g_{1},g_{2}:U\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\Sigma_{i}\cap U=\{(x,y,z)\in U\mid g_{i}(x,y,z)=0\}
, where
U
is a sufficiently small neighborhood of the origin. We normalize these functions such that their gradients at the origin align with the unit inner normal vectors of
\Pi_{1}
and
\Pi_{2}
:

\nabla g_{1}(0)=(0,0,1),\quad\nabla g_{2}(0)=(0,\sin\theta,-\cos\theta).

We define the diffeomorphism
\Phi\colon U\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
explicitly as:

\Phi(x,y,z)=\left(x,\frac{g_{2}(x,y,z)+g_{1}(x,y,z)\cos\theta}{\sin\theta},g_{1}(x,y,z)\right)

Now a straightforward computation shows that
\Phi
is a local diffeomorphism and its derivative at the origin is the identity map, that is,
\Phi^{\prime}(0)=I
. By construction,
\Phi
maps
\Sigma_{i}
to
T_{0}\Sigma_{i}
.

Example 3.4
.

Suppose
N
is a three-dimensional polyhedral manifold in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and the origin
0
is a vertex of
N
. The boundary
\partial N
near the origin consists of the collection of smooth surfaces
\mathcal{S}=\{\Sigma_{1},\dots,\Sigma_{n}\}
passing through the origin. The tangent planes
\Pi_{i}=T_{0}\Sigma_{i}
enclose a polyhedral cone
\mathbb{F}
(see Definition
2.3
) in the tangent space
T_{0}\mathbb{R}^{3}\cong\mathbb{R}^{3}
. We shall construct a local
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism near the origin of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
such that it simultaneously maps each
\Sigma_{i}
to its tangent plane
\Pi_{i}
.

For each pair of adjacent
\Sigma_{i}
and
\Sigma_{j}
, let
\Phi_{ij}\colon U\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
be the diffeomorphism constructed as in Example
3.3
that maps
\Sigma_{i}
and
\Sigma_{j}
to its corresponding tangent plane at the origin and
\Phi^{\prime}_{ij}(0)=I
. To obtain a single map
\Phi\colon U\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
that simultaneously maps each
\Sigma_{i}
to its tangent plane
\Pi_{i}
, we pass to polar coordinates.
The link
\mathbb{L}=\mathbb{F}\cap\mathbb{S}^{2}
of the polyhedral cone
\mathbb{F}
is a spherical polygon in
\mathbb{S}^{2}
. Let
\{W_{\alpha}\}
be an open cover of
\mathbb{S}^{2}
such that each
W_{\alpha}
contains at most one vertex of the polygon
\mathbb{L}
, and, if
W_{\alpha}
does not contain a vertex of
\mathbb{L}
, then it intersects at most one edge of
\mathbb{L}
. Let
\{\rho_{\alpha}\}
be a partition of unity subordinate to this cover. For each
W_{\alpha}
, choose
\Phi_{\alpha}\colon U\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
as follows:

(1)

If
W_{\alpha}
contains a vertex of
\mathbb{L}
formed by the intersection
\Pi_{i}\cap\Pi_{j}\cap\mathbb{S}^{2}
, we set
\Phi_{\alpha}=\Phi_{ij}
.

(2)

If
W_{\alpha}
intersects an edge
\Pi_{i}\cap\mathbb{S}^{2}
but contains no vertices of
\mathbb{L}
, consider the endpoints of this edge, which are the vertices of
\mathbb{L}
given by
\Pi_{i}\cap\Pi_{j}\cap\mathbb{S}^{2}
and
\Pi_{i}\cap\Pi_{k}\cap\mathbb{S}^{2}
. Both associated maps,
\Phi_{ij}
and
\Phi_{ik}
, map
\Sigma_{i}
to
\Pi_{i}
; thus, we set
\Phi_{\alpha}
to be either one.

(3)

If
W_{\alpha}
is disjoint from the edges and vertices of
\mathbb{L}
, then we set
\Phi_{\alpha}
to be the identity map.

We define the map
\Phi\colon U\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
by:

(3.1)

\Phi(x)=\sum_{\alpha}\rho_{\alpha}(\sigma)\Phi_{\alpha}(x),

where
x\in U\setminus\{0\}
is expressed in polar coordinates as
x=r\sigma
with
r=\|x\|
and
\sigma=x/\|x\|\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
. We extend this map to the origin by setting
\Phi(0)=0
.

We claim that
\Phi\colon U\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
is a local
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism near the origin. It is clear that
\Phi
is smooth away from the origin. Now let us consider the differentiability of
\Phi
at the origin. Since each
\Phi_{\alpha}
satisfies
\Phi_{\alpha}(0)=0
and
\Phi^{\prime}_{\alpha}(0)=I
, we have the Taylor expansion:

\Phi_{\alpha}(x)=x+R_{\alpha}(x),

where the remainder term
R_{\alpha}(x)
satisfies

\lim_{x\to 0}\frac{\|R_{\alpha}(x)\|}{\|x\|}=0.

It follows that

(3.2)

\begin{split}\Phi(x)&=\sum_{\alpha}\rho_{\alpha}(\sigma)\left(x+R_{\alpha}(x)\right)\\
&=\Big(\sum_{\alpha}\rho_{\alpha}(\sigma)\Big)x+\sum_{\alpha}\rho_{\alpha}(\sigma)R_{\alpha}(x)\\
&=x+\sum_{\alpha}\rho_{\alpha}(\sigma)R_{\alpha}(x)\end{split}

where we have used
\sum_{\alpha}\rho_{\alpha}(\sigma)=1
for all
\sigma\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
. This implies that
\Phi
is differentiable at the origin with derivative
\Phi^{\prime}(0)=I
. A similar computation shows that in fact the derivative
\Phi^{\prime}
is continuous at the origin. Therefore
\Phi
is a local
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism. By construction,
\Phi
maps each surface
\Sigma_{i}
to its corresponding tangent plane
\Pi_{i}
at the origin.

The following theorem (Theorem
3.5
) is a key result that allows us to reduce the verification of the essential self-adjointness of
D_{B}
to certain standard model cases. We emphasize that Theorem
3.5
does not itself establish the essential self-adjointness of
D_{B}
; rather, it serves purely as a reduction principle. In particular, the hypotheses involving the comparison of dihedral angles are slightly more general than those that will be imposed in the actual essential self-adjointness result (Theorem
3.15
).

Theorem 3.5
.

Let
f\colon\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}\to M
be a spin polyhedral map between two polyhedral manifolds of dimension
3
. Let
D
be the Dirac operator associated with the bundle
E=S(T\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}\oplus TM)
. Let
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k}
be the unit inner normal vector of each codimension one face
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
, and let
\nu_{k}
be a smooth unit-length section of the bundle

on
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
. Let

be the local boundary condition
7
7

7

Note that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k}

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
, whereas

is not necessarily the unit inward normal vector to the corresponding face

of

. Thus the boundary condition

considered here is slightly more general than those in Definition
2.13
. This additional flexibility will be needed for our index theorem (Theorem
6.4
). Nevertheless, it is still straightforward to verify that the Dirac operator

is symmetric with respect to this more general boundary condition
B
.
on
E
given by

\mathscr{E}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k})c(\nu_{k})\sigma=-\sigma\textup{ on }\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}.

Assume that all dihedral angles of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
are less than
\pi
. If for each pair of adjacent codimension one faces

(3.3)

\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{i},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{j}\rangle\neq\langle\nu_{i},\nu_{j}\rangle\textup{ along each connected component of }\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{i}\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{j},

or

(3.4)

\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{i},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{j}\rangle=\langle\nu_{i},\nu_{j}\rangle\textup{ along each connected component of }\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{i}\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{j},

then for any point

of

in
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}

\mathcal{C}_{x}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}

from the trivial spinor bundle

over

to the spinor bundle

over

, satisfying the following properties:

(1)

\Psi
maps the constant boundary condition determined by the
constant vector fields

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{i}\equiv\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{i}(x)\textup{ and }\omega_{i}\equiv\nu_{i}(x)

on the codimension-one faces
U\cap\Pi_{i}
of
U
(see line (
3.5
)), where
\Pi_{i}=T_{x}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{i}
is the tangent plane of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{i}
at
x
, to the boundary condition along codimension one faces

of

.

(2)

The operator
\Psi^{*}D\Psi
on

takes the form

\Psi^{*}D\Psi=D^{\mathrm{dR}}+\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{B},

where
D^{\mathrm{dR}}
is the standard de Rham on the Euclidean tangent cone
\mathcal{C}_{x}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
,
\mathcal{A}
is a first-order differential operator whose coefficients are continuous and vanish at the origin, and
\mathcal{B}
is a zeroth-order operator given by a uniformly bounded matrix-valued function over
U
. Consequently, for any
\delta>0
, the neighborhood

can be chosen sufficiently small such that the coefficients of
\mathcal{A}
are bounded by

in the supremum norm.

Proof.

The case where
x
lies in the interior of a codimension one face is classical and elementary. We shall focus the proof on the case where
x
lies in the interior of a codimension two face (i.e. an edge) or
x
lies in a codimension three face (i.e. a vertex).

Codimension two case.
In this case,
x
lies in the interior of an edge, say, the intersection of codimension one faces
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{1}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{2}
. Recall that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
is contained in an open manifold
X
. Let
\exp_{x}\colon T_{x}X\to X
be the exponential map at
x
, which a diffeomorphism from a small neighborhood
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}\subset T_{x}X
of the origin onto a neighborhood
\mathcal{W}\subset X
of
x
. The hypersurfaces
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{1}\cap\mathcal{W}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{2}\cap\mathcal{W}
pull back to two smooth hypersurfaces
\Sigma_{1},\Sigma_{2}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}
. Let
\widetilde{E}=\exp_{x}^{*}(E)
be the pullback bundle over
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}
. We identify
\widetilde{E}
with the trivial bundle
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}\times E_{x}
via parallel transportation along radial geodesics of
\mathcal{W}
. Note that, under this identification, the unit inner normal vector
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{i}(x)
of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{i}
at
x
coincides with the unit inner normal vector of
\Sigma_{i}
. Let
\Pi_{i}
be the tangent plane of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{i}
at
x
. The tangent cone
\mathcal{C}_{x}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
at
x
is the polyhedral cone in
T_{x}X
enclosed by
\Pi_{1}
and
\Pi_{2}
. Again, under the above identification, the unit inner normal vector of
\Pi_{i}
coincides with
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{i}(x)
.

Let
\Phi\colon\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
be the local diffeomorphism constructed in Example
3.3
. This map satisfies:

•

\Phi(0)=0
and
\Phi^{\prime}(0)=I
.

•

\Phi
maps
\Sigma_{i}
to
\Pi_{i}
.

By choosing a smaller neighborhood of the origin if necessary, we may assume without loss of generality that
\Phi
is a diffeomorphism
\Phi\colon\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}\to\mathcal{U}
, where
\mathcal{U}=\Phi(\widetilde{\mathcal{W}})
. Define
\Lambda\coloneqq\exp_{x}\circ\Phi^{-1}\colon\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{W}
. By construction,
\Lambda
is a diffeomorphism such that
\Lambda(0)=x
and
\Lambda^{\prime}(0)=I
, and maps
\Pi_{i}\cap\mathcal{U}
to
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{i}\cap\mathcal{W}
. Moreover, we have the following identification of bundles.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}\times E_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}\times E_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E|_{\mathcal{W}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{U}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{-1}\times\operatorname{id}_{E_{x}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathcal{U}}}
Parallel Transport
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\mathcal{W}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exp_{x}}

We denote this bundle identification
by
\mathcal{J}
.
Now set
U=\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{C}_{x}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
and
W=\mathcal{W}\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
. Recall that, at the codimension one face
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{j}
, the boundary condition for a section
\sigma
of
E
is defined by the operator
B_{j}=\mathscr{E}\bar{c}(\bar{\nu}_{j})c(\nu_{j})
:

\mathscr{E}\bar{c}(\bar{\nu}_{j})c(\nu_{j})\sigma=-\sigma.

we define a
constant boundary condition
on the codimension one faces
\Pi_{1}
and
\Pi_{2}
of
\mathcal{C}_{x}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
by

(3.5)

\mathscr{E}\bar{c}(\bar{\omega}_{i})c(\omega_{i})\sigma=-\sigma.

where
\bar{\omega}_{i}\equiv\bar{\nu}_{i}(x)
and
\omega_{i}\equiv\nu_{i}(x)
are constant vector fields along
\Pi_{i}
.

With the above bundle identification,
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{i}
and
\nu_{i}
become unit-length vector fields
8
8

8

Although
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{i}
coincides with the unit inner normal vector of
\Pi_{i}
at the origin of
U
, but
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{i}
is not necessarily the unit inner normal vector of
\Pi_{i}
at other points of
\Pi_{i}
.
of the trivial vector bundle
\mathbb{R}^{3}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{3}
along
\Pi_{i}
. For each
y\in\Pi_{i}\cap U
, let
V_{i}(y)\subset S(\mathbb{R}^{3}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{3})
be the
(-1)
-eigenspace of the operator

B_{i}=\mathscr{E}\bar{c}(\bar{\nu}_{i})c(\nu_{i})\colon S(\mathbb{R}^{3}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{3})\to S(\mathbb{R}^{3}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{3}).

Note that
\bar{c}(\bar{\nu}_{i})B_{i}=-B_{i}\bar{c}(\bar{\nu}_{i})
, which forces

to be of dimension
8/2=4
. In other words, the above bundle identification pulls back the boundary conditions for codimension one faces
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{i}\cap W
of

of

.

This pull-back boundary condition
B_{i}
coincides with the constant boundary condition for
\Pi_{i}\cap U
at
x
, but it generally does not coincide with the constant boundary condition at nearby points

. To remedy this, we shall construct a bundle isomorphism that maps the boundary conditions for
U
to the boundary conditions for
W
.

The bundle isomorphism
\Theta\colon U\times S(\mathbb{R}^{3}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{3})\to U\times S(\mathbb{R}^{3}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{3})
is constructed as follows. Importantly, because the subbundles
V_{i}
are preserved by the grading operator
\mathscr{E}
(Lemma
3.6
and
3.7
), the following construction can and should be carried out individually on each subspace
S^{\pm}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{3})
, where
S^{\pm}(\mathbb{R}^{3}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{3})
is the
(\pm 1)
-eigenspace of the grading operator
\mathscr{E}
. This ensures that the resulting isomorphism
\Theta
commutes with
\mathscr{E}
, hence preserves the grading on
S(\mathbb{R}^{3}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{3})
. For notational simplicity, we shall omit the superscript
\pm
in the following construction.

(1)

If
\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{1},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{2}\rangle\neq\langle\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\rangle
along
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{1}\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{2}
, then it follows from Lemma
3.7
that the intersection
V_{1}(y)\cap V_{2}(y)=\{0\}
for all
y\in\Pi_{1}\cap\Pi_{2}\cap U
. Let
p_{i}(y)
be the orthogonal projection
S(\mathbb{R}^{3}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{3})\to V_{i}(y)
. Since
V_{1}(y)\cap V_{2}(y)=\{0\}
and
\dim V_{1}(y)=\dim V_{2}(y)=\frac{1}{2}\dim S(\mathbb{R}^{3}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{3})=4
, it follows that
p_{1}(y)+p_{2}(y)
is invertible. As of now,
p_{i}
is a projection-valued function only defined on
\Pi_{i}\cap U
. We extend it smoothly to a projection-valued function on
U
, for example, consider an extension
9
9

9

Such an extension of, say
p_{1}
, is only used to help us define the bundle isomorphism on
U
, and is
not
meant as introducing an auxiliary boundary condition on the other codimension one face
\Pi_{2}
.
that is constant along the normal direction of
\Pi_{i}
. Since
\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{1},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{2}\rangle\neq\langle\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\rangle
is an open condition, by choosing a smaller neighborhood of the origin of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
if necessary, we define
\Theta
by setting

\Theta(z)=\left(p_{1}(0)p_{1}(z)+p_{2}(0)p_{2}(z)\right)\left(p_{1}(z)+p_{2}(z)\right)^{-1}

for all
z\in U
. By construction,
\Theta
maps
V_{i}(y)
to
V_{i}(0)
for each
y\in\Pi_{i}\cap U
in a small neighborhood of the origin.

(2)

The case where
\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{1},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{2}\rangle=\langle\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\rangle
is similar, only slightly more involved. In this case, it follows from Lemma
3.7
that the intersection
\dim(V_{1}(y)\cap V_{2}(y))=2
for all
y\in\Pi_{1}\cap\Pi_{2}\cap U
. Without loss of generality, assume that

\displaystyle\Pi_{1}

\displaystyle=\{(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\mid z_{3}=0\},

\displaystyle\Pi_{2}

\displaystyle=\{(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\mid z_{2}\sin\alpha-z_{3}\cos\alpha=0\},

where
\alpha\in(0,\pi)
is the dihedral angle between
\Pi_{1}
and
\Pi_{2}
.

Define the linear coordinates
(u,v)
on the
(z_{2},z_{3})
-plane by:

(3.6)

u(z_{2},z_{3})=z_{3},\qquad v(z_{2},z_{3})=z_{2}\sin\alpha-z_{3}\cos\alpha.

Under this transformation,
\Pi_{1}
corresponds exactly to
u=0
, and
\Pi_{2}
corresponds exactly to
v=0
.

Let
\{e_{i}(z_{1},0,0)\}_{i=1,2}
be a smooth orthonormal basis for the intersection
V_{1}\cap V_{2}
along the edge
\Pi_{1}\cap\Pi_{2}\cap U
. Because
V_{1}
is a smooth bundle over
\Pi_{1}
, we can smoothly extend
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
to orthonormal sections
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
of
V_{1}\subset S(\mathbb{R}^{3}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{3})
over
\Pi_{1}
, and then extend
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
to smooth sections of
S(\mathbb{R}^{3}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{3})
over
U
by extending them constantly along the lines where
v
and
z_{1}
are constant and
u
varies. We still denote these extended sections over
U
by
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
.

Similarly, we can smoothly extend
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
to orthonormal sections
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
of
V_{2}\subset S(\mathbb{R}^{3}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{3})
over
\Pi_{2}
, and then extend
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
to smooth sections of
S(\mathbb{R}^{3}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{3})
over
U
by extending them constantly along the lines where
u
and
z_{1}
are constant and
v
varies. We still denote these extended sections over
U
by
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
.

We define a section
\tilde{e}_{i}
of

over

by

(3.7)

\tilde{e}_{i}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})=\varphi_{i}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})+\psi_{i}(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})-e_{i}(z_{1},0,0).

By construction, we have

\tilde{e}_{i}(z_{1},0,0)=e_{i}(z_{1},0,0)

and
\tilde{e}_{i}|_{\Pi_{1}}\in V_{1}
and
\tilde{e}_{i}|_{\Pi_{2}}\in V_{2}
.

Now let
\tilde{w}_{1}
and
\tilde{w}_{2}
be an orthonormal basis of the orthogonal complement of
\{\tilde{e}_{1},\tilde{e}_{2}\}
in
V_{1}
along
\Pi_{1}
. We extend them to smooth sections of
S(\mathbb{R}^{3}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{3})
over
U
by extending them constantly along the lines where
v
and
z_{1}
are constant and
u
varies. We still denote these extended sections over
U
by
\tilde{w}_{1}
and
\tilde{w}_{2}
.

Now let
\tilde{w}^{\prime}_{1}
and
\tilde{w}^{\prime}_{2}
be an orthonormal basis of the orthogonal complement of
\{\tilde{e}_{1},\tilde{e}_{2}\}
in
V_{2}
along
\Pi_{2}
. We extend them to smooth sections of

over
U
by extending them constantly along the lines where

and

are constant and

varies. We still denote these extended sections over

by
\tilde{w}^{\prime}_{1}
and
\tilde{w}^{\prime}_{2}
.

Let
\widetilde{V}_{1}(z)
be the linear span of
\{\tilde{e}_{1}(z),\tilde{e}_{2}(z),\tilde{w}_{1}(z),\tilde{w}_{2}(z)\}
and
\widetilde{V}_{2}(z)
be the linear span of
\{\tilde{e}_{1}(z),\tilde{e}_{2}(z),\tilde{w}^{\prime}_{1}(z),\tilde{w}^{\prime}_{2}(z)\}
.
Since
\tilde{e}_{1}(z),\tilde{e}_{2}(z),\tilde{w}_{1}(z)
and
\tilde{w}_{2}(z)
are linearly independent at
z=(z_{1},0,0)
, by choosing a smaller neighborhood of the origin if necessary, we may assume without loss of generality that
\tilde{e}_{1}(z),\tilde{e}_{2}(z),\tilde{w}_{1}(z)
and
\tilde{w}_{2}(z)
are linearly independent at every
z=(z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})\in U
. By the same reasoning, we may assume without loss of generality that
\tilde{e}_{1}(z),\tilde{e}_{2}(z),\tilde{w}^{\prime}_{1}(z)
and
\tilde{w}^{\prime}_{2}(z)
are linearly independent at every

, and
\dim\widetilde{V}_{1}(z)\cap\widetilde{V}_{2}(z)=2
at every

.

Let
p_{1}(z)
be the projection
S(\mathbb{R}^{3}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{3})\to\widetilde{V}_{1}(z)
and
p_{2}(z)
be the projection
S(\mathbb{R}^{3}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{3})\to\widetilde{V}_{2}(z)
. Let
q_{3}(z)
be the projection
S(\mathbb{R}^{3}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{3})\to\widetilde{V}_{1}(z)\cap\widetilde{V}_{2}(z)
. Define
q_{1}(z)=p_{1}(z)-q_{3}(z)
,
q_{2}(z)=p_{2}(z)-q_{3}(z)
, and
q_{4}(z)
as the projection onto
(V_{1}(z)+V_{2}(z))^{\perp}
. We define the bundle isomorphism
\Theta
by

\Theta(z)=\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{4}q_{j}(0)q_{j}(z)\Big)\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{4}q_{j}(z)\Big)^{-1}.

By construction,
\Theta
maps
V_{i}(y)
to
V_{i}(0)
for each
y\in\Pi_{i}\cap U
.

We emphasize again that the construction of
\Theta
should be interpreted as being carried out on each subspace

independently (cf. Lemmas
3.6
and
3.7
). In particular,
\Theta
commutes with the grading operator
\mathscr{E}
by construction. We define
\Psi\coloneqq\mathcal{J}\circ\Theta^{-1}
. Recall that
\mathcal{J}
is the bundle identification obtained from
\Phi^{-1}
,
\exp_{x}
, and parallel
transport. By construction,
\Psi
maps sections satisfying the constant boundary conditions on
U
to sections satisfying the boundary conditions on
W
.

Let us consider the difference
\Psi^{*}D\Psi-D^{\mathrm{dR}}
. Let
\{f_{j}=\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{j}}\}_{1\leq j\leq 3}
be the standard orthonormal basis of
TU=\mathbb{R}^{3}
. We choose a local orthonormal frame
\{e_{j}\}
for
TW
such that
e_{j}(x)=d\Lambda_{0}(f_{j})
, where
d\Lambda_{0}
is the differential map of
\Lambda
at the origin
0\in U
.

Let
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}
denote the Riemannian metric on
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
. The Riemannian volume form on
W
pulls back to a volume form on
U
given by

\sqrt{\det(\Lambda^{*}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g})_{y}}\,dy_{1}\wedge dy_{2}\wedge dy_{3},

where the entries of the matrix
(\Lambda^{\ast}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g})_{y}
are defined as

((\Lambda^{\ast}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g})_{y})_{ij}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}_{\Lambda(y)}(d\Lambda_{y}(f_{i}),d\Lambda_{y}(f_{j}))

for each
y\in U
.

The difference
\Psi^{*}D\Psi-D^{\mathrm{dR}}
can be written as

\Psi^{*}D\Psi-D^{\mathrm{dR}}=\sum_{k=1}^{3}A_{k}(y)\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{k}}+\mathcal{B}(y),

where
A_{k}
and
\mathcal{B}
are matrix-valued smooth functions on
U
.
The coefficients
A_{k}(y)
are given by

A_{k}(y)=-\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(f_{k})+\sqrt{\det(\Lambda^{*}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g})_{y}}\sum_{j=1}^{3}a_{jk}(y)\,(\Theta^{-1})^{*}_{y}\circ\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(e_{j}(\Lambda(y)))\circ\Theta^{-1}_{y},

where
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(e_{j}(\Lambda(y)))
is the Clifford multiplication by the vector
e_{j}(\Lambda(y))
and the continuous functions
a_{jk}(y)
are the coefficients of
d\Lambda^{-1}(e_{j})
in the standard basis
\{f_{j}=\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{j}}:1\leq j\leq 3\}
:

d\Lambda^{-1}(e_{j})=\sum_{k=1}^{3}a_{jk}(y)\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{k}}.

At the origin
y=0
, we have
\Lambda(0)=x
,
d\Lambda_{0}=I
, and
\Theta_{0}=I
. It follows that
\sqrt{\det(\Lambda^{*}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g})_{0}}=1
. Furthermore, since
e_{j}(x)=d\Lambda_{0}(f_{j})
, we have
a_{jk}(0)=\delta_{jk}
. Therefore, we have
