A_{k}(0)=-\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(f_{k})+\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(f_{k})=0

It follows that for any
\delta>0
, there exists a sufficiently small neighborhood where
\|A_{k}(y)\|<\delta
.

Codimension three case.
In this case,
x
lies at a vertex, the intersection of codimension one faces
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{1},\dots,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{n}
. Recall that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
is a subspace inside some open manifold
X
. Let
\exp_{x}\colon T_{x}X\to X
be the exponential map at
x
, which is a diffeomorphism from a small neighborhood
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}\subset T_{x}X
of the origin onto a neighborhood
\mathcal{W}\subset X
of
x
. The hypersurfaces
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{1}\cap\mathcal{W},\dots,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{n}\cap\mathcal{W}
pull back to smooth surfaces
\Sigma_{1},\dots,\Sigma_{n}\subset\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}
.

Let
\widetilde{E}=\exp_{x}^{*}(E)
be the pullback bundle over
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}
. We identify
\widetilde{E}
with the trivial bundle
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}\times E_{x}
via parallel transportation along radial geodesics of
\mathcal{W}
. Note that, under this identification, the unit inner normal vector
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{i}(x)
of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{i}
at
x
coincides with the unit inner normal vector of
\Sigma_{i}
. Let
\Pi_{i}
be the tangent plane of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{i}
at
x
. The tangent cone
\mathcal{C}_{x}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
at
x
is the polyhedral cone in
T_{x}X
enclosed by
\Pi_{1},\dots,\Pi_{n}
. Again, under the above identification, the unit inner normal vector of
\Pi_{i}
coincides with
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{i}
.

Let
\Phi\colon\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}^{3}
be the local
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism constructed in Example
3.4
. This map satisfies:

•

\Phi(0)=0
and
\Phi^{\prime}(0)=I
.

•

\Phi
maps
\Sigma_{i}
to
\Pi_{i}
.

By choosing a smaller neighborhood of the origin if necessary, we may assume without loss of generality that
\Phi
is a
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism
\Phi\colon\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}\to\mathcal{U}
, where
\mathcal{U}=\Phi(\widetilde{\mathcal{W}})
. Define
\Gamma\coloneqq\exp_{x}\circ\Phi^{-1}\colon\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{W}
. By construction, we have
\Gamma
is a
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism such that
\Gamma(0)=x
and
\Gamma^{\prime}(0)=I
, and maps
\Pi_{i}\cap\mathcal{U}
to
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{i}\cap\mathcal{W}
. Similar to the codimension two case, let
\mathcal{J}\colon\mathcal{U}\times E_{x}\to E|_{\mathcal{W}}
be the the bundle identification obtained from
\Phi^{-1}
,
\exp_{x}
, and parallel
transport.

Now set
U=\mathcal{U}\cap\mathcal{C}_{x}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
and
W=\mathcal{W}\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
.
We consider a
constant boundary condition
on the codimension one faces
\Pi_{i}
of
\mathcal{C}_{x}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
by

(3.8)

\mathscr{E}\bar{c}(\bar{\omega}_{i})c(\omega_{i})\sigma=-\sigma.

where
\bar{\omega}_{i}\equiv\bar{\nu}_{i}(x)
and
\omega_{i}\equiv\nu_{i}(x)
are constant vector fields along
\Pi_{i}
.

With the above bundle identification,
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{i}
and
\nu_{i}
become unit-length vector fields of the trivial vector bundle
\mathbb{R}^{3}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{3}
along
\Pi_{j}
. For each
y\in\Pi_{i}
, let
V_{i}(y)\subset S(\mathbb{R}^{3}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{3})
be the
(-1)
-eigenspace of the operator

B_{i}=\mathscr{E}\bar{c}(\bar{\nu}_{i})c(\nu_{i})\colon S(\mathbb{R}^{3}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{3})\to S(\mathbb{R}^{3}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{3}).

In other words, the bundle identification pulls back the boundary conditions for codimension one faces
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{i}\cap W
of
W
to boundary conditions for codimension one faces
\Pi_{i}\cap U
of
U
.

This pull-back boundary condition
B_{i}
coincides with the constant boundary condition for
\Pi_{i}\cap U
at
x
, but it generally does not coincide with the constant boundary condition at nearby points
y\in\Pi_{i}\cap U
. To remedy this, we construct a bundle isomorphism
\Theta\colon U\times S(\mathbb{R}^{3}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{3})\to U\times S(\mathbb{R}^{3}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{3})
using a partition of unity approach analogous to the construction of
\Phi
.

Let
\Theta_{ij}
be the bundle isomorphism near the origin constructed in the codimension two case for the intersection of faces
\Pi_{i}
and
\Pi_{j}
. Using the same open cover
\{W_{\alpha}\}
of
\mathbb{S}^{2}
and partition of unity
\{\rho_{\alpha}\}
as in the construction of the map
\Phi
of Example
3.4
, we select local bundle isomorphisms
\Theta_{\alpha}
as follows:

(1)

If
W_{\alpha}
contains a vertex of
\mathbb{L}
formed by
\Pi_{i}\cap\Pi_{j}\cap\mathbb{S}^{2}
, we set
\Theta_{\alpha}=\Theta_{ij}
.

(2)

If
W_{\alpha}
intersects an edge
\Pi_{i}\cap\mathbb{S}^{2}
but no vertices, its endpoints correspond to intersections
\Pi_{i}\cap\Pi_{j}
and
\Pi_{i}\cap\Pi_{k}
. Both
\Theta_{ij}
and
\Theta_{ik}
map the constant boundary condition on
\Pi_{i}
to the boundary condition pulled back from
\Sigma_{i}
; thus, we set
\Theta_{\alpha}
to be either one.

(3)

If
W_{\alpha}
is disjoint from the edges and vertices of
\mathbb{L}
, we set
\Theta_{\alpha}
to be the identity map.

We define the global bundle isomorphism
\Theta
on
U
by:

(3.9)

\Theta(z)=\sum_{\alpha}\rho_{\alpha}(\sigma)\Theta_{\alpha}(z),

where
z\in U\setminus\{0\}
is expressed in polar coordinates as
z=r\sigma
. Because
\Theta_{\alpha}(0)=I
for all
\alpha
, it is clear that
\Theta(0)=I
. By choosing a smaller neighborhood of the origin if necessary,
\Theta(z)
remains invertible on
U
, thus defining a bundle isomorphism. Clearly, by construction,
\Theta
is smooth on
U\setminus\{0\}
. A computation similar to the line (
3.2
) shows that
\Theta
is Lipschitz on
U
.

We define
\Psi\coloneqq\mathcal{J}\circ\Theta^{-1}
, where
\mathcal{J}\colon U\times E_{x}\to E|_{W}
is the bundle identification obtained from
\Phi^{-1}
,
\exp_{x}
, and parallel
transport. By construction,
\Psi
maps sections satisfying the constant boundary conditions on
U
to sections satisfying the boundary conditions on
W
.

The difference
\Psi^{*}D\Psi-D^{\mathrm{dR}}
can be written as

\Psi^{*}D\Psi-D^{\mathrm{dR}}=\sum_{k=1}^{3}A_{k}(y)\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{k}}+\mathcal{B}(y),

where
A_{k}
are matrix-valued continuous functions and
\mathcal{B}
is a matrix-valued
uniformly bounded
10
10

10

\mathcal{B}
is continuous on
U\setminus\{0\}
, but not necessarily continuous at the origin. This is because
\mathcal{B}
involves the derivative of
\Theta
, where
\Theta
is Lipschitz but its derivative is generally not continuous.
function on
U
. By the same argument for the coefficients
A_{k}(y)
used in the
codimension two case
, we have
A_{k}(0)=0.

In particular, it follows that for any
\delta>0
, there exists a sufficiently small neighborhood where
\|A_{k}(y)\|<\delta
. ∎

Lemma 3.6
.

With the same notation as Theorem
3.5
, at a point
y
in a codimension one face
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
, let
V=V(y)
be
(-1)
-eigenspace of the operator

B=\mathscr{E}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k})c(\nu_{k})\colon E_{y}\to E_{y}.

We denote by
E_{y}^{\pm}
the
(\pm 1)
-eigenspace of the grading operator
\mathscr{E}
, then

\dim(V\cap E^{+}_{y})=\dim(V\cap E_{y}^{-})=\frac{1}{2}\dim V.

Proof.

Note that
B
commutes with
\mathscr{E}
, that is
B\mathscr{E}=\mathscr{E}B
. It follows that
V
has an orthogonal decomposition
V=V^{+}\oplus V^{-}
, where
V^{\pm}=V\cap E^{\pm}_{y}
.
Let
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e}\in T_{y}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
be a unit vector that is orthogonal to
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k}
. Since
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e})
anticommutes with
\mathscr{E}
and commutes with
B
, it follows that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e})
maps
V^{\pm}
isomorphically to
V^{\mp}
. Consequently,
\dim V^{+}=\dim V^{-}=\frac{1}{2}\dim V
.
∎

Lemma 3.7
.

With the same notation as Theorem
3.5
, at a point
y\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{1}\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{2}
, let
V_{1}=V_{1}(y)
and
V_{2}=V_{2}(y)
be the
(-1)
-eigenspace of the operator

B_{j}=\mathscr{E}\bar{c}(\bar{\nu}_{j})c(\nu_{j})\colon E_{y}\to E_{y}.

Then the dimension of
V_{1}\cap V_{2}
satisfies the following:

(a)

if
\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{1},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{2}\rangle=\langle\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\rangle
, then
\dim(V_{1}\cap V_{2})=2
;

(b)

if
\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{1},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{2}\rangle\neq\langle\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\rangle
, then
\dim(V_{1}\cap V_{2})=0
.

Moreover, we have

\dim(V_{1}\cap V_{2}\cap E^{+}_{y})=\dim(V_{1}\cap V_{2}\cap E^{-}_{y})=\frac{1}{2}\dim(V_{1}\cap V_{2}).

Proof.

The operators
B_{j}
are involutions (i.e.,
B_{j}^{2}=1
) on
E_{y}
. A spinor
\sigma\in V_{1}\cap V_{2}
must satisfy
B_{1}\sigma=-\sigma
and
B_{2}\sigma=-\sigma
. Consequently,
\sigma
must be an eigenvector of the product operator
A=B_{1}B_{2}
with eigenvalue
1
.

Consider the orthogonal decomposition
T_{y}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}=\mathfrak{V}\oplus\mathfrak{V}^{\perp}
, where
\mathfrak{V}=\operatorname{span}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{1},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{2})
is the linear subspace spanned by
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{1}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{2}
. Note that
\mathfrak{V}
is two dimensional, since
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{1}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{2}
are linearly independent by our geometric assumption on
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
. We also choose a two-dimensional subspace

\mathfrak{W}\subset(f^{*}TM)_{y}
containing
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
. Of course, if
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
are linearly
independent, then
\mathfrak{W}=\operatorname{span}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2}).
Similarly, decompose
(f^{*}TM)_{y}=\mathfrak{W}\oplus\mathfrak{W}^{\perp}
. So we have
E_{y}=S(\mathfrak{V})\otimes S(\mathfrak{W})\otimes S(\mathfrak{V}^{\perp}\oplus\mathfrak{W}^{\perp})
. The operators
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
act as the identity on the factor
S(\mathfrak{V}^{\perp}\oplus\mathfrak{W}^{\perp})
; therefore, it suffices to analyze their action on the factor
S(\mathfrak{V})\otimes S(\mathfrak{W})
.

A direct computation shows that

A=B_{1}B_{2}=\overline{c}(\overline{\nu}_{1})\overline{c}(\overline{\nu}_{2})\,c(\nu_{1})c(\nu_{2}).

Let
\alpha\in(0,\pi)
be the angle such that
\langle\overline{\nu}_{1},\overline{\nu}_{2}\rangle=\cos\alpha
. The operator
L\coloneqq\overline{c}(\overline{\nu}_{1})\overline{c}(\overline{\nu}_{2})
acts on
S(\mathfrak{V})
with eigenvalues
\{-e^{i\alpha},-e^{-i\alpha}\}
and acts by identity on
S(\mathfrak{W})
.
Similarly, let
\beta\in[0,\pi]
be the angle such that
\langle\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\rangle=\cos\beta
. The operator
R\coloneqq c(\nu_{1})c(\nu_{2})
acts on
S(\mathfrak{W})
with eigenvalues
\{-e^{i\beta},-e^{-i\beta}\}
and acts by identity on
S(\mathfrak{V})
.

It follows that the eigenvalues of
A\colon S(\mathfrak{V})\otimes S(\mathfrak{W})\to S(\mathfrak{V})\otimes S(\mathfrak{W})
are

\{e^{i(\alpha+\beta)},e^{-i(\alpha+\beta)},e^{i(\alpha-\beta)},e^{-i(\alpha-\beta)}\}.

Now, consider the two cases:

(a)

If
\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{1},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{2}\rangle=\langle\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\rangle
, then
\alpha=\beta
. Then
1
occurs as an eigenvalue of
A
with multiplicity
2
. Let
Q
denote
this two-dimensional eigenspace. Let
u_{+},u_{-}
be eigenvectors of

L=\bar{c}(\bar{\nu}_{1})\bar{c}(\bar{\nu}_{2})
with eigenvalues

-e^{i\alpha},-e^{-i\alpha}
, respectively; and

w_{+},w_{-}
eigenvectors of
R=c(\nu_{1})c(\nu_{2})
with eigenvalues

-e^{i\alpha},-e^{-i\alpha}
, respectively. Then

Q=\operatorname{span}\{u_{+}\otimes w_{-},\,u_{-}\otimes w_{+}\}.

It remains to impose the condition

B_{1}\sigma=-\sigma
.
A direct computation shows that
AB_{1}=B_{1}A^{-1}
, which implies that
B_{1}
maps
Q
to
Q
.
Moreover, we have

B_{1}LB_{1}=L^{-1}\textup{ and }B_{1}RB_{1}=R^{-1}.

This implies that
B_{1}(Q_{+})=Q_{-}
and
B_{1}(Q_{-})=Q_{+}
, where
Q_{+}=\mathbb{C}(u_{+}\otimes w_{-})
and
Q_{-}=\mathbb{C}(u_{-}\otimes w_{+})
. Therefore, with respect to this basis,

B_{1}|_{Q}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\lambda\\
\lambda^{-1}&0\end{pmatrix}

for some
\lambda\neq 0
. It follows that the subspace

J\coloneqq\{\sigma\in Q\mid B_{1}\sigma=-\sigma\}

has dimension
1
.
The intersection
V_{1}\cap V_{2}
is then given by

V_{1}\cap V_{2}=J\otimes S(\mathfrak{V}^{\perp}\oplus\mathfrak{W}^{\perp}).

Since
\dim(J)=1
and
\dim(S(\mathfrak{V}^{\perp}\oplus\mathfrak{W}^{\perp}))=2
, we have
\dim(V_{1}\cap V_{2})=2
.

(b)

If
\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{1},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{2}\rangle\neq\langle\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\rangle
, then
\alpha\neq\beta
. Consequently,
1
is not an eigenvalue of
A
. Thus
V_{1}\cap V_{2}=0
.

To prove the final statement, let
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e}\in T_{y}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
be a unit vector that is orthogonal to both
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{1}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{2}
. Since
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e})
anticommutes with
\mathscr{E}
and commutes with
B_{j}
, it follows that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e})
preserves the subspace
V_{1}\cap V_{2}
. Moreover,
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e})
maps
V_{1}\cap V_{2}\cap E^{\pm}_{y}
isomorphically to
V_{1}\cap V_{2}\cap E^{\mp}_{y}
. Consequently,

\dim(V_{1}\cap V_{2}\cap E^{+}_{y})=\dim(V_{1}\cap V_{2}\cap E^{-}_{y})=\frac{1}{2}\dim(V_{1}\cap V_{2}).

This finishes the proof.
∎

3.2.
Coordinate change and conical operators

Theorem
3.5
allows us to reduce the verification of the essential self-adjointness of
D_{B}
to the following standard model cases. Let
f\colon\mathbb{F}\to\mathbb{F}^{\prime}
be a polyhedral map between two polyhedral cones in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
that are bounded by hyperplanes passing through the origin. Since these cones and their faces are flat, their tangent bundles are trivial, the normal vectors to the codimension-one faces are constant vector fields, canonically identified with vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
. In this setting, the precise details of the map
f
become irrelevant; its only role is to prescribe the correspondence between the faces of
\mathbb{F}
and the faces of
\mathbb{F}^{\prime}
.

Under the trivializations of
T\mathbb{F}
and
f^{*}T\mathbb{F}^{\prime}
, the spinor bundle
S(T\mathbb{F}\oplus f^{\ast}T\mathbb{F}^{\prime})
is naturally identified with
\mathord{\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{$\bigwedge$}}}}}}}^{*}\mathbb{F}\coloneqq\mathord{\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{$\bigwedge$}}}}}}}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{k}
equipped with the trivial connection. Under this identification, the Clifford actions
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}
and
c
are identified with the usual Clifford actions on forms given by

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(v)=v\wedge-\iota_{v,L},\textup{ and }c(v)=\wedge v-\iota_{v,R},

where
\iota_{v,L}
and
\iota_{v,R}
denote the corresponding left and right contraction by
v
. See
[
21
, I.3.9]
. With these identifications, the twisted Dirac operator
D
on
S(T\mathbb{F}\oplus f^{\ast}T\mathbb{F}^{\prime})
coincides with the standard de Rham operator
D^{\mathrm{dR}}=d+d^{*}
acting on
\mathord{\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{$\bigwedge$}}}}}}}^{*}\mathbb{R}^{k}
. The boundary condition
B
is determined simply by the inner normal vectors of
\mathbb{F}
and
\mathbb{F}^{\prime}
. Since the faces are flat, these normal vectors are constant along each codimension-one face of
\mathbb{F}
. Consequently, the boundary condition
B
is constant along each codimension one face.

The Euclidean metric on
\mathbb{F}
can be written in polar coordinates as

g^{\mathbb{F}}=dr^{2}+r^{2}g^{\mathbb{L}},

where
r
is the radial distance from the origin and
\mathbb{L}=\mathbb{F}\cap\mathbb{S}^{k-1}
is the link of
\mathbb{F}
.

There is a natural unitary that identifies the de Rham operator
D^{\mathrm{dR}}
on
\mathbb{F}
with an elliptic operator on the cylinder
(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{L}
equipped with the cylindrical metric (i.e., product metric), see
[
23
, Section 5]
. Denote by
\Omega^{\ast}(\mathbb{F})
the space of differential forms on
\mathbb{F}
. The unitary
\Psi
is given by

(3.10)

\Psi=(\Psi_{\mathrm{even}},\Psi_{\mathrm{odd}})\colon C^{\infty}((0,\infty),\Omega^{*}\mathbb{L})\oplus C^{\infty}((0,\infty),\Omega^{*}\mathbb{L})\longrightarrow\Omega^{\ast}(\mathbb{F}),

where

(3.11)

\Psi_{\mathrm{even}}\colon C^{\infty}((0,\infty),\Omega^{*}\mathbb{L})\longrightarrow\Omega^{\mathrm{even}}\mathbb{F},~\omega_{p}\longmapsto\begin{cases}r^{p-\frac{k-1}{2}}\omega_{p},&\text{ if $p$ is even}\\
r^{p-\frac{k-1}{2}}\omega_{p}\wedge dr,&\text{ if $p$ is odd}\end{cases}

and

(3.12)

\Psi_{\mathrm{odd}}\colon C^{\infty}((0,\infty),\Omega^{*}\mathbb{L})\longrightarrow\Omega^{\mathrm{odd}}\mathbb{F},~\omega_{p}\longmapsto\begin{cases}r^{p-\frac{k-1}{2}}\omega_{p},&\text{ if $p$ is odd}\\
r^{p-\frac{k-1}{2}}\omega_{p}\wedge dr,&\text{ if $p$ is even}\end{cases}

The Clifford actions in the cylindrical coordinates are given by

(3.13)

\Psi^{-1}\mathscr{E}\Psi=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix},~\Psi^{-1}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\partial_{r})\Psi=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix},~\Psi^{-1}c(\partial_{r})\Psi=\begin{pmatrix}0&-\mathscr{E}_{\mathbb{L}}\\
\mathscr{E}_{\mathbb{L}}&0\end{pmatrix},

where
\mathscr{E}_{\mathbb{L}}
is the grading operator on
\Omega^{*}\mathbb{L}
, and

(3.14)

\Psi^{-1}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(v)\Psi=\begin{pmatrix}0&\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(v)\\
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(v)&0\end{pmatrix},~\Psi^{-1}c(v)\Psi=\begin{pmatrix}0&\mathscr{E}_{\mathbb{L}}c(v)\\
-\mathscr{E}_{\mathbb{L}}c(v)&0\end{pmatrix}

if
v
is orthogonal to
\partial_{r}
.

With respect to the even/odd grading of differential forms, we have

D^{\mathrm{dR}}=\begin{pmatrix}&D^{\mathrm{dR},-}\\
D^{\mathrm{dR},+}&\end{pmatrix},

where
D^{\mathrm{dR},-}\colon\Omega^{\mathrm{odd}}\mathbb{F}\to\Omega^{\mathrm{even}}\mathbb{F}
and

D^{\mathrm{dR},+}\colon\Omega^{\mathrm{even}}\mathbb{F}\to\Omega^{\mathrm{odd}}\mathbb{F}
. Let us define

(3.15)

P\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}c_{0}&d^{*}&&&\\
d&c_{1}&&&\\
&&\ddots&&\\
&&&c_{k-2}&d^{*}\\
&&&d&c_{k-1}\end{pmatrix}

where
d
is the de Rham differential on
\Omega^{\ast}\mathbb{L}
,
d^{\ast}
is the adjoint of
d
, and

c_{p}=(-1)^{p}\big(p-\frac{k-1}{2}\big).

The operator
P
above differs from the de Rham operator of
\mathbb{L}
by a bounded endomorphism. A direct computation shows that

(3.16)

\Psi_{\mathrm{odd}}^{-1}D^{\mathrm{dR},+}\Psi_{\mathrm{even}}=\frac{\partial}{\partial r}+\frac{1}{r}P\colon C^{\infty}((0,\infty),\Omega^{*}\mathbb{L})\to C^{\infty}((0,\infty),\Omega^{*}\mathbb{L}),

and

(3.17)

\Psi_{\mathrm{even}}^{-1}D^{\mathrm{dR},-}\Psi_{\mathrm{odd}}=-\frac{\partial}{\partial r}+\frac{1}{r}P\colon C^{\infty}((0,\infty),\Omega^{*}\mathbb{L})\to C^{\infty}((0,\infty),\Omega^{*}\mathbb{L}).

Equivalently,

(3.18)

\Psi^{*}D^{\mathrm{dR}}\Psi=\begin{pmatrix}0&-\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\\
\frac{\partial}{\partial r}&0\end{pmatrix}+\frac{1}{r}\begin{pmatrix}0&P\\
P&0\end{pmatrix}.

See
[
7
, Section 5]
and
[
23
, Proposition 5.3]
.

We now turn to the boundary condition
B
. On a codimension one face
F_{j}
of
\mathbb{F}
, the boundary condition
B
is defined by

\gamma_{j}\sigma=-\sigma,\quad\text{where}\quad\gamma_{j}\coloneqq\mathscr{E}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\nu_{j})c(\nu_{j}^{\prime}).

Here,
\nu_{j}
is the unit inner normal to
F_{j}
,
\nu_{j}^{\prime}
is the unit inner normal to the corresponding face
F_{j}^{\prime}
of
\mathbb{F}^{\prime}
, and
\mathscr{E}
is the even-odd grading operator on
\mathord{\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{$\bigwedge$}}}}}}}^{*}T\mathbb{F}
.

Note that the operator
\gamma_{j}
preserves the even-odd grading of differential forms, that is,
\gamma_{j}
commutes with
\mathscr{E}
. Under the unitary transformation
\Psi
, the boundary condition
B
induces a boundary condition
B_{\mathbb{L}}
on the bundle
(\mathord{\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{$\bigwedge$}}}}}}}^{*}T\mathbb{L})^{\oplus 2}
over the link
\mathbb{L}=\mathbb{F}\cap\mathbb{S}^{k-1}
. The link
\mathbb{L}
is itself a polyhedral manifold of dimension
k-1
. Observe that, for a codimension one face
F_{j}
of
\mathbb{F}
, it unit inner normal vector
\nu_{j}
is orthogonal to the radial vector
\partial_{r}
. Consequently, the operator
\gamma_{j}
commutes with
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\partial_{r})
. Together with the fact that
\gamma_{j}
commutes with
\mathscr{E}
, it follows from the matrix representation in (
3.13
) that the boundary condition
B
splits into two identical boundary conditions on the two copies of
\mathord{\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{$\bigwedge$}}}}}}}^{*}T\mathbb{L}
. We denote this induced boundary condition on the faces of the link by
B_{\mathbb{L}}
.

Although
\nu_{j}
is orthogonal to
\partial_{r}
,
\nu_{j}’
need not be orthogonal to
\partial_{r}
. Consequently, the induced boundary condition
B_{\mathbb{L}}
may not respect the even-odd grading
\mathscr{E}_{\mathbb{L}}
on
\mathord{\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{$\bigwedge$}}}}}}}^{*}T\mathbb{L}
.

Let us denote by
D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{B}
the de Rham operator of
\mathbb{F}
with initial domain consisting of smooth differential forms that satisfy the boundary condition
B
and are supported away from the vertex of
\mathbb{F}
(i.e., forms in
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{F}\setminus\{0\},\mathord{\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{$\bigwedge$}}}}}}}^{*}T\mathbb{F};B)
). Similarly, let
P_{B}
be the operator
P
on the link
\mathbb{L}
(see (
3.18
)) subject to the boundary condition
B_{\mathbb{L}}
.

The following lemma characterizes when
D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{B}
is essentially self-adjoint in terms of the spectrum of the operator
P_{B}
.

Lemma 3.8

(cf.
[
7
, Theorem 3.1]
)
.

Assume that
P_{B}
is essentially self-adjoint. Then
D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{B}
is essentially self-adjoint if and only if
|P_{B}|\geq 1/2
.

Proof.

Set
E_{\pm}(D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{B})\coloneqq\ker((D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{B})^{*}\mp i)
, where
(D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{B})^{*}
is the adjoint of
D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{B}
(as an unbounded operator). The von Neumann deficiency indices theorem states that
D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{B}
is essentially self-adjoint if and only if

E_{+}(D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{B})=E_{-}(D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{B})=0.

Since
\mathbb{L}
is compact and
P_{B}
is an essentially self-adjoint elliptic operator with local boundary condition, its closure has compact resolvent. Hence
L^{2}(\mathbb{L},\bigwedge^{*}T^{*}\mathbb{L})
admits an orthonormal basis
\{\phi_{\lambda}\}
, where each
\phi_{\lambda}
is the eigenvector of
P_{B}
with eigenvalue
\lambda
.

Suppose that
\varphi=\varphi_{\text{even}}\oplus\varphi_{odd}
lies in the kernel of
(D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{B})^{*}-i
. Let us denote
\psi_{0}=\Psi_{\mathrm{even}}^{-1}(\varphi_{\text{even}})
and

\psi_{1}=\Psi_{\mathrm{odd}}^{-1}(\varphi_{\text{odd}})
. We have

(3.19)

\Big(\begin{pmatrix}0&-\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\\
\frac{\partial}{\partial r}&0\end{pmatrix}+\frac{1}{r}\begin{pmatrix}0&P\\
P&0\end{pmatrix}-i\Big)\begin{pmatrix}\psi_{0}\\
\psi_{1}\end{pmatrix}=0.

If we write

\psi_{0}=\sum_{\lambda}\psi_{0,\lambda}(r)\phi_{\lambda}\textup{ and }\psi_{1}=\sum_{\lambda}\psi_{1,\lambda}(r)\phi_{\lambda},

then Equation (
3.19
) then splits into the following system of
ordinary differential equations for each eigenvector
\phi_{\lambda}
:

(3.20)

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\psi_{0,\lambda}+\frac{\lambda}{r}\psi_{0,\lambda}=i\psi_{1,\lambda},\\
\displaystyle-\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\psi_{1,\lambda}+\frac{\lambda}{r}\psi_{1,\lambda}=i\psi_{0,\lambda}.\end{cases}

It follows that

\begin{cases}\displaystyle-\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial r^{2}}\psi_{0,\lambda}+\frac{\lambda}{r^{2}}\psi_{0,\lambda}+\frac{\lambda^{2}}{r^{2}}\psi_{0,\lambda}+\psi_{0,\lambda}=0,\vskip 8.5359pt\\
\displaystyle i\psi_{1,\lambda}=\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\psi_{0,\lambda}+\frac{\lambda}{r}\psi_{0,\lambda},\end{cases}

where the solution to the first differential equation consists of modified Bessel functions. More precisely, we have

(3.21)

\begin{cases}\psi_{0,\lambda}=c_{1}\sqrt{r}\cdot K_{\lambda+1/2}(r)+c_{2}\sqrt{r}\cdot I_{\lambda+1/2}(r)\\
\psi_{1,\lambda}=ic_{1}\sqrt{r}\cdot K_{\lambda-1/2}(r)-ic_{2}\sqrt{r}\cdot I_{\lambda-1/2}(r),\end{cases}

where
I_{\nu}
and
K_{\nu}
are modified Bessel functions of the first and the second kind, respectively. Since
I_{\nu}
grows exponentially as
r\to\infty
and
K_{\nu}
decays exponentially as
r\to\infty
, in order for
\psi_{0,\lambda}\oplus\psi_{1,\lambda}
to be in
L^{2}
, we have

\begin{cases}\psi_{0,\lambda}=c_{1}\sqrt{r}\cdot K_{\lambda+1/2}(r)\phi_{\lambda}\\
\psi_{1,\lambda}=ic_{1}\sqrt{r}\cdot K_{\lambda-1/2}(r)\phi_{\lambda}.\end{cases}

Moreover, since

K_{\nu}(r)\sim\begin{cases}\ln r&\textup{ if }\nu=0,\\
r^{-|\nu|}&\textup{ if }\nu\neq 0,\end{cases}

it follows that
\psi_{0,\lambda}\oplus\psi_{1,\lambda}
is an
L^{2}
solution if and only if we have
-1/2<\lambda<1/2
(cf.
[
23
, Lemma 4.2]
). This shows that
E_{+}(D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{B})=0
if and only if
|P_{B}|\geq 1/2
. The same argument shows that
E_{-}(D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{B})=0
if and only if
|P_{B}|\geq 1/2
. This finishes the proof.
∎

3.3.
Essential self-adjointness of
D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{B}
for two dimensional model spaces

In this subsection, we investigate the essential self-adjointness of
D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{B}
on
\mathbb{F}
when
\dim\mathbb{F}=2
.

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
\mathbb{F}
and
\mathbb{F}^{\prime}
be two sectors in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with angles
\alpha
and
\beta
. Let
B
be the boundary condition on
\mathord{\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{$\bigwedge$}}}}}}}^{*}T\mathbb{F}=\mathord{\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{$\bigwedge$}}}}}}}^{\ast}\mathbb{R}^{2}
along each edge of
\mathbb{F}
given by

\mathscr{E}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\nu_{k})c(\nu_{k}^{\prime})\omega=-\omega

for
k=1,2
as in Definition
2.13
, where
\nu_{k}
are unit inner normal vectors of the edges of
\mathbb{F}
and
\nu^{\prime}_{k}
are unit inner normal vectors of the corresponding edges of
\mathbb{F}^{\prime}
(see Figure
11
). Let
D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{B}
be the de Rham operator acting on
\mathord{\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{$\bigwedge$}}}}}}}^{*}T\mathbb{F}
with the boundary condition
B
. Suppose that the angle
\alpha
of
\mathbb{F}
is less than or equal to
\pi
. Then
D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{B}
is essentially self-adjoint if and only if
\alpha\leq\beta
and
\alpha+\beta\leq 2\pi
.

Proof.

\scriptstyle\nu_{1}=\nu_{1}^{\prime}
\scriptstyle\nu_{2}
\scriptstyle\nu_{2}^{\prime}
\mathbb{F}

Figure 11.
The boundary conditions at the two edges of
\mathbb{F}
.

By applying a rotation on
\mathbb{F}^{\prime}
if necessary, we may assume
\nu_{1}=\nu_{1}^{\prime}
. Then the vector
\nu_{2}^{\prime}
differs from
\nu_{2}
by a counterclockwise rotation through the angle
(\beta-\alpha)
.

By line (
3.18
), the de Rham operator
D^{\mathrm{dR}}
is conjugate to

(3.22)

\Psi^{*}D^{\mathrm{dR}}\Psi=\begin{pmatrix}0&-\frac{\partial}{\partial r}\\
\frac{\partial}{\partial r}&0\end{pmatrix}+\frac{1}{r}\begin{pmatrix}0&P\\
P&0\end{pmatrix}.

Here, the operator
P
is given by

P=\begin{pmatrix}-1/2&-\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\\
\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}&-1/2\end{pmatrix}=D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{\mathbb{L}}-\frac{1}{2}

with respect to the splitting
\Omega^{*}\mathbb{L}\cong\Omega^{0}\mathbb{L}\oplus\Omega^{1}\mathbb{L}
, where
D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{\mathbb{L}}
is the de Rham operator on
\mathbb{L}=[0,\alpha]
. See line (
3.15
).

By a direct computation, the boundary condition
B_{\mathbb{L}}
induced by
B
is as follows. If

\Psi^{-1}w=\begin{pmatrix}\varphi_{0}\\
\varphi_{1}\end{pmatrix}\textup{, where }\varphi_{i}=\varphi_{i,0}+\varphi_{i,1}d\theta,

then the boundary condition
B_{\mathbb{L}}
requires

(3.23)

\varphi_{i,1}(0)=0,\textup{ and }-\varphi_{i,0}(\alpha)\sin\frac{\beta-\alpha}{2}+\varphi_{i,1}(\alpha)\cos\frac{\beta-\alpha}{2}=0,~\forall i=0,1.

Suppose that
\phi=\phi_{0}+\phi_{1}d\theta
satisfying
B_{\mathbb{L}}
above is an eigenvector of
P
with eigenvalue
\lambda-\frac{1}{2}
. Therefore, we have
D_{\mathbb{L}}^{\mathrm{dR}}\phi=\lambda\phi
, namely

(3.24)

-\phi^{\prime}_{1}=\lambda\phi_{0},\text{ and }\phi^{\prime}_{0}=\lambda\phi_{1}.

Hence
\phi^{\prime\prime}_{1}=-\lambda^{2}\phi_{1}
. By the boundary condition
\phi_{1}(0)=0
, we see that
\phi_{1}(\theta)=C\cdot\sin(\lambda\theta)
for some constant
C
. It follows that
\phi_{0}(\theta)=-C\cdot\cos(\lambda\theta)
. The boundary condition at
\theta=\alpha
implies that

(3.25)

\sin(\lambda\alpha)\cos\frac{\beta-\alpha}{2}+\cos(\lambda\alpha)\sin\frac{\beta-\alpha}{2}=0,

that is,
\sin(\lambda\alpha+\frac{\beta-\alpha}{2})=0
. Therefore, the spectrum of the operator
D_{\mathbb{L}}^{\mathrm{dR}}
with respect to
B_{\mathbb{L}}
is

\Big\{-\frac{\beta-\alpha}{2\alpha}+\frac{m\pi}{\alpha}\Big\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}.

Hence the spectrum of
P_{B}=-1/2+D_{\mathbb{L}}^{\mathrm{dR}}
with the boundary condition
B_{\mathbb{L}}
is given by

\Big\{-\frac{\beta}{2\alpha}+\frac{k\pi}{\alpha}\Big\}_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}=\left\{\cdots,-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\beta-\alpha}{2\alpha},\frac{1}{2}+\frac{2\pi-\beta-\alpha}{2\alpha},\cdots\right\}.

In particular,
P_{B}
has a spectral gap
\geq 1/2
if and only if
\beta\geq\alpha
and
\alpha+\beta\leq 2\pi
. By Lemma
3.8
, this is equivalent to the essential
self-adjointness of
D_{B}^{\mathrm{dR}}
. This finishes the proof.
∎

A direct computation shows that the angle comparison condition:

\alpha\leq\pi,\alpha\leq\beta\textup{ and }\alpha+\beta\leq 2\pi

in Lemma
3.9
is equivalent to the following inner product comparison condition:

\alpha\leq\pi\textup{ and }\langle\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\rangle\leq\langle\nu^{\prime}_{1},\nu^{\prime}_{2}\rangle.

This shows that the key input from the sector
\mathbb{F}^{\prime}
is the unit inner normal vectors along its edges, rather than the geometric angle itself.
In fact, boundary condition
B
along the edges of
\mathbb{F}
:

\mathscr{E}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\nu_{k})c(\nu_{k}^{\prime})\omega=-\omega

is well-defined as long as we specify some unit vectors

\nu^{\prime}_{k}
. Here the vectors
\nu_{k}
remain the unit inner normal vector of the edges of
\mathbb{F}
, but
\nu^{\prime}_{k}
need not be the unit inner normal vectors of an actual sector
\mathbb{F}^{\prime}
. In fact, we may omit
\mathbb{F}^{\prime}
from the discussion entirely and focus solely on
\mathbb{F}
, its unit inner normal vectors, and a chosen auxiliary unit vector along each edge.

The above observation will play a crucial role in the computation of the Fredholm index of the twisted Dirac operator
D_{B}
later. Let us summarize the above discussion by the following lemma.

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
\mathbb{F}
be a sectors in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
with angle
0<\alpha\leq\pi
.
Suppose that
\nu_{1}
and
\nu_{2}
are the inner normal vectors of
\mathbb{F}
, and
\nu_{1}^{\prime}
and
\nu_{2}^{\prime}
are two unit vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
Let
B
be the boundary condition on
\mathord{\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{$\bigwedge$}}}}}}}^{*}T\mathbb{F}=\mathord{\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{$\bigwedge$}}}}}}}^{\ast}\mathbb{R}^{2}
over each edge given by

\mathscr{E}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\nu_{k})c(\nu_{k}^{\prime})\omega=-\omega

for
k=1,2
. Let
D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{B}
be the de Rham operator acting on
\mathord{\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{$\bigwedge$}}}}}}}^{*}T\mathbb{F}
with the boundary condition
B
. Then
D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{B}
is essentially self-adjoint if and only if

\langle\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\rangle\leq\langle\nu_{1}^{\prime},\nu_{2}^{\prime}\rangle.

Moreover,
|P_{B}|>1/2
if and only if
\langle\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\rangle<\langle\nu_{1}^{\prime},\nu_{2}^{\prime}\rangle
, where
P_{B}
is the operator
P
along the link (subject to the boundary condition
B_{\mathbb{L}}
) as in the proof of Lemma
3.9
.

3.4.
Essential self-adjointness for three dimensional model spaces

In this subsection, we investigate the essential self-adjointness of

D_{B}^{\mathrm{dR}}
on
\mathbb{F}
in the case
\dim\mathbb{F}=3
.

\mathbb{L}

Figure 12.
A two dimensional link
\mathbb{L}
of a three dimensional cone.

Let
\mathbb{F}
be a convex polyhedral cone in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, bounded by planes through the
origin. The boundary condition
B
on
\mathord{\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{$\bigwedge$}}}}}}}^{*}T\mathbb{F}
over
\mathbb{F}
is given as

\mathscr{E}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\nu_{k})c(\nu_{k}^{\prime})w=-w\textup{ on each face }F_{k},

where
\nu_{k}
is the inner normal vector on
F_{k}
, and
\nu_{k}^{\prime}
is some constant unit vector.
The metric on
\mathbb{F}
is conical, and the link
\mathbb{L}=\mathbb{F}\cap\mathbb{S}^{2}
is a convex spherical polygon. See Figure
12
. The boundary condition
B
induces a boundary condition
B_{\mathbb{L}}
on
\mathbb{L}
.
The dihedral angles of
\mathbb{L}
are precisely the corresponding dihedral angles of
\mathbb{F}
.

Lemma 3.11
.

With the above notation, let
D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{\mathbb{L}}
be the de Rham operator on
\mathord{\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{$\bigwedge$}}}}}}}^{*}T\mathbb{L}
, and
B_{\mathbb{L}}
the boundary condition on
\mathbb{L}
induced by
B
. Assume that dihedral angles of
\mathbb{L}
are less than
\pi
. If

\langle\nu_{i},\nu_{j}\rangle\leq\langle\nu_{i}^{\prime},\nu_{j}^{\prime}\rangle

for any adjacent pair of faces
F_{i},F_{j}
, then
D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{\mathbb{L}}
subject to the boundary condition
B_{\mathbb{L}}
is essentially self-adjoint.

Proof.

By the discussion after Definition

3.1
, it suffices to show that the de Rham operator
D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{\mathbb{L}}
subject to the boundary condition
B_{\mathbb{L}}
is locally essentially self-adjoint at every point
x\in\mathbb{L}
.

Interior of
\mathbb{L}
.
If
x
lies in the interior of
\mathbb{L}
, local essential self-adjointness follows from classical elliptic regularity theory.

Edge of
\mathbb{L}
.
Suppose
x
lies in the interior of a codimension-one face of
\mathbb{L}
, say
x\in F_{i}\cap\mathbb{L}
for some fixed
i
. Recall that
\mathord{\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{$\bigwedge$}}}}}}}^{*}T\mathbb{F}=S(\mathbb{R}^{3}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{3})
. A rotation on the second copy of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
induces a unitary operator
U
on
S(\mathbb{R}^{3}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{3})
such that
U^{*}D^{\mathrm{dR}}U=D^{\mathrm{dR}}
. Moreover, by fixing an orthogonal identification between the two copies of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
in
S(\mathbb{R}^{3}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{3})
, we can naturally view both
\nu^{\prime}_{i}
and
\nu_{i}
as elements of the same ambient space
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Now after applying a unitary transformation on the second copy of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, we may assume without loss of generality that
\nu^{\prime}_{i}=\nu_{i}
. In this case, the induced boundary condition
B_{\mathbb{L}}
preserves the even-odd grading of the differential forms
\mathord{\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{$\bigwedge$}}}}}}}^{*}T\mathbb{L}
of
\mathbb{L}
. In fact,
B_{\mathbb{L}}
becomes the standard absolute boundary condition (see Example
2.14
) for the de Rham operator
D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{\mathbb{L}}
. It is a classical result that
D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{\mathbb{L}}
is locally essentially self-adjoint at
x
in this case.

Vertex of
\mathbb{L}
.
Suppose
x
is a vertex of
\mathbb{L}
, say
x=F_{i}\cap F_{j}\cap\mathbb{L}
for some fixed
i
and
j
. By applying a unitary transformation on the second copy of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
if necessary, we may assume without loss of generality that
\nu^{\prime}_{i}=\nu_{i}
and that
\nu_{j},\nu^{\prime}_{j}
are tangential to
\mathbb{L}
at the vertex
x
.

Let
\mathbb{L}_{r}=\mathbb{F}\cap\mathbb{S}^{2}_{r}
denote the link at radius
r
. Let
\mathbb{G}_{r}\coloneqq\mathcal{C}_{rx}\mathbb{L}_{r}
be the tangent cone of
\mathbb{L}_{r}
at
rx
, which is a sector in
T_{rx}\mathbb{L}_{r}
. Let
U\subset\mathbb{G}=\mathbb{G}_{1}
be a sufficiently small neighborhood of the origin in
\mathcal{C}_{x}\mathbb{L}
. The exponential map
\exp_{x}\colon U\to\mathbb{L}
takes
U
to the corresponding spherical sector in
\mathbb{L}
. Define

\Phi\colon(1-\varepsilon,1+\varepsilon)\times U\to\mathbb{F},\qquad\Phi(r,z)=r\exp_{x}(z).

Under the canonical identification of the tangent space
T_{rx}\mathbb{F}
with
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, the tangent space
T_{rx}\mathbb{S}^{2}_{r}
can be naturally viewed as the plane passing through
rx
and orthogonal to the edge
F_{i}\cap F_{j}
. In particular,
(1-\varepsilon,1+\varepsilon)\times U
can be naturally viewed as subspace of
\mathbb{F}
. Therefore,
\Phi
is a smooth map from a subspace of
\mathbb{F}
to
\mathbb{F}
.

Because the bundle
S(\mathbb{R}^{3}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{3})
is trivial on
\mathbb{F}
, its pullback bundle under the map
\Phi
is canonically identified with the trivial bundle
S(\mathbb{R}^{3}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{3})
itself. Consequently, the boundary condition
B
along codimension one faces
F_{i}
and
F_{j}
of
\mathbb{F}
pulls back exactly to the same constant boundary condition. In particular, the induced boundary condition
B_{\mathbb{G}}
on the boundary rays of
\mathbb{G}
is tangential to
\mathbb{G}
, since the vectors
\nu_{i}
,
\nu^{\prime}_{i}
,
\nu_{j}
, and
\nu^{\prime}_{j}
all lie within the plane of
\mathbb{G}
.

The operator
D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{\mathbb{L}}
on the spherical link
\mathbb{L}
pulls back to a differential operator on the flat sector
\mathbb{G}
. For sufficiently small
\rho
, an explicit computation in polar coordinates
(\rho,\theta)
for the sector yields:

\displaystyle\Phi^{\ast}D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{\mathbb{L}}\Phi

\displaystyle=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\partial_{\rho})\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial\rho}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\cot\rho-\rho^{-1}\right)\Big)+\frac{1}{\sin\rho}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\partial_{\theta})\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}

\displaystyle=D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{\mathbb{G}}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\cot\rho-\rho^{-1}\right)\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\partial_{\rho})+\Big(\frac{1}{\sin\rho}-\frac{1}{\rho}\Big)\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\partial_{\theta})\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}

where
D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{\mathbb{G}}

is the standard de Rham operator on the flat sector
\mathbb{G}
.

By assumption, dihedral angles of
\mathbb{L}
are less than
\pi
and
\langle\nu_{i},\nu_{j}\rangle\leq\langle\nu_{i}^{\prime},\nu_{j}^{\prime}\rangle
holds. The canonical identification above preserves these properties for
\mathbb{G}
. Therefore, by Lemma
3.10
, the operator
D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{\mathbb{G}}
is essentially self-adjoint on the flat sector
\mathbb{G}
.

To conclude that the operator
\Phi^{\ast}D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{\mathbb{L}}\Phi
is locally essentially self-adjoint at the vertex
x\in\mathbb{L}
, we apply the Kato–Rellich perturbation theorem (see for example
[
22
, Chapter 33, Section 4]
).
11
11

11

Recall the Kato–Rellich theorem: Suppose
\mathbf{D}
is essentially self-adjoint acting on a Hilbert space
H
, and let
\mathbf{T}
be a symmetric operator whose domain contains
\mathrm{dom}(\mathbf{D})
. If there exist constants
a\geq 0
and
b<1
such that for all
u\in\mathrm{dom}(\mathbf{D})
,
\|\mathbf{T}u\|^{2}\leq a\|u\|^{2}+b\|\mathbf{D}u\|^{2}
, then
\mathbf{D}+\mathbf{T}
is also essentially self-adjoint. Furthermore, the domain of the closure
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
contains
\mathrm{dom}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{})
, and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}+\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
is self-adjoint on
\mathrm{dom}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{})
.
Note that the coefficients
\left(\cot\rho-\rho^{-1}\right)
and
\left(\frac{1}{\sin\rho}-\frac{1}{\rho}\right)
vanish as
\rho\to 0
. Therefore, by restricting to a sufficiently small neighborhood of the origin of
\mathbb{G}
, we have

\big|\cot\rho-\rho^{-1}\big|\leq\delta\textup{ and }\big|\frac{1}{\sin\rho}-\frac{1}{\rho}\big|\leq\delta,

for an arbitrarily small
\delta>0
. We can smoothly extend these coefficients to compactly supported functions
\varphi_{1}
and
\varphi_{2}
defined on all of
\mathbb{G}
, satisfying the uniform bounds
|\varphi_{1}|\leq 2\delta
and
|\varphi_{2}|\leq 2\delta
. Let us define the operator:

\mathcal{D}=D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{\mathbb{G}}+\frac{1}{2}\varphi_{1}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\partial_{\rho})+\varphi_{2}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\partial_{\theta})\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}.

As long as
\delta
is chosen to be sufficiently small, the assumptions of the Kato-Rellich theorem are satisfied for
\mathcal{D}
relative to
D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{\mathbb{G}}
. It immediately follows that
\mathcal{D}
is essentially self-adjoint on
\mathbb{G}
. In particular,
\mathcal{D}
is locally essentially self-adjoint at the origin of
\mathbb{G}
.

Because
\Phi^{\ast}D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{\mathbb{L}}\Phi=\mathcal{D}
in a neighborhood of the origin,
\Phi^{\ast}D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{\mathbb{L}}\Phi
is also locally essentially self-adjoint at the origin. Finally, since the exponential map
\Phi
is smooth and naturally maps
H^{1}(\mathbb{L},S(\mathbb{R}^{3}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{3});B)
to
H^{1}(\mathbb{G},S(\mathbb{R}^{3}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{3});B)
, we conclude that the original operator
D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{\mathbb{L}}
is locally essentially self-adjoint at the vertex
x
. This completes the proof.
∎

Let
P
be the link operator as in (
3.15
). Since
P
differs from the
D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{\mathbb{L}}
by a bounded order zero operator. The essentially self-adjointness of
P
(subject to the boundary condition
B_{\mathbb{L}}
) is equivalent to that of
D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{\mathbb{L}}
(subject to the boundary condition
B_{\mathbb{L}}
).
We now estimate the spectral gap of
P_{B_{\mathbb{L}}}
.

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
\mathbb{F}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a convex polyhedral cone. The boundary condition
B
on
\mathord{\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{$\bigwedge$}}}}}}}^{*}T\mathbb{F}
over
\mathbb{F}
is given as

\mathscr{E}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\nu_{k})c(\nu_{k}^{\prime})w=-w\textup{ on each face }F_{k},

where
\nu_{k}
is the inner normal vector on
F_{k}
, and
\nu_{k}^{\prime}
is some constant unit vector. Let
P
be the link operator in
(
3.15
), and let
B_{\mathbb{L}}
be the boundary condition on
the link
\mathbb{L}
induced by the boundary condition
B
on
\mathbb{F}
. Assume that

P_{B_{\mathbb{L}}}
is essentially self-adjoint. Then
P_{B_{\mathbb{L}}}
is invertible
and satisfies
|P_{B_{\mathbb{L}}}|\geq\sqrt{2}/2
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.11
, the operator
P
on the spherical link
\mathbb{L}
with boundary condition
B_{\mathbb{L}}
is essentially self-adjoint with domain
H^{1}(\mathbb{L},\mathord{\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{$\bigwedge$}}}}}}}^{*}T\mathbb{L};B_{\mathbb{L}})
, provided that the inner product comparison holds.
Since
P_{B_{\mathbb{L}}}
is essentially self-adjoint, it suffices to estimate its spectrum on
C^{\infty}_{00}(\mathbb{L},\mathord{\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{$\bigwedge$}}}}}}}^{*}T\mathbb{L};B_{\mathbb{L}})
consisting of smooth forms satisfying
the boundary condition
B_{\mathbb{L}}
, supported away from the vertices of

\mathbb{L}
.

Let
D=D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{\mathbb{L}}
the de Rham operator acting on
\mathord{\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{$\bigwedge$}}}}}}}^{*}\mathbb{L}
over the link
\mathbb{L}
. The operator
P
differs from
D
by the diagonal zeroth-order term

(-1)^{p}(p-1)
on
p
-forms. See (
3.15
). Let
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}
and
c
be the left and right Clifford multiplications on
\mathord{\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{$\bigwedge$}}}}}}}^{*}\mathbb{L}
. A direct computation gives

(3.26)

P=D+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(e_{i})c(e_{i}),

where
\{e_{i}\}
is a local orthonormal frame of
T\mathbb{L}
. Define a new connection
\widehat{\nabla}
on
\mathord{\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{$\bigwedge$}}}}}}}^{*}\mathbb{L}
by

(3.27)

\widehat{\nabla}_{X}\coloneqq\nabla_{X}+\frac{1}{2}c(X),

for
X\in T\mathbb{L}
, where
\nabla
is the canonical connection on
\mathord{\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{$\bigwedge$}}}}}}}^{*}T\mathbb{L}
induced by the Levi-Civita connection of
\mathbb{L}
. Then

P=\sum_{i}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(e_{i})\widehat{\nabla}_{e_{i}}.

Now we derive a Lichnerowicz formula for
P^{2}
. At a point
x\in\mathbb{L}
, choose a local orthonormal frame
\{e_{i}\}
of
T\mathbb{L}
such that
[e_{i},e_{j}]=0
at
x
. Since the Clifford multiplications
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}
and
c
commute, we have

P^{2}=-\sum_{i}\widehat{\nabla}_{e_{i}}\widehat{\nabla}_{e_{i}}+\sum_{i<j}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(e_{i})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(e_{j})\widehat{R}_{e_{i},e_{j}},

where
\widehat{R}
is the curvature operator of
\widehat{\nabla}
:

\widehat{R}_{e_{i},e_{j}}=\widehat{\nabla}_{e_{i}}\widehat{\nabla}_{e_{j}}-\widehat{\nabla}_{e_{j}}\widehat{\nabla}_{e_{i}}.

Since the Levi-Civita connection on
\mathbb{L}
is torsion free, we obtain that

\displaystyle\widehat{R}_{e_{i},e_{j}}=

\displaystyle~(\nabla_{e_{i}}+\frac{1}{2}c(e_{i}))(\nabla_{e_{j}}+\frac{1}{2}c(e_{j}))-(\nabla_{e_{j}}+\frac{1}{2}c(e_{j}))(\nabla_{e_{i}}+\frac{1}{2}c(e_{i}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\nabla_{e_{i}}\nabla_{e_{j}}-\nabla_{e_{j}}\nabla_{e_{i}}+\frac{1}{2}c(e_{i})c(e_{j}).

Since
\mathbb{L}
has constant sectional curvature
1
with scalar curvature
2
, we obtain that

\sum_{i<j}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(e_{i})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(e_{j})(\nabla_{e_{i}}\nabla_{e_{j}}-\nabla_{e_{j}}\nabla_{e_{i}})=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i<j}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(e_{i})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(e_{j})c(e_{i})c(e_{j}),

cf. the curvature identity (
2.2
), applied with
f=\mathrm{id}\colon\mathbb{L}\to\mathbb{L}
.
Hence the Lichnerowicz formula for
P^{2}
reads

\displaystyle P^{2}=

\displaystyle~\widehat{\nabla}^{*}\widehat{\nabla}+\sum_{i<j}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(e_{i})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(e_{j})\widehat{R}_{e_{i},e_{j}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\widehat{\nabla}^{*}\widehat{\nabla}+\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i<j}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(e_{i})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(e_{j})c(e_{i})c(e_{j})+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i<j}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(e_{i})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(e_{j})c(e_{i})c(e_{j})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\widehat{\nabla}^{*}\widehat{\nabla}+\frac{1}{2}

Let
\mathbb{L}_{k}=F_{k}\cap\mathbb{L}

be an edge of
\mathbb{L}
. For a differential form
\varphi
in
C_{00}^{\infty}(\mathbb{L},\mathord{\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{$\bigwedge$}}}}}}}^{*}\mathbb{L};B_{\mathbb{L}})
,
we have

(3.28)

\begin{split}&\int_{\mathbb{L}}\langle P\varphi,P\varphi\rangle\\
=&\int_{\mathbb{L}}\langle P^{2}\varphi,\varphi\rangle+\sum_{k}\int_{\mathbb{L}_{k}}\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\nu_{k})P\varphi,\varphi\rangle\\
=&\int_{\mathbb{L}}\langle\big(\widehat{\nabla}^{*}\widehat{\nabla}+\frac{1}{2}\big)\varphi,\varphi\rangle+\sum_{k}\int_{\mathbb{L}_{k}}\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\nu_{k})P\varphi,\varphi\rangle\\
=&\int_{\mathbb{L}}|\widehat{\nabla}\varphi|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}|\varphi|^{2}+\sum_{k}\int_{\mathbb{L}_{k}}\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\nu_{k})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(e^{k})\widehat{\nabla}_{e^{k}}\varphi,\varphi\rangle,\end{split}

where
e^{k}
is a unit vector field of
T\mathbb{L}_{k}
. Note that
e^{k}
is an orthonormal basis of
T\mathbb{L}_{k}
, since the edge
\mathbb{L}_{k}
is one dimensional.

Define

(3.29)

\widehat{D}^{\partial}_{k}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\nu_{k})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(e^{k})\widehat{\nabla}_{e^{k}}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\nu_{k})\left(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(e^{k})\nabla_{e^{k}}+\frac{1}{2}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(e^{k})c(e^{k}).\right)

It remains to show that the boundary integrals in
(
3.28
) vanish. The boundary condition
B_{\mathbb{L}}
on the edge
\mathbb{L}_{k}
of
\mathbb{L}
is induced by the boundary condition

\mathscr{E}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\nu_{k})c(\nu_{k}^{\prime})w=-w,

where
\nu_{k}
is the inner unit normal vector field of
F_{k}
and
\nu_{k}^{\prime}
is a constant unit vector. Let
\gamma_{k}=\mathscr{E}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\nu_{k})c(\nu_{k}^{\prime}).

Claim
.

\widehat{D}^{\partial}_{k}\gamma_{k}+\gamma_{k}\widehat{D}^{\partial}_{k}=0.

Since
\gamma_{k}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\nu_{k})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(e^{k})=-\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\nu_{k})\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(e^{k})\gamma_{k}
, it suffices to show that
[\widehat{\nabla}_{e^{k}},\gamma_{k}]=0
.
In general, the unit vector
\nu_{k}^{\prime}
may not be tangent to the link
\mathbb{L}
, so we write its decomposition along
\mathbb{L}_{k}
as

(3.30)

\nu_{k}^{\prime}=\alpha_{k}\partial_{r}+\widehat{\nu}_{k},

where
\widehat{\nu}_{k}
is orthogonal to
\partial_{r}
. Under the identification from (
3.13
) and (
3.14
), we have

\gamma_{k}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathscr{E}_{\mathbb{L}}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\nu_{k})\left(-\alpha_{k}+c(\widehat{\nu}_{k})\right)&0\\
0&\mathscr{E}_{\mathbb{L}}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\nu_{k})\left(-\alpha_{k}+c(\widehat{\nu}_{k})\right)\end{pmatrix}

on two copies of
\Omega^{\ast}\mathbb{L}
, where
\mathscr{E}_{\mathbb{L}}
is the even-odd grading operator on
\mathord{\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{$\bigwedge$}}}}}}}^{*}T\mathbb{L}
. Therefore, the induced
the boundary condition
B_{\mathbb{L}}
on each copy of
\Omega^{\ast}\mathbb{L}
along
\mathbb{L}_{k}
is given by

(3.31)

\mathscr{E}_{\mathbb{L}}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\nu_{k})\left(-\alpha_{k}+c(\widehat{\nu}_{k})\right)\varphi=-\varphi.

To simplify notation, we omit the subscript
k
in
\gamma_{k},\nu_{k}
, etc., since no ambiguity is likely to arise. Let
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
be the Euclidean flat connection on
T\mathbb{F}
over
\mathbb{F}
, and
\nabla^{\mathbb{L}}
the Levi-Civita connection on
T\mathbb{L}
over
\mathbb{L}
. Then, for any tangent vector fields
X
and
Y
along
\mathbb{L}
,

\begin{cases}\displaystyle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{X}Y=\nabla^{\mathbb{L}}_{X}Y-\langle X,Y\rangle\partial_{r},\\
\displaystyle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{X}\partial_{r}=X.\end{cases}

Since
\nu^{\prime}
is constant in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, applying
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}
to (
3.30
) gives

0=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{X}\nu^{\prime}=X(\alpha)\partial_{r}+\alpha X+\nabla^{\mathbb{L}}_{X}\widehat{\nu}-\langle X,\widehat{\nu}\rangle\partial_{r}.

Separating radial and tangential components yields

(3.32)

\begin{cases}X(\alpha)=\langle X,\widehat{\nu}\rangle\\
-\alpha X=\nabla^{\mathbb{L}}_{X}\widehat{\nu}\end{cases}

Since every edge of
\mathbb{L}
is totally geodesic and
\nu
is constant in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, it follows that
c(\nu)
is parallel along the edge
\mathbb{L}_{k}
. Equivalently,
c(\nu)
commutes with the connection
\nabla
on
\mathord{\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{$\bigwedge$}}}}}}}^{*}\mathbb{L}
. Recall that
\gamma=\mathscr{E}_{\mathbb{L}}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\nu)(-\alpha+c(\widehat{\nu}))
. Applying (
3.32
), we have

\displaystyle[\nabla_{X},\gamma]=

\displaystyle~\mathscr{E}_{\mathbb{L}}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\nu)\left(-X(\alpha)+c(\nabla^{\mathbb{L}}_{X}\widehat{\nu})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\mathscr{E}_{\mathbb{L}}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\nu)\left(\langle X,\widehat{\nu}\rangle+\alpha c(X)\right).

On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}[c(X),\gamma]=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\mathscr{E}_{\mathbb{L}}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\nu)\left(c(X)(-\alpha+c(\widehat{\nu}))+(-\alpha+c(\widehat{\nu}))c(X)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\mathscr{E}_{\mathbb{L}}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\nu)\left(\alpha c(X)+\langle X,\widehat{\nu}\rangle\right).

Therefore

[\widehat{\nabla}_{X},\gamma]=[\nabla_{X},\gamma]+\frac{1}{2}[c(X),\gamma]=0.

This proves the claim.

It follows from
\widehat{D}^{\partial}_{k}\gamma_{k}+\gamma_{k}\widehat{D}^{\partial}_{k}=0
that
\langle\widehat{D}^{\partial}_{k}\varphi,\varphi\rangle=0
if
\varphi
satisfies the boundary condition
B_{\mathbb{L}}
on every face
\mathbb{L}_{k}
. Consequently, by (
3.28
), we obtain that

|P_{B_{\mathbb{L}}}|\geq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}.

∎

Remark 3.13
.

In the proof of Lemma
3.12
, the computation involving the modified connection
\widehat{\nabla}
is carried out explicitly. Here is a more conceptual viewpoint. The connection
\widehat{\nabla}
can be interpreted as tensor product connection of the spinorial connection on
T\mathbb{L}
over
\mathbb{L}
, with the spinorial flat connection on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
over
\mathbb{L}
. Under this interpretation,
\widehat{D}_{k}^{\partial}\gamma_{k}+\gamma_{k}\widehat{D}_{k}^{\partial}
is precisely determined by two contributions: the mean curvature of the
face
\mathbb{L}_{k}\subset\mathbb{L}
and the derivative of the vector

\nu^{\prime}_{k}
. The first contribution vanishes because
\mathbb{L}_{k}
is totally geodesic
in
\mathbb{L}
, and the second vanishes because
\nu^{\prime}_{k}
is a constant vector in
the ambient Euclidean space. This gives a conceptual proof of the vanishing of the boundary integrals in (
3.28
).

By Lemma
3.8
, Lemma
3.10
and Lemma
3.12
, we conclude this subsection by the following result.

Corollary 3.14
.

Let
\mathbb{F}
be a polyhedral corner in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, and
B
the boundary condition on
\mathord{\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{$\bigwedge$}}}}}}}^{*}T\mathbb{F}
over
\mathbb{F}
given by

\mathscr{E}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\nu_{k})c(\nu_{k}^{\prime})w=-w\textup{ on each face }\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k},

where
\nu_{k}
is the inner normal vector on
F_{k}
, and
\nu_{k}^{\prime}
is a constant unit vector.
Assume the dihedral angles of
\mathbb{F}
are less than
\pi
. If

\langle\nu_{i},\nu_{j}\rangle\leq\langle\nu_{i}^{\prime},\nu_{j}^{\prime}\rangle

on every edge made by adjacent faces
F_{i}
and
F_{j}
, then the de Rham operator subject to the boundary condition
B
is essentially self-adjoint.

3.5.
Essential self-adjointness for the general case

In this subsection, we establish the essential self-adjointness of the twisted Dirac operator
D_{B}
subject to suitable conditions on dihedral angles.

Theorem 3.15
.

Let
f\colon\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}\to M
be a spin polyhedral map between two compact polyhedral manifolds of dimension
3
. For each codimension-one face
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
, let

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k}
denote its inward unit normal, and let
\nu_{k}
be a smooth
unit-length section of
f^{*}TM
over
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
. Let
B
be the local boundary condition on
E=S(T\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}\oplus f^{\ast}TM)
given by

\mathscr{E}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k})c(\nu_{k})\sigma=-\sigma\textup{ on }\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}.

Assume that all dihedral angles of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
are less than
\pi
. If for each pair of adjacent codimension one faces
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{i}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{j}
of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
, on each connected component of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{i}\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{j}
, we have either

(3.33)

\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{i},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{j}\rangle<\langle\nu_{i},\nu_{j}\rangle

or

(3.34)

\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{i},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{j}\rangle=\langle\nu_{i},\nu_{j}\rangle,

then
D
is essentially self-adjoint subject to the boundary condition
B
.
Furthermore, its self-adjoint closure
{D}_{B}
is Fredholm with domain
H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E;B)
.

Remark 3.16
.

The assumption that the comparison of inner products is either strict or an equality along each connected component of a codimension-two
face in Theorem
3.15
is a technical hypothesis used to control the perturbation of boundary conditions along edges. In this form, Theorem
3.15
does not
directly cover the geometric setting of Gromov’s dihedral rigidity conjecture,
where the dihedral angle comparison is only non-strict and may vary between
strict inequality and equality along the same edge. This difficulty will be resolved via an approximation lemma from Section
5
.

Proof of Theorem
3.15
.

By the discussion after Definition

3.1
, it suffices to show that
D
(subject to the boundary condition
B
) is locally essentially self-adjoint at every point
x\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
.

Interior of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
.
If
x\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
lies in the interior of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
, the classical elliptic regularity theory implies that
D
is locally essentially self-adjoint at
x
.

Codimension one face of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
.
If
x
lies in the interior of a codimension-one face of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
, then
D
is locally modeled on a Dirac-type operator on a smooth manifold with
boundary, subject to a local elliptic boundary condition. Alternatively, by Theorem
3.5
, we can reduce this case to the standard de Rham operator on manifolds with smooth boundary subject to the absolute boundary condition (see Example
2.14
). Therefore,
D
is locally essentially self-adjoint at
x
.

Codimension two face of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
.
Suppose that
x
lies in the interior of an edge

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{i}\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{j}
. Let

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{i}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{i}(x),\qquad\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{j}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{j}(x),\qquad\omega_{i}=\nu_{i}(x),\qquad\omega_{j}=\nu_{j}(x).

By Theorem
3.5
, after passing to a neighborhood of
x
and
conjugating by the Lipschitz bundle isomorphism
\Psi
constructed there, we have

\Psi^{*}D\Psi=D^{\mathrm{dR}}+\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{B}

on a neighborhood
U
of the origin in the tangent cone

\mathcal{C}_{x}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
. Here
D^{\mathrm{dR}}
is the standard de Rham operator on the
flat tangent cone,
\mathcal{A}
is a first-order operator whose coefficient
matrices are continuous and vanish at the origin, and
\mathcal{B}
is a
bounded zeroth-order operator. Moreover,
\Psi
maps the original
boundary condition
B
to the constant model boundary condition determined by

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{i},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{j},\omega_{i},\omega_{j}
.

The tangent cone
\mathcal{C}_{x}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
is isometric to

\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{G}
, where
\mathbb{G}
is a sector in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. After fixing an orthogonal identification of the two copies of

\mathbb{R}^{3}
in
S(\mathbb{R}^{3}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{3})
, and after applying an orthogonal transformation on the
second copy of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
if necessary, we may assume that

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{i},\quad\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{j},\quad\omega_{i},\quad\omega_{j}

all lie in the plane of
\mathbb{G}
. This transformation commutes with
the de Rham operator and preserves essential self-adjointness.

Since
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{i},\omega_{i},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{j}
and
\omega_{j}
are all tangential to
\mathbb{G}
, the operator
D^{\mathrm{dR}}
subject to the constant boundary condition
B_{cst}
is

D^{\mathrm{dR}}=D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{\mathbb{R}}\otimes 1+1\otimes D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{\mathbb{G}}

where
D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{\mathbb{G}}
is the standard de Rham subject to the induced boundary condition and
D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{\mathbb{R}}
is the standard de Rham operator on
\mathbb{R}
. Since either (
3.33
) or
(
3.34
) holds along the edge, we have

\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{i},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{j}\rangle\leq\langle\omega_{i},\omega_{j}\rangle.

By Lemma
3.10
, the de Rham operator on
the sector
\mathbb{G}
with this boundary condition is essentially
self-adjoint. Hence
D^{\mathrm{dR}}
on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{G}
subject to the constant boundary condition
is essentially self-adjoint.

For any

\delta>0
, Theorem
3.5
allows us to shrink
U
so that
the coefficient matrices of
\mathcal{A}
have supremum norm at most

\delta
. After multiplying by a smooth cutoff function, we may extend
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
to operators on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{G}
with compactly supported coefficients such that
\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{B}
is formally symmetric,
\mathcal{B}
is bounded and
\mathcal{A}
satisfies

\|\mathcal{A}\varphi\|\leq C_{1}\delta\|D^{\mathrm{dR}}\varphi\|+C_{2}\|\varphi\|

for
\varphi
in the domain of
D^{\mathrm{dR}}
, where
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are independent of
\delta
. Choosing
\delta
sufficiently small gives bound
C_{1}\delta<1
. The Kato–Rellich perturbation theorem (see for example
[
22
, Chapter 33, Section 4]
) therefore implies that

D^{\mathrm{dR}}+\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{B}

is locally essentially self-adjoint. Since
\Psi
is a Lipschitz bundle
isomorphism with Lipschitz inverse and maps the constant model boundary
condition to
B
, it follows that
D
is locally essential self-adjoint at

x
.

Codimension three face of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
.
Now suppose that
x
is a vertex of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
. The argument is completely similar to the codimension two case above. By using the Lipschitz bundle isomorphism
\Psi
constructed in Theorem
3.5
for the codimension-three case, the local geometry reduces to a flat polyhedral cone in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. The operator
D
is then analyzed via a perturbation of the standard de Rham operator
D^{\mathrm{dR}}
defined on this flat cone. The same argument as in the codimension-two case above allows us to conclude that the original Dirac operator
D
on
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
is locally essentially self-adjoint at the vertex
x
. This completes the proof that
D
(subject to boundary condition
B
) is essentially self-adjoint.

It remains to prove the closure
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{D}_{B}
of
D_{B}
is Fredholm. Since
D_{B}
is essentially self-adjoint,
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{D}_{B}
is self-adjoint and its domain is the minimal domain
H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E;B)
. Consider the resolvent operator

(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{D}_{B}+i)^{-1}\colon L^{2}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E)\to H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E;B).

By Proposition
2.16
and the discussion preceding it, this map is norm-continuous. Furthermore, by Rellich’s lemma, the inclusion mapping
H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E;B)\hookrightarrow L^{2}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E)
is compact.
By composing these maps, it follows that
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{D}_{B}+i)^{-1}\colon L^{2}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E)\to L^{2}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E)
is a compact operator. As a result, the spectrum of
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{D}_{B}+i)^{-1}
, and therefore that of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{D}_{B}
, is discrete. The kernel of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{D}_{B}
corresponds exactly to the eigenspace of
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{D}_{B}+i)^{-1}
associated with the eigenvalue
-i
. Because the eigenspaces of a compact operator corresponding to non-zero eigenvalues are finite-dimensional, it follows that the kernel of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{D}_{B}
is finite-dimensional.

Moreover, the discreteness of the spectrum of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{D}_{B}
ensures a spectral gap around zero, which implies that the image of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{D}_{B}
is closed. Because
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{D}_{B}
is self-adjoint, its cokernel is isomorphic to its kernel and is thus also finite-dimensional. This completes the proof that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{D}_{B}
is Fredholm.
∎

3.6.
Boundedness of multiplication of
1/r
on
\mathbb{R}^{3}

In this subsection, we recall Hardy’s inequality in the form needed later for
the proof of the index theorem for polyhedral manifolds
(Theorem
6.4
). For the reader’s convenience, we
include a proof.

Lemma 3.17
.

Let
n\geq 3
, and let
r=|x|
be the Euclidean radial function on

\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then multiplying by
r^{-1}
defines a bounded linear operator

H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})\to L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{n}),~\varphi\mapsto r^{-1}\varphi,

where
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is the Sobolev
H^{1}
-space on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Proof.

By the density of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
in
H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, it
suffices to prove the estimate for
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, where
C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
is the space of compactly supported smooth functions on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Using polar coordinates
x=r\theta
, with

\theta\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
and
r\in(0,\infty)
, we have

\left\|r^{-1}\varphi\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}\int_{0}^{\infty}|\varphi(r,\theta)|^{2}r^{n-3}\,dr\,d\theta.

For fixed
\theta
, integration by parts gives

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}|\varphi(r,\theta)|^{2}r^{n-3}\,dr

\displaystyle=-\frac{2}{n-2}\operatorname{Re}\int_{0}^{\infty}\overline{\varphi(r,\theta)}\,\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial r}(r,\theta)r^{n-2}\,dr

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{n-2}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}|\varphi(r,\theta)|^{2}r^{n-3}\,dr\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\left|\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial r}(r,\theta)\right|^{2}r^{n-1}\,dr\right)^{1/2}.

Therefore,

\int_{0}^{\infty}|\varphi(r,\theta)|^{2}r^{n-3}\,dr\leq\frac{4}{(n-2)^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left|\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial r}(r,\theta)\right|^{2}r^{n-1}\,dr.

Integrating over
\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
yields

\left\|r^{-1}\varphi\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq\frac{4}{(n-2)^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\left|\frac{\partial\varphi}{\partial r}(r,\theta)\right|^{2}r^{n-1}\,dr\,d\theta.

Since
\left|\partial\varphi/\partial r\right|\leq|\nabla\varphi|,

we obtain

\left\|r^{-1}\varphi\right\|_{L^{2}}\leq\frac{4}{(n-2)^{2}}\|\nabla\varphi\|_{L^{2}}\leq\frac{4}{(n-2)^{2}}\|\varphi\|_{H^{1}}.

The estimate extends by density to all
\varphi\in H^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, proving
the lemma.
∎

4.
A gluing formula for the Fredholm index

In this section, we establish a gluing formula for the Fredholm index of the
Dirac operator. This formula will be used later in the computation of the
Fredholm index of
D_{B}
.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
f\colon\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}\to M
be a spin polyhedral map between two compact polyhedral manifolds of dimension
3
. For each codimension-one face
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
, let

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k}
denote its inward unit normal, and let
\nu_{k}
be a smooth
unit-length section of
f^{*}TM
over
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
. Let
B
be the local boundary condition on
E=S(T\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}\oplus f^{\ast}TM)
given by

\mathscr{E}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k})c(\nu_{k})\sigma=-\sigma\textup{ on }\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}.

Assume that all dihedral angles of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
are less than
\pi
. If for each pair of adjacent codimension one faces
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{i}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{j}
of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
, on each connected component of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{i}\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{j}
, we have either

\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{i},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{j}\rangle<\langle\nu_{i},\nu_{j}\rangle

or

\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{i},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{j}\rangle=\langle\nu_{i},\nu_{j}\rangle.

Suppose that
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
decomposes as
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}\cup_{\Sigma}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{2}
, where
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{2}
are polyhedral manifolds, and
\Sigma=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{2}
is a hypersurface in
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
that is disjoint from all faces of codimension greater than two, and is orthogonal to every codimension-one face
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
that it intersects; see Figure
13
.

Let
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{\Sigma}
denote the unit inner normal vector field to
\Sigma
, when
\Sigma
is viewed as a boundary hypersurface of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{2}
. Assume
\nu_{\Sigma}
is a smooth unit-length section of
f^{\ast}TM
on
\Sigma
such that
\nu_{\Sigma}
is orthogonal to
\nu_{k}
along
\Sigma\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
. Let
B_{1}
be the boundary condition for
E|_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}}
which coincides with
B
on
\partial\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}\setminus\Sigma
and is given on
\Sigma
by

(4.1)

\mathscr{E}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{\Sigma})c(\nu_{\Sigma})\sigma=\sigma.

Similarly, let
B_{2}
be the boundary condition for
E|_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{2}}
which coincides with
B
on
\partial\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{2}\setminus\Sigma
and is given on
\Sigma
by

(4.2)

\mathscr{E}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{\Sigma})c(\nu_{\Sigma})\sigma=-\sigma.

Let
D^{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{i}}
denote the restriction of
D
to
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{i}
. Then

D^{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}}_{B_{1}}
and
D^{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{2}}_{B_{2}}
are essentially self-adjoint
and Fredholm. Moreover,

(4.3)

\textup{Ind}(D_{B})=\textup{Ind}(D^{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}}_{B_{1}})+\textup{Ind}(D^{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{2}}_{B_{2}}).

As a preparation for the proof of Theorem
4.1
, we first prove a few lemmas.

\Sigma
\Sigma

Figure 13.
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
decomposes into
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{2}
along
\Sigma
.

Lemma 4.2
.

The operators
D^{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}}_{B_{1}}
and
D^{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{2}}_{B_{2}}
are essentially
self-adjoint and Fredholm.

Proof.

We prove the statement for
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}
; the proof for
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{2}
is
identical. The codimension-one faces of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}
consist of
\Sigma
and
the faces

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{1,k}\coloneqq\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}

coming from codimension-one faces
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
.

For adjacent faces
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{1,i}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{1,j}
inherited from

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
, the required inner-product comparison is exactly the corresponding
comparison on
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{i}\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{j}
. Now suppose that
\Sigma
and

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{1,i}
are adjacent. The unit inner normal to
\Sigma
as a
boundary face of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}
is
-\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{\Sigma}
. Thus the boundary
condition (
4.1
) is precisely the standard condition

\mathscr{E}\,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(-\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{\Sigma})c(\nu_{\Sigma})\sigma=-\sigma

written using the inward normal of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}
. Moreover, by assumption,

\langle-\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{\Sigma},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{i}\rangle=0=\langle\nu_{\Sigma},\nu_{i}\rangle
