along
\Sigma\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{1,i}
. Hence the equality case of the
inner-product comparison holds for the pair

\Sigma,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{1,i}
.

Therefore all hypotheses of Theorem
3.15
hold for

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}
with boundary condition
B_{1}
. It follows that

D^{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}}_{B_{1}}
is essentially self-adjoint and Fredholm.
∎

With the same notation as in Theorem
4.1
, let
D^{\Sigma}
be the restriction of the Dirac operator
D
on
\Sigma
. The boundary condition
B
along the codimension one faces
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{i}
of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
restricts to a boundary condition on the codimension one faces
\Sigma_{k}\coloneqq\Sigma\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
of
\Sigma
. Let us denote this induced boundary condition by
B_{\Sigma}
.

Lemma 4.3
.

Assume the hypotheses of Theorem
4.1
. Then

D^{\Sigma}
, subject to
B_{\Sigma}
, is essentially self-adjoint and
Fredholm.

Proof.

On
\Sigma
, consider the orthogonal decompositions

T\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}=\mathbb{R}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{\Sigma}\oplus T\Sigma\textup{ and }f^{*}TM=\mathbb{R}\nu_{\Sigma}\oplus(\mathbb{R}\nu_{\Sigma})^{\perp}.

Set

\omega=\mathscr{E}\,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{\Sigma})c(\nu_{\Sigma}).

The operator
\omega
is a self-adjoint involution. With respect to its

(\pm 1)
-eigenspace decomposition, the bundle
E|_{\Sigma}
is identified
with two copies of
S\bigl(T\Sigma\oplus(\mathbb{R}\nu_{\Sigma})^{\perp}\bigr)
:

E\cong S\bigl(T\Sigma\oplus(\mathbb{R}\nu_{\Sigma})^{\perp}\bigr)\oplus S\bigl(T\Sigma\oplus(\mathbb{R}\nu_{\Sigma})^{\perp}\bigr).

Since
\Sigma
is orthogonal to every face
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
that it
intersects and
\nu_{\Sigma}
is orthogonal to the corresponding
\nu_{k}
, a
direct computation shows that
\omega
commutes with the induced boundary condition

B_{\Sigma}
along each
\Sigma_{k}=\Sigma\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
. Hence
B_{\Sigma}
splits to boundary conditions on each
copy of
S\bigl(T\Sigma\oplus(\mathbb{R}\nu_{\Sigma})^{\perp}\bigr)
. Moreover, the
inner-product comparison at the vertices of
\Sigma
is exactly the
corresponding comparison along the adjacent faces of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
.

The endomorphism
\omega
need not be parallel for the original connection. We
therefore introduce the modified connection

\widetilde{\nabla}_{X}=\nabla_{X}+\frac{1}{2}\omega(\nabla_{X}\omega),\qquad X\in T\Sigma.

Since
\omega^{2}=1
, this connection satisfies
\widetilde{\nabla}_{X}\omega=0.

Define

\widetilde{D}^{\Sigma}=\sum_{a}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e}_{a})\widetilde{\nabla}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e}_{a}},

where
\{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{e}_{a}\}
is a local orthonormal frame of
T\Sigma
. By construction,

\widetilde{D}^{\Sigma}
commutes with
\omega
, and hence restricts to each
of the two summands above.

To summarize, we see that
the two-dimensional essential self-adjointness criterion
(Lemma
3.10
and Theorem
3.15
) implies that
\widetilde{D}^{\Sigma}
with boundary
condition
B_{\Sigma}
is essentially self-adjoint and Fredholm. Finally,
D^{\Sigma}-\widetilde{D}^{\Sigma}
is a bounded zeroth-order
operator. It follows immediately that

D^{\Sigma}
with boundary condition
B_{\Sigma}
is also essentially
self-adjoint and Fredholm.
∎

Since the operator
D^{\Sigma}_{B_{\Sigma}}
from Lemma
4.3
is essentially self-adjoint, the domain of its closure is
H^{1}(\Sigma,E;B_{\Sigma})
. The operator
D^{\Sigma}_{B_{\Sigma}}
has discrete spectrum. Choose a complete orthonormal basis
\{\varphi_{\lambda}\}
of
L^{2}(\Sigma,E)
consisting of eigenfunctions of
D^{\Sigma}_{B_{\Sigma}}
with

D^{\Sigma}_{B_{\Sigma}}\varphi_{\lambda}=\lambda\varphi_{\lambda}.

Definition 4.4
.

Define

H^{1/2}(\Sigma,E;B_{\Sigma})=\Big\{\varphi=\sum_{\lambda}a_{\lambda}\varphi_{\lambda}\in L^{2}(\Sigma,E):\sum_{\lambda}|a_{\lambda}|^{2}(1+|\lambda|)<\infty\Big\},

equipped with the norm

\|\varphi\|_{1/2}^{2}=\sum_{\lambda}|a_{\lambda}|^{2}(1+|\lambda|).

Lemma 4.5
.

For
i=1,2
, let

\tau_{i}\colon H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{i},E)\longrightarrow H^{1/2}(\Sigma,E)

be the trace map. Then there exists a bounded linear operator

\mathcal{E}_{i}\colon H^{1/2}(\Sigma,E;B_{\Sigma})\longrightarrow H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{i},E;B_{\mathrm{ext},i})

such that
\tau_{i}\circ\mathcal{E}_{i}=\mathrm{id}.

Here
B_{\mathrm{ext},i}
denotes the boundary condition
B
on

\partial\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{i}\setminus\Sigma
; no boundary condition is imposed on

\Sigma
.

Proof.

It suffices to construct the extension in a collar neighborhood of

\Sigma
. We first consider the product model

[0,1]\times\Sigma

with product metric
dt^{2}+g_{\Sigma}
, and with a boundary condition
\widetilde{B}

constant in the
t
-direction along the side faces

[0,1]\times\Sigma_{k}
. In this case, the corresponding Dirac operator takes the form

\not{D}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\partial_{t})\frac{\partial}{\partial t}+D^{\Sigma}.

Let
\chi\in C^{\infty}([0,1])
satisfy
\chi(t)=1
for

0\leq t\leq 1/3
and
\chi(t)=0
for
t\geq 2/3
. Choose an even positive
Schwartz function
\Phi
on
\mathbb{R}
such that
\Phi(0)=1
. For

\varphi=\sum_{\lambda}a_{\lambda}\varphi_{\lambda}\in H^{1/2}(\Sigma,E;B_{\Sigma}),

define

\mathcal{E}^{\sharp}\varphi(t)=\chi(t)\Phi(tD^{\Sigma}_{B_{\Sigma}})\varphi=\chi(t)\sum_{\lambda}\Phi(t\lambda)a_{\lambda}\varphi_{\lambda}.

Then
\tau(\mathcal{E}^{\sharp}\varphi)=\varphi
,
because
\chi(0)=1
and
\Phi(0)=1
.

We now estimate the
H^{1}
norm of
\mathcal{E}^{\sharp}\varphi
. The
L^{2}
norm satisfies

\|\mathcal{E}^{\sharp}\varphi\|_{L^{2}([0,1]\times\Sigma)}\leq C\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}.

For the normal derivative, we have

\partial_{t}\mathcal{E}^{\sharp}\varphi(t)=\chi^{\prime}(t)\Phi(tD^{\Sigma})\varphi+\chi(t)D^{\Sigma}\Phi^{\prime}(tD^{\Sigma})\varphi.

The term involving
\chi^{\prime}
is bounded by

C\|\varphi\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}
. The remaining term satisfies:

\displaystyle\left\|\chi(t)D^{\Sigma}\Phi^{\prime}(tD^{\Sigma})\varphi\right\|_{L^{2}([0,1]\times\Sigma)}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{\lambda}|\lambda|\,|a_{\lambda}|^{2}\int_{0}^{|\lambda|}|\Phi^{\prime}(s)|^{2}\,ds

\displaystyle\leq C\|\Phi^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}\|\varphi\|_{1/2}^{2}.

Similarly,

\displaystyle\left\|\chi(t)D^{\Sigma}_{B_{\Sigma}}\Phi(tD^{\Sigma}_{B_{\Sigma}})\varphi\right\|_{L^{2}([0,1]\times\Sigma)}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{\lambda}|\lambda|\,|a_{\lambda}|^{2}\int_{0}^{|\lambda|}|\Phi(s)|^{2}\,ds

\displaystyle\leq C\|\Phi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R})}^{2}\|\varphi\|_{1/2}^{2}.

For each
t>0
,
\Phi(tD^{\Sigma}_{B_{\Sigma}})
maps

L^{2}(\Sigma,E)
to
\operatorname{Dom}(D^{\Sigma}_{B_{\Sigma}})=H^{1}(\Sigma,E;B_{\Sigma})
.
In particular,

\mathcal{E}^{\sharp}\varphi(t)=\chi(t)\Phi(tD^{\Sigma}_{B_{\Sigma}})\varphi

belongs to
H^{1}(\Sigma,E;B_{\Sigma})
for every
t>0
.
Since the graph norm of
D^{\Sigma}_{B_{\Sigma}}
is equivalent to the

H^{1}
norm on
H^{1}(\Sigma,E;B_{\Sigma})
, we have

\|u\|_{H^{1}(\Sigma)}^{2}\leq C\left(\|u\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}^{2}+\|D^{\Sigma}_{B_{\Sigma}}u\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}^{2}\right),\qquad u\in H^{1}(\Sigma,E;B_{\Sigma}),

Applying this to
u(t)=\mathcal{E}^{\sharp}\varphi(t)
, we obtain

\int_{0}^{1}\|\nabla^{\Sigma}u(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}^{2}\,dt\leq C\int_{0}^{1}\left(\|u(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}^{2}+\|D^{\Sigma}_{B_{\Sigma}}u(t)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}^{2}\right)\,dt.

These
estimates together imply that

\mathcal{E}^{\sharp}\colon H^{1/2}(\Sigma,E;B_{\Sigma})\longrightarrow H^{1}([0,1]\times\Sigma,E;B_{\mathrm{side}})

is bounded. Here
B_{\mathrm{side}}
denotes the product boundary condition
on the side faces
[0,1]\times\Sigma_{k}
.

Now we consider the general case. Since
\Sigma
is disjoint from codimension three faces of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
, by an argument similar to the codimension two case of Theorem
3.5
, there is a smooth bundle isomorphism
\Psi
from a tubular neighborhood of
\Sigma
in
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
to the direct product space
(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\times\Sigma
(viewed as a subspace of the normal bundle of
\Sigma
) such that
\Psi|_{\Sigma}
equals the identity bundle map,
\Psi
maps the boundary condition
B
for the operator
D
to the boundary condition
\widetilde{B}
for
\not{D}
that is constant along the
\partial_{t}
-direction.

For
\varphi=\sum_{\lambda}a_{\lambda}\varphi_{\lambda}
in
H^{1/2}(\Sigma,E;B_{\Sigma})
, we define the extension map

\mathcal{E}(\varphi)\coloneqq\Psi^{*}\circ\mathcal{E}^{\sharp}\circ\Psi(\varphi)

where
\mathcal{E}^{\sharp}
is the extension map defined using
\not{D}
(subject to the boundary condition
\widetilde{B}
) in the product case above.

Since
\Psi
maps
H^{1}
Sobolev space to
H^{1}
Sobolev space, it follows that
\mathcal{E}
is a bounded linear map
H^{1/2}(\Sigma,E;B_{\Sigma})\to H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{i},E;B_{\mathrm{ext},i})
. Furthermore, since
\Psi|_{\Sigma}
is the identity, we have

\tau\circ\mathcal{E}(\varphi)=\tau\circ\Psi^{*}\circ\mathcal{E}^{\sharp}\circ\Psi(\varphi)=\varphi.

This finishes the proof.
∎

Proposition 4.6
.

Assume the notation of Theorem
4.1
. Consider the disjoint union
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}\sqcup\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{2}
, and let
V\subset E|_{\Sigma}
denote the subbundle over
\Sigma
given by

V=\left\{\sigma\in E|_{\Sigma}:\mathscr{E}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{\Sigma})c(\nu_{\Sigma})\sigma=\sigma\right\}.

Let
Q\colon E|_{\Sigma}\to V
be the orthogonal projection onto
V
, and set
Q^{\perp}=1-Q
. For
s\in[0,1]
, define a family of boundary conditions
B^{s}
on
E
over
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}\sqcup\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{2}
as follows. A pair of sections
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
of
E|_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}}\sqcup E|_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{2}}
satisfies
B^{s}
if:

(i)

sQ(\sigma_{1})=Q(\sigma_{2})
and
Q^{\perp}(\sigma_{1})=sQ^{\perp}(\sigma_{2})
on
\Sigma
;

(ii)

\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}
satisfy the original boundary condition

B
on all codimension-one faces of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{2}
other
than
\Sigma
.

Then the Dirac operator
D
on
E
over
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}\sqcup\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{2}
, subject to the boundary condition
B^{s}
, is essentially self-adjoint and Fredholm.

Proof.

A direct computation shows that
D_{B^{s}}
is formally symmetric. We will prove that
D_{B^{s}}
is locally essentially self-adjoint at every point
x\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
.

By Theorem
3.15
and its proof,
D
is clearly locally essential self-adjoint at any point that does not lie in
\Sigma
.

On
\Sigma
, we reduce the verification of essential self-adjointness to a product case. Consider the orthogonal decompositions

T\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}=\mathbb{R}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{\Sigma})\oplus T\Sigma\textup{ and }f^{\ast}TM=\mathbb{R}(\nu_{\Sigma})\oplus\mathbb{R}(\nu_{\Sigma})^{\perp}.

Consider the bundle
\widetilde{E}\coloneqq S(T\Sigma\oplus\mathbb{R}(\nu_{\Sigma})^{\perp})\otimes S(\mathbb{R}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{\Sigma})\oplus\mathbb{R}(\nu_{\Sigma}))
over the product space
(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\times\Sigma
obtained by trivially extending the bundle
E
over
\{0\}\times\Sigma
along the interval direction
(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)
. The boundary condition
B
for codimension one face
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
restricts to a boundary condition
B_{\Sigma}
along the codimension one face
\Sigma_{k}=\Sigma\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
of
\Sigma
. We extend
B_{\Sigma}
constantly along the interval direction to obtain a boundary condition
\widetilde{B}
along the codimension one face
(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\times\Sigma_{k}
of
(-\varepsilon,\varepsilon)\times\Sigma
.

On the disjoint union
(-\varepsilon,0]\times\Sigma\sqcup[0,\varepsilon)\times\Sigma
, we say a pair of sections
(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})
of
\widetilde{E}|_{(-\varepsilon,0]\times\Sigma}\sqcup\widetilde{E}|_{[0,\varepsilon)\times\Sigma}
satisfies
\widetilde{B}^{s}
if
sQ(\sigma_{1})=Q(\sigma_{2})
and
Q^{\perp}(\sigma_{1})=sQ^{\perp}(\sigma_{2})
on
\Sigma
.

By an argument similar to the codimension-two case of Theorem
3.5
, there exist a tubular neighborhood
U_{1}
(resp.
U_{2}
) of
\Sigma
in
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}
(resp.
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{2}
) and a smooth bundle isomorphism from the spinor bundle
\widetilde{E}=S(T\Sigma\oplus R(\nu_{\Sigma})^{\perp})\otimes S(\mathbb{R}\oplus\mathbb{R})
on the product space
(-\varepsilon,0]\times\Sigma
(resp.
[0,\varepsilon)\times\Sigma
) to the spinor bundle
E
over
U_{1}
(resp.
U_{2}
) such that

(1)

Restricted to
\Sigma
,
\Psi
equals the identity bundle map:

\Psi|_{\Sigma}=\operatorname{id}:E|_{\Sigma}\to\widetilde{E}|_{\{0\}\times\Sigma};

(2)

\Psi
maps the codimension one faces of
(-\varepsilon,0]\times\Sigma
(resp.
[0,\varepsilon)\times\Sigma
) to the corresponding codimension one faces of
U_{1}
(resp.
U_{2}
), and the boundary condition
\widetilde{B}^{s}
for sections of
\widetilde{E}
to the boundary condition
B^{s}
for sections of
E
;

(3)

and
\Psi^{*}D\Psi=\not{D}+\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{B}

where

\not{D}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\partial_{t})\frac{\partial}{\partial t}+D^{\Sigma},

and
\mathcal{A}
is a first-order differential operator whose coefficients are smooth and vanish on
\{0\}\times\Sigma
, and
\mathcal{B}
is a smooth zeroth-order operator.

Similar to the proof of Theorem
3.15
, by applying the Kato-Rellich perturbation theorem, it suffices to show that
\not{D}
subject to the boundary condition
\widetilde{B}^{s}
is essentially self-adjoint on
((-\infty,0]\times\Sigma)\sqcup([0,\infty)\times\Sigma)
.

To verify the essential self-adjointness of
\not{D}_{\widetilde{B}^{s}}
, it suffices to show that the deficiency spaces

\mathcal{N}_{\pm}\coloneqq\left\{\sigma\in\operatorname{Dom}_{\max}(\not{D}_{B^{s}})\mid\not{D}\sigma=\pm i\sigma\right\}

are zero. We give the
argument for
\mathcal{N}_{+}
; the proof for
\mathcal{N}_{-}
is identical.

Let
\sigma=(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\in\mathcal{N}_{+}
, that is,

(4.4)

\not{D}\sigma_{k}-i\sigma_{k}=0.

Applying
(\not{D}+i)
to the above equation yields

(-\partial_{t}^{2}+(D^{\Sigma})^{2}+1)\sigma_{k}=0.

The
L^{2}
condition on the half-cylinders implies

\sigma_{1}(t)=e^{tA}\varphi_{1}\quad(t\leq 0),\textup{ and }\sigma_{2}(t)=e^{-tA}\varphi_{2}\quad(t\geq 0),

where
A=\sqrt{(D^{\Sigma})^{2}+1}
and
\varphi_{k}\in L^{2}(\Sigma,\widetilde{E})
. Substituting
these expressions into equation (
4.4
) gives

(4.5)

(JA+D^{\Sigma})\varphi_{1}=i\varphi_{1}\textup{ and }(-JA+D^{\Sigma})\varphi_{2}=i\varphi_{2}.

where
J=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\partial_{t})
.
Decompose

\varphi_{k}=a_{k}+b_{k},\qquad a_{k}\in V,\quad b_{k}\in V^{\perp}.

Since
D^{\Sigma}
and
A
commute with
12
12

12

Under the identification
\Psi\colon E\xrightarrow{\cong}\widetilde{E}
, the operator
\mathscr{E}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{\Sigma})c(\nu_{\Sigma})
becomes
\mathscr{E}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\partial_{t})c(\partial_{t})
.

\mathscr{E}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\partial_{t})c(\partial_{t})
, they preserve
V
and

V^{\perp}
. Since
J
anticommutes with
\mathscr{E}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\partial_{t})c(\partial_{t})
, it interchanges
V
and

V^{\perp}
. The boundary condition
\widetilde{B}^{s}
gives

(4.6)

a_{2}=sa_{1},\qquad b_{1}=sb_{2}.

Taking the
V
-component of the first equation in
(
4.5
), multiplying it by
s
, and using
(
4.6
), gives

s^{2}JAb_{2}+sD^{\Sigma}a_{1}=isa_{1}.

Taking the
V
-component of the second equation in
(
4.5
) gives

-JAb_{2}+sD^{\Sigma}a_{1}=isa_{1}.

Subtracting the two equations yields

(1+s^{2})JAb_{2}=0.

Since
J
is invertible and
A\geq 1
, we get
b_{2}=0
, and hence
b_{1}=0
. Now
the
V^{\perp}
-component of the first equation in
(
4.5
) gives

JAa_{1}=0,

so
a_{1}=0
, and therefore
a_{2}=0
. Hence
\varphi_{1}=\varphi_{2}=0
, and so

\sigma_{1}=\sigma_{2}=0
. Thus
\mathcal{N}_{+}=0
. Similarly,

\mathcal{N}_{-}=0
.

Therefore,
\not{D}_{\widetilde{B}^{s}}
is essentially self-adjoint. By the Kato-Rellich perturbation theorem,
\Psi^{*}D\Psi=\not{D}+\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{B}
, subject to boundary condition
\widetilde{B}^{s}
, is essentially self-adjoint; consequently, so is
D_{B^{s}}
. The domain of the self-adjoint closure of
D_{B^{s}}
is thus
H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}\sqcup\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{2},E;B^{s})
, from which it follows that
D_{B^{s}}
is Fredholm.
∎

Now let us prove Theorem
4.1
.

Proof of Theorem
4.1
.

By Proposition
4.6
, for each
s\in[0,1]
, we have a Fredholm operator

D_{B^{s}}\colon H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}\sqcup\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{2},E;B^{s})\to L^{2}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}\sqcup\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{2},E),

where
B^{s}
is the boundary condition defined in Proposition
4.6
.

For
i=1,2
, let
\tau_{i}
be the trace map from
H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{i},E)
to
H^{1/2}(\Sigma,E)
, and
\mathcal{E}_{i}
from
H^{1/2}(\Sigma,E;B_{\Sigma})
to
H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{i},E)
be the extension map as in Lemma
4.5
. For
s,t\in[0,1]
, define the linear map

\displaystyle T_{s,t}\colon H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}\sqcup\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{2},E;B^{s})

\displaystyle\to H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}\sqcup\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{2},E;B^{t})

\displaystyle(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})

\displaystyle\mapsto(\sigma_{1}+(t-s)\mathcal{E}_{1}Q^{\perp}\tau_{2}\sigma_{2},\sigma_{2}+(t-s)\mathcal{E}_{2}Q\tau_{1}\sigma_{1}),

where
Q
is the projection
E|_{\Sigma}\to V
defined in Proposition
4.6
.
Note that
T_{s,t}
is invertible with its inverse given by
T_{t,s}
, and is continuous in both
s
and
t
with respect to the operator norm. Therefore the family of Fredholm operators

D_{B^{s}}\circ T_{1,s}\colon H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}\sqcup\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{2},E;B^{1})\longrightarrow L^{2}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}\sqcup\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{2},E)

depends continuously on
s
in operator norm. By the homotopy invariance of the Fredholm index,
\textup{Ind}(D_{B^{s}}\circ T_{1,s})
is independent of
s
. Since
T_{1,s}
is invertible,

\textup{Ind}(D_{B^{s}}\circ T_{1,s})=\textup{Ind}(D_{B^{s}}).

Hence
\textup{Ind}(D_{B^{s}})
is independent of
s\in[0,1]
.

When
s=1
, the boundary condition
B^{1}
is the matching condition across

\Sigma
, so
D_{B^{1}}
is the original glued operator
D_{B}
. When
s=0
, the boundary condition
B^{0}
becomes

Q(\sigma_{2})=0\textup{ and }Q^{\perp}(\sigma_{1})=0.

Since
V
is the
+1
-eigenspace of

\mathscr{E}\,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{\Sigma})c(\nu_{\Sigma})
, these
are exactly the boundary conditions
B_{1}
on
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}
and
B_{2}
on

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{2}
. Therefore

\textup{Ind}(D_{B^{1}})=\textup{Ind}(D_{B}),\qquad\textup{Ind}(D_{B^{0}})=\textup{Ind}(D^{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}}_{B_{1}})+\textup{Ind}(D^{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{2}}_{B_{2}}).

Since the index is constant in
s
, it follows that

\textup{Ind}(D_{B})=\textup{Ind}(D^{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}}_{B_{1}})+\textup{Ind}(D^{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{2}}_{B_{2}}).

∎

5.
An approximation lemma and its consequences

In this section, we prove an approximation lemma for boundary conditions. The
lemma allows us to pass to the limit from solutions satisfying a family of
approximating boundary conditions
B_{t}
to a limiting solution satisfying the limiting boundary condition

B_{0}
, provided that
B_{0}
is extremal in the sense of Definition
5.1
below.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g})
and
(M,g)
be polyhedral manifolds and
f\colon\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}\to M
a spin polyhedral map. Let
D
be the Dirac operator associated with the spinor bundle
E=S(T\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}\oplus f^{*}TM)
over
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
. Let
B
be the boundary condition defined on each codimension-one face
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
by

\mathscr{E}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k})c(\nu_{k})\sigma=-\sigma,

where
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k}
is the unit inner normal vector of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
and
\nu_{k}
is a Lipschitz unit-length section over
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
with values in
f^{*}TM
. Let
\mathscr{R}
and
\mathscr{A}
be the endomorphisms on
E
that appears in the Stokes formula for
D
(see (
2.7
)):

\int_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}|D\sigma|^{2}=\int_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}|\nabla\sigma|^{2}+\int_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}\langle\mathscr{R}\sigma,\sigma\rangle+\sum_{k}\int_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}}\langle\mathscr{A}\sigma,\sigma\rangle

for
\sigma\in C^{\infty}_{00}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E;B)
. We say that the boundary condition
B
is
extremal
if we have
\mathscr{R}\geq 0
on
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
and
\mathscr{A}\geq 0
on each
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
.

We will use the approximation lemma only in the case where the target bundle is trivial. We therefore state the lemma in that form.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g})
be a
3
-dimensional polyhedral manifold and
D
be the Dirac operator associated with
E=S(T\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}\oplus\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{3})
over
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
, where
\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
is the trivial flat bundle. Consider a family of boundary conditions
\{B_{t}\}_{t\in[0,\varepsilon]}
defined on each codimension-one face
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
by

\mathscr{E}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k})c(\nu_{k,t})\sigma=-\sigma,

where
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k}
is the unit inner normal vector of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
and
\nu_{k,t}
is a Lipschitz section over
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
taking values in
\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
. Assume that
B_{t}
depends continuously on
t
in Lipschitz norm; that is, for each codimension one face
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
, the maps

t\mapsto\nu_{k,t}\textup{ and }t\mapsto\nabla\nu_{k,t}

are continuous with respect to the
L^{\infty}
-norm. If

(1)

B_{0}
is extremal in the sense of Definition
5.1
, and

(2)

for any
t\in(0,\varepsilon]
, there exists a non-zero
\sigma_{t}\in H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E;B_{t})
such that
D\sigma_{t}=0
,

then there exists a non-zero section
\sigma_{0}\in H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E;B_{0})
such that
\nabla\sigma_{0}=0
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, assume that
\|\sigma_{t}\|_{L^{2}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M})}=1
for each
t\in(0,\varepsilon]
. Let
\mathscr{R}_{t}
and
\mathscr{A}_{t}
denote the endomorphisms appearing in the Stokes formula of
D
(see (
2.7
)) for the boundary condition
B_{t}
. Note that
\mathscr{R}_{t}\equiv\mathscr{R}
is independent of
t
. Since
B_{0}
is extremal, we have
\mathscr{R}\geq 0
on
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
and
\mathscr{A}_{0}\geq 0
on
\partial\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
. Moreover, since
B_{t}\to B_{0}
in Lipschitz norm,
\mathscr{A}_{t}
converges to
\mathscr{A}_{0}
in
L^{\infty}
norm as
t\to 0
. Hence there exist constants
\alpha_{t}\geq 0
, with
\alpha_{t}\to 0
as

t\to 0
, such that,

\langle\mathscr{A}_{t}\sigma(x),\sigma(x)\rangle\geq-\alpha_{t}|\sigma(x)|^{2}\quad\text{for a.e. }x\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}\textup{ and }\sigma\in C_{00}^{\infty}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E).

Applying the Stokes formula (see (
2.7
)) and using the fact that
D\sigma_{t}=0
, we have

0=\int_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}|D\sigma_{t}|^{2}=\int_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}|\nabla\sigma_{t}|^{2}+\int_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}\langle\mathscr{R}\sigma_{t},\sigma_{t}\rangle+\int_{\partial\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}\langle\mathscr{A}_{t}\sigma_{t},\sigma_{t}\rangle.

Using
\mathscr{R}\geq 0
and the lower bound for
\mathscr{A}_{t}
, we obtain

\int_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}|\nabla\sigma_{t}|^{2}\leq-\int_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}\langle\mathscr{R}\sigma_{t},\sigma_{t}\rangle-\int_{\partial\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}\langle\mathscr{A}_{t}\sigma_{t},\sigma_{t}\rangle\leq\alpha_{t}\int_{\partial\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}|\sigma_{t}|^{2}.

Let
\tau\colon H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E)\to L^{2}(\partial\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E)
be the trace operator. Since
\tau
is bounded, there exists a constant
C>0
such that
\|\tau\sigma\|_{L^{2}(\partial\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M})}\leq C\|\sigma\|_{H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M})}
for all
\sigma\in H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E)
. Therefore

\|\nabla\sigma_{t}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M})}\leq\alpha_{t}\|\tau\sigma_{t}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\partial\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M})}\leq C^{2}\alpha_{t}\|\sigma_{t}\|^{2}_{H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M})}=C^{2}\alpha_{t}(\|\sigma_{t}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M})}+\|\nabla\sigma_{t}\|_{L^{2}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M})}^{2}).

Rearranging the terms yields

(1-C^{2}\alpha_{t})\|\nabla\sigma_{t}\|_{L^{2}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M})}^{2}\leq C^{2}\alpha_{t}\|\sigma_{t}\|_{L^{2}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M})}^{2}.

For sufficiently small
t
, we have
1-C^{2}\alpha_{t}>0
. Since
\|\sigma_{t}\|_{L^{2}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M})}=1
, it follows that

\|\nabla\sigma_{t}\|_{L^{2}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M})}^{2}\leq\frac{C^{2}\alpha_{t}}{1-C^{2}\alpha_{t}}.

This implies that
\|\nabla\sigma_{t}\|_{L^{2}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M})}\to 0
as
t\to 0
.

The family
\{\sigma_{t}\}
is bounded in
H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E)
. By the Rellich compact embedding theorem, there exists a sequence
\{\sigma_{t_{n}}\}
that converges strongly in
L^{2}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E)
to some limit
\sigma_{0}
, as
t_{n}\to 0
. Furthermore, since
\|\nabla\sigma_{t_{n}}\|\to 0
, the sequence
\{\sigma_{t_{n}}\}
is a Cauchy sequence in
H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E)
, and consequently converges strongly to
\sigma_{0}
in
H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E)
.
In particular, we have
\|\sigma_{0}\|=1
(so
\sigma_{0}\neq 0
) and
\nabla\sigma_{0}=0
.

Finally, by the boundedness of the trace operator and the convergence of the boundary conditions
B_{t}\to B_{0}
, the limit section
\sigma_{0}
satisfies the boundary condition
B_{0}
.
∎

We have the following immediate corollary of Lemma
5.2
.

Corollary 5.3
.

Let
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g})
be a
3
-dimensional polyhedral manifold and
D
be the Dirac operator associated with
E=S(T\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}\oplus\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{3})
over
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
. Consider a family of boundary conditions
\{B_{t}\}_{t\in[0,\varepsilon]}
defined on each codimension-one face
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
by

\mathscr{E}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k})c(\nu_{k,t})\sigma=-\sigma,

where
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k}
is the unit inner normal vector of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
and
\nu_{k,t}
is a Lipschitz section over
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
taking values in the trivial bundle
\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
. Assume that
B_{t}
depends continuously on
t
in Lipschitz norm. If

(1)

B_{0}
is extremal in the sense of Definition
5.1
, and

(2)

there is no non-zero solution
\sigma\in H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E;B_{0})
of
D\sigma=0
,

then there exists
\delta>0
such that for all
t\in[0,\delta]
,
D
subject to the boundary condition
B_{t}
does not admit a nonzero solution in
H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E;B_{t})
.

We emphasize that in Lemma
5.2
and Corollary
5.3
, the operator
D
subject to the boundary condition
B_{t}
is
not
required to be essentially self-adjoint. In the
applications below,
D
with boundary condition
B_{t}
will typically be
essentially self-adjoint for
t>0
, but essential self-adjointness of
D
with boundary condition
B_{0}
may be unknown and, in any case, is not needed.

The following lemma and its corollary provide concrete examples of extremal boundary conditions for which the associated Dirac operator is invertible.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
\mathbb{G}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a three dimensional compact convex polyhedral manifold. Assume that the boundary of
\mathbb{G}
consists of codimension one
faces

F_{0},F_{1},\ldots,F_{K},

where the second fundamental form of each face is
nonnegative,
F_{0}
meets all other faces orthogonally, and moreover the second fundamental form of
F_{0}
is strictly positive in
a neighborhood of some point
x_{0}\in F_{0}
. Let
B_{\textup{mix}}
be the boundary condition on
\mathord{\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{$\bigwedge$}}}}}}}^{*}T\mathbb{G}
given by

\mathscr{E}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\nu_{k})c(\nu_{k})\omega=-\omega\quad\textup{on }F_{k}\textup{ for }k>0,

and

\mathscr{E}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\nu_{0})c(\nu_{0})\omega=\omega\quad\textup{on }F_{0},

where
\nu_{k}
is the unit inner normal vector field of
F_{k}
for
k\geq 0
. Then
B_{\textup{mix}}
is extremal, and the de Rham operator
D^{\mathrm{dR}}
subject to
B_{\textup{mix}}
is essentially self-adjoint and invertible. In particular, there is no non-zero parallel section in
H^{1}(\mathbb{G},\mathord{\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{$\bigwedge$}}}}}}}^{*}T\mathbb{G};B_{\textup{mix}})
.

Proof.

It is clear that the inner-product comparison conditions as in Theorem
3.15
are satisfied. The essential self-adjointness of
D^{\mathrm{dR}}
follows from Theorem
3.15
. Since
\mathbb{G}
is flat and all faces have nonnegative second fundamental form,
B_{\textup{mix}}
is extremal by Lemma
2.15
and Proposition
2.16
.

Let
\omega\in H^{1}(\mathbb{G},\mathord{\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{$\bigwedge$}}}}}}}^{*}T\mathbb{G};B_{\textup{mix}})
satisfy
D^{\mathrm{dR}}\omega=0
. It follows from Proposition
2.16
that
\nabla\omega=0
. Thus
\omega
is a parallel, hence constant, differential form. On
F_{0}
, the boundary condition gives

\mathscr{E}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\nu_{0})c(\nu_{0})\omega=\omega,

equivalently,

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\nu_{0})\omega=\mathscr{E}c(\nu_{0})\omega.

Since the second fundamental form of
F_{0}
is strictly positive near
x_{0}
, the Gauss map of
F_{0}
has image containing an open subset of the unit sphere.
Thus the preceding identity holds for all unit vectors in an open subset of

\mathbb{S}^{2}
. By linearity, it follows that

(5.1)

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(v)\omega=\mathscr{E}\,c(v)\omega

for every
v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
.

On the other hand, on any face
F_{k}
with
k>0
, the boundary condition is

\mathscr{E}\,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\nu_{k})c(\nu_{k})\omega=-\omega,

which is equivalent to

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\nu_{k})\omega=-\mathscr{E}\,c(\nu_{k})\omega.

Combining this with the identity (
5.1
) with
v=\nu_{k}
gives

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\nu_{k})\omega=0.

Since the operator
c(\nu_{k})
is invertible, it follows that

\omega=0
.

Thus the kernel of
D^{\mathrm{dR}}_{B_{\mathrm{mix}}}
is trivial. Since the operator
is self-adjoint and Fredholm, it is invertible.
∎

Example 5.5
.

Let
\mathcal{C}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
be a closed polyhedral cone with its vertex at the origin, bounded by planes
H_{1},\dots,H_{\ell}
passing through the origin. For each plane
H_{j}
, let
\nu_{j}
denote the unit inner normal vector. Let

w_{1},\ldots,w_{J}
be unit vectors along the edges of the cone, pointing away
from the origin.

We construct a smooth hypersurface
\Sigma\subset\mathcal{C}
with the following conditions:

(1)

the second fundamental form of
\Sigma
is nonnegative everywhere;

(2)

\Sigma
is strictly convex away from neighborhoods of the edges of

\mathcal{C}
;

(3)

near each edge,
\Sigma
coincides with a flat plane;

(4)

\Sigma
intersects each boundary face
H_{j}
orthogonally.

Choose
0<a\ll b\ll 1
and let
m_{a}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be smooth and nonneaative with

(i)

m_{a}(t)=0
for
t\leq-a
,

(ii)

m_{a}(t)=t
for
t\geq a
,

(iii)

0\leq m_{a}^{\prime}(t)\leq 1
for all
t\in\mathbb{R},

(iv)

m_{a}^{\prime\prime}(t)>0
for
t\in(-a,a)
.

For each edge vector
w_{i}
, we define a linear function
L_{i}\colon\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}
:

L_{i}(x)=\frac{x\cdot w_{i}}{1-b}.

Define a function
\varphi\colon\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}
:

\varphi(x)=\|x\|+\sum_{i=1}^{J}m_{a}\big(L_{i}(x)-\|x\|\big).

Set

\Sigma\coloneqq\{x\in\mathcal{C}\mid\varphi(x)=1\}

and

\mathcal{R}_{i}=\{x\in\Sigma\mid L_{i}(x)-\|x\|\geq-a\}.

We may choose
0<a\ll b\ll 1
so that the sets
\mathcal{R}_{i}
are pairwise disjoint and so that each
\mathcal{R}_{i}
meets only those faces which contain
w_{i}
. Then near any point
x\in\Sigma
, at most one summand in the summation
\sum_{i=1}^{J}m_{a}\big(L_{i}(x)-\|x\|\big)
is non-zero.
Now a direct computation shows that, for sufficiently small
a
and
b
, the hypersurface
\Sigma
satisfies the required properties (1) through (4).

We have the following immediate consequence of Lemma
5.4
.

Corollary 5.6
.

Let
\mathbb{F}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
be the compact convex polyhedral manifold bounded by the planes
H_{1},\cdots,H_{\ell}
and
\Sigma
as in Example
5.5
. Let
\nu_{j}
be the
unit inner normal to
H_{j}
, and let
\nu_{0}
be the unit inner normal to
\Sigma
.
Consider the boundary condition
B_{\mathrm{mix}}
on

\mathord{\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{$\bigwedge$}}}}}}}^{*}T\mathbb{F}
given by

\mathscr{E}\,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\nu_{j})c(\nu_{j})\omega=-\omega\qquad\text{on }H_{j},

and

\mathscr{E}\,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\nu_{0})c(\nu_{0})\omega=\omega\qquad\text{on }\Sigma.

Then
B_{\textup{mix}}
is extremal, and the de Rham operator
D^{\mathrm{dR}}
subject to
B_{\textup{mix}}
is essentially self-adjoint and invertible. In particular, there is no non-zero parallel section in
H^{1}(\mathbb{F},\mathord{\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\resizebox{}{}{{\hbox{{$\bigwedge$}}}}}}}^{*}T\mathbb{F};B_{\textup{mix}})
.

6.
An index theorem for manifolds with polyhedral boundary

In this section, we prove our main theorem, Theorem
1.2
. As
explained in the introduction, we do not work directly with the Dirac operator

D_{B}
arising from the geometric assumptions of Theorem
1.2
.
Instead, we consider a sequence of Dirac operators with approximating boundary
conditions. We show that each approximating operator is Fredholm with nonzero
Fredholm index, and hence has a nontrivial kernel. We then apply the
approximation lemma, Lemma
5.2
, to obtain a nontrivial
solution for the limiting boundary condition
B
. Finally, applying
Lemma
2.17
to this limiting solution completes the proof
of Theorem
1.2
.

6.1.
An algebraic angle enlarging lemma

Our strategy for computing the Fredholm index combines continuous deformation
with the gluing formula from Section
4
. One of the main
difficulties is to determine whether the polyhedral manifolds in
Theorem
1.2
admit a deformation that preserves the dihedral-angle
comparison, or equivalently the corresponding inner-product comparison. This
preservation is crucial for Fredholmness, in view of
Theorem
3.15
. See also Gromov’s

\angle
-shrinking conjecture
[
18
, Section 7]
.

Theorem
3.15
provides an additional flexibility: the sections
\nu_{k}
appearing in the boundary condition need not be the inward unit
normals of the faces of the target polyhedral manifold
M
. Instead,
they may be prescribed independently as unit vector fields in the relevant
auxiliary bundle, provided that the required inner-product comparisons are
satisfied. This observation removes the geometric and combinatorial
restrictions imposed by actual polyhedral deformations and reduces the problem
to an algebraic deformation theorem for vector fields, as stated below.

Remark 6.1
.

The main reason for using the algebraic deformation approach, namely
Lemma
6.2
and
Proposition
6.3
, is that Gromov’s

\angle
-shrinking conjecture remains open in higher dimensions. Since the
present paper focuses on the three-dimensional case, one could instead use a
more geometric deformation argument, taking advantage of the fact that
Gromov’s
\angle
-shrinking conjecture is known in dimension three; see

[
31
, Appendix B]
. However, our aim is also to highlight several
of the key ingredients in our proof
[
31
]
of Gromov’s dihedral
rigidity conjecture in arbitrary dimensions. For this reason, we use the
algebraic deformation framework here, which is closer to the higher-dimensional
strategy.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
\{v_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq q}\subset\mathbb{S}^{n}
be a set of distinct points
13
13

13

In typical geometric settings, each
v_{i}
arises as the unit inner normal vector of a hyperplane in
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
.
in the open upper hemisphere, and let
\mathbf{n}
denote the north pole. Then, there exists a family of continuous paths
\{v_{i}(t)\}_{1\leq i\leq q}
for
t\in[0,1]
in the open upper hemisphere such that:

•

v_{i}(0)=v_{i}
and
v_{i}(1)=\mathbf{n}
for all
i
,

•

\langle v_{i}(t),\mathbf{n}\rangle>0
for all
i
and
t\in[0,1]
,

•

\langle v_{i}(t),v_{j}(t)\rangle
is non-decreasing with respect to
t
for all
i,j
, and

•

\langle v_{i}(t),v_{j}(t)\rangle>\langle v_{i},v_{j}\rangle
for all
i\neq j
and
t\in(0,1]
.

Proof.

Let

\theta_{i}=\mathrm{dist}(v_{i},\mathbf{n})\in[0,\pi/2).

For each
i
, let
\gamma_{i}:[0,\theta_{i}]\to\mathbb{S}^{n}
be the unit-speed geodesic from
\mathbf{n}
to
v_{i}
, so that

\gamma_{i}(0)=\mathbf{n},\qquad\gamma_{i}(\theta_{i})=v_{i}.

Define

v_{i}(t)=\gamma_{i}((1-t)\theta_{i}),\qquad t\in[0,1].

Then
v_{i}(0)=v_{i}
,
v_{i}(1)=\mathbf{n}
, and

\langle v_{i}(t),\mathbf{n}\rangle=\cos((1-t)\theta_{i})>0,

so each path stays in the open upper hemisphere. It remains to prove the monotonicity of the pairwise inner products. The case where
i=j
is trivial. Now fix

i\neq j
. If one of the two points is already
\mathbf{n}
, say

v_{i}=\mathbf{n}
, then
v_{i}(t)=\mathbf{n}
, and

\langle v_{i}(t),v_{j}(t)\rangle=\cos((1-t)\theta_{j}),

which is non-decreasing in
t
, and is strictly larger than

\cos\theta_{j}=\langle v_{i},v_{j}\rangle
for every
t>0
, since

\theta_{j}>0
.

Now assume
\theta_{i},\theta_{j}>0
. Let
\phi\in[0,\pi]
be the angle at

\mathbf{n}
between the two geodesics from
\mathbf{n}
to
v_{i}
and
v_{j}
.
Put
\lambda=1-t
. By the spherical law of cosines,

F(\lambda):=\langle v_{i}(t),v_{j}(t)\rangle=\cos(\lambda\theta_{i})\cos(\lambda\theta_{j})+\sin(\lambda\theta_{i})\sin(\lambda\theta_{j})\cos\phi.

We show that
F(\lambda)
is strictly decreasing for
\lambda\in(0,1]
, which is equivalent
to proving
\langle v_{i}(t),v_{j}(t)\rangle
is strictly increasing for
t\in[0,1)
.

Differentiating gives

\displaystyle F^{\prime}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=-(\theta_{i}-\theta_{j})\sin(\lambda(\theta_{i}-\theta_{j}))

\displaystyle-(1-\cos\phi)\left[\theta_{i}\cos(\lambda\theta_{i})\sin(\lambda\theta_{j})+\theta_{j}\sin(\lambda\theta_{i})\cos(\lambda\theta_{j})\right].

Since
\theta_{i},\theta_{j}\in[0,\pi/2)
, we have

(\theta_{i}-\theta_{j})\sin(\lambda(\theta_{i}-\theta_{j}))\geq 0.

Also
1-\cos\phi\geq 0
, and the term in the brackets is nonnegative. Hence

F^{\prime}(\lambda)\leq 0.

Moreover, for
\lambda\in(0,1]
, equality can occur only if
\theta_{i}=\theta_{j}
and

\phi=0
, which would imply
v_{i}=v_{j}
, contradicting the assumption that
the points are distinct. Therefore
F^{\prime}(\lambda)<0
for
\lambda\in(0,1]
.

Thus, for every
t\in(0,1)
,

\langle v_{i}(t),v_{j}(t)\rangle>\langle v_{i},v_{j}\rangle.

At
t=1
, we have

\langle v_{i}(1),v_{j}(1)\rangle=1>\langle v_{i},v_{j}\rangle,

since
v_{i}\neq v_{j}
. This proves both the monotonicity and the strict
inequality.
∎

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g})
be a three-dimensional polyhedral manifold. Let
\mathbf{n}
be a smooth
14
14

14

More precisely,
\mathbf{n}
is the restriction to
\partial\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
of a smooth section defined on a tubular neighborhood of
\partial\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
.
unit length section of the trivial bundle
\mathbb{R}^{3}
on
\partial\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
. For each codimension one face
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
, let
\nu_{k}
be a smooth unit length section of the trivial bundle
\mathbb{R}^{3}
on
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
such that
\langle\mathbf{n},\nu_{k}\rangle>0.

Assume moreover that, for every
i\neq j
and every
x\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{i}\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{j}
, one has

\nu_{i}(x)\neq\nu_{j}(x).

Then there exists a smooth family
\nu_{k,t}
of unit length sections of the trivial bundle
\mathbb{R}^{3}
on
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
, with
t\in[0,1]
, such that

•

\nu_{k,0}=\nu_{k}
and
\nu_{k,1}=\mathbf{n}
;

•

\langle\nu_{k,t},\mathbf{n}\rangle>0
for all
t\in[0,1]
and every
k
;

•

along
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{i}\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{j}
, the function
t\mapsto\langle\nu_{i,t},\nu_{j,t}\rangle

is non-decreasing, and

\langle\nu_{i,t},\nu_{j,t}\rangle>\langle\nu_{i},\nu_{j}\rangle

for every
i\neq j
and every
t\in(0,1]
.

Proof.

For each
x\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
, let

\theta_{k}(x)=\mathrm{dist}_{\mathbb{S}^{2}}(\nu_{k}(x),\mathbf{n}(x)).

Since
\langle\nu_{k}(x),\mathbf{n}(x)\rangle>0
, we have
\theta_{k}(x)<\pi/2
. Let
\eta_{k}(x,s)
be the unit-speed minimizing geodesic in
\mathbb{S}^{2}
from
\nu_{k}(x)
to
\mathbf{n}(x)
, with
0\leq s\leq\theta_{k}(x).

Define

\nu_{k,t}(x)=\eta_{k}(x,t\theta_{k}(x)).

Hence
\nu_{k,t}
is a smooth family of unit length sections with
\nu_{k,0}=\nu_{k}
and
\nu_{k,1}=\mathbf{n}.

The proposition now follows from Lemma
6.2
.
∎

6.2.
Index theorem for polyhedral manifolds

In this subsection, we prove the index theorem for three-dimensional
polyhedral manifolds. We isolate the index calculation from the scalar
curvature and mean curvature hypotheses used later in the rigidity argument.
The curvature assumptions will enter only after the index theorem has produced
a nonzero harmonic spinor.

Let
(M,g)
be a compact convex polyhedron in Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, and let
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},\overline{g})
be a spin polyhedral manifold. Let
f\colon\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}\to M
be a polyhedral map of nonzero degree. We assume that the dihedral angles of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
and
M
satisfy

\theta_{ij}(\overline{g})\leq f^{*}\theta_{ij}(g)\quad\text{on }\overline{F}_{ij}=\overline{F}_{i}\cap\overline{F}_{j},

for each pair of adjacent codimension-one faces
\overline{F}_{i}
and
\overline{F}_{j}
of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
.

We will use the following auxiliary vector field. Choose a point

p_{0}\in\operatorname{int}(M)
and define

(6.1)

\mathbf{n}(y)=\frac{p_{0}-y}{\|p_{0}-y\|},\qquad y\in\partial M.

Since
M
is convex,
\mathbf{n}
is a smooth unit vector field on each
face of
\partial M
and satisfies

\langle\mathbf{n},\nu_{k}\rangle>0

on every codimension-one face
F_{k}
of
M
, where
\nu_{k}
is the unit inner normal vector field of
F_{k}
.

By Proposition
6.3
,

\nu_{k}
can be deformed through unit vector fields
\nu_{k,t}
,

t\in[0,1]
, with
\nu_{k,0}=\nu_{k}
and
\nu_{k,1}=\mathbf{n}
,
such that the inner products
\langle\nu_{i,t},\nu_{j,t}\rangle
are
non-decreasing in
t
. Moreover, for every

t>0
,

\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{i},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{j}\rangle<f^{*}\langle\nu_{i,t},\nu_{j,t}\rangle

on each edge of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
.

In what follows, we replace the original normals
\nu_{k}
by the deformed vector fields
\nu_{k,t}
. This leads us to formulate the following theorem.

Theorem 6.4
.

Let
(M,g)
be a compact convex polyhedron in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
with
g
the Euclidean metric, and let
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},\overline{g})
be a spin polyhedral manifold. Let
f\colon\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}\to M
be a polyhedral map of nonzero degree. Set

E=S(T\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}\oplus f^{*}TM)\simeq S(T\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}\oplus\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{3}),

where
\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
is the trivial flat bundle,
and let
D
be the associated Dirac operator.

Assume that all dihedral angles of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
are less than
\pi
. For each
codimension-one face
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
, let
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k}
be
its unit inner normal and
\nu_{k}
a smooth unit length section of the trivial
bundle
\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
over
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
. Let
\mathbf{n}
be the vector field over
\partial M
in (
6.1
). Assume that

\langle f^{*}\mathbf{n},\nu_{k}\rangle>0\qquad\text{on }\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k},

where
f^{*}\mathbf{n}
is the pull-back of
\mathbf{n}
, viewed as a unit-length section of
\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
over
\partial\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
.
Let
B
be the boundary condition

\mathscr{E}\,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k})c(\nu_{k})\sigma=-\sigma\qquad\text{on }\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}.

Assume that, for every adjacent pair
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{i},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{j}
, we have

(6.2)

\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{i},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{j}\rangle<\langle\nu_{i},\nu_{j}\rangle

on each connected component of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{i}\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{j}
. Then
D
with
boundary condition
B
is essentially self-adjoint and Fredholm. Moreover,

\mathrm{ind}(D_{B})=\deg(f),

where
\deg(f)
denotes the degree of
f
.

Proof.

The essential self-adjointness and Fredholmness of
D_{B}
follows from Theorem
3.15
. So it remains to compute the Fredholm index of
D_{B}
. The proof is
by a sequence of index-preserving deformations and cut-and-paste operations,
reducing the problem to the classical smooth-boundary index theorem.

Step 1. Deforming the vector fields
\nu_{k}
to
\mathbf{n}
.

Let
\mathbf{n}
be the smooth unit vector field over
\partial M
in (
6.1
).
For simplicity, we still denote its pull-back via
f
by
\mathbf{n}
, which is a unit-length section of the trivial bundle
\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
over
\partial\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
.

By Proposition
6.3
, there are smooth unit-length sections
\nu_{k,t}
,
t\in[0,1]
, of the trivial bundle
\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
over
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
such that

\nu_{k,0}=\nu_{k},\qquad\nu_{k,1}=\mathbf{n}\quad\textup{ and }\quad\langle\nu_{k,t},\mathbf{n}\rangle>0.

Let
B_{t}
be the boundary condition

\mathscr{E}\,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k})c(\nu_{k,t})\sigma=-\sigma\qquad\text{on }\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}.

The assumption (
6.2
) and the monotonicity from Proposition
6.3
imply that, for every adjacent pair of codimension-one faces
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{i}
and

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{j}
,

\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{i},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{j}\rangle<\langle\nu_{i,t},\nu_{j,t}\rangle

along
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{i}\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{j}
for all
t\in[0,1]
. Hence
D_{B_{t}}
is essentially self-adjoint and Fredholm
for all
t\in[0,1]
by Theorem
3.15
.

We now prove that the Fredholm index is independent of
t
. The idea is to
construct a norm-continuous family of bundle isomorphisms

\Theta_{t}\colon E\longrightarrow E

such that
\Theta_{t}
maps the boundary condition
B_{0}
to
B_{t}
.

We first construct local isomorphisms mapping
B_{s}
to
B_{t}
, for
s,t
close
to each other. The construction is the same as the construction of the
bundle isomorphism in the proof of Theorem
3.5
.

(1)

At interior points of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
, we take the local isomorphism to be
the identity.

(2)

At points in the interior of a codimension-one face, we choose a
local unitary bundle map carrying the subbundle determined by
B_{s}
to
the corresponding subbundle determined by
B_{t}
, and carrying the
orthogonal complement to the orthogonal complement.

(3)

At points in the interior of an edge

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{i}\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{j}
, we use the explicit construction from the
codimension-two case in the proof of Theorem
3.5
, using the fact that
the inner product comparison is strict for all
t\in[0,1]
.

(4)

At a vertex, we use the same partition-of-unity construction as in
the codimension-three case of Theorem
3.5
. Namely, we use an open cover
\{W_{\alpha}\}
of the link and a subordinate partition of unity
\{\rho_{\alpha}\}
to define the local bundle isomorphism near a vertex by:

\Theta_{s,t}^{\mathrm{loc}}(z)=\sum_{\alpha}\rho_{\alpha}(\sigma)\Theta_{\alpha,s,t}(z),

where
\Theta_{\alpha,s,t}
is a local bundle isomorphism constructed as in the codimension two case. See equation (
3.9
) and its construction for more details.

To pass from local maps to a bundle isomorphism over
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
, we use a partition of unity
subordinate to a finite open cover of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
. Since the local maps are
uniformly close to the identity whenever
s
and
t
are sufficiently close,
a linear combination remains pointwise invertible. Thus, after
subdividing
[0,1]
into sufficiently small intervals

0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\cdots<t_{N}=1,

we obtain global bundle isomorphisms

\Theta_{s,t}\colon E\longrightarrow E

for
s,t
in the same subinterval, with
\Theta_{s,t}
mapping
B_{s}
to

B_{t}
. For
t\in[t_{\ell},t_{\ell+1}]
, define

\Theta_{t}=\Theta_{t_{\ell},t}\circ\Theta_{t_{\ell-1},t_{\ell}}\circ\cdots\circ\Theta_{t_{0},t_{1}}.

Then
\Theta_{t}
maps
B_{0}
to
B_{t}
.

We next record the Sobolev estimates needed below. Away from the vertices of

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
,
\Theta_{t}
is smooth and depends smoothly on
t
. Near a vertex

x
, write
z=r\sigma
in polar coordinates centered at
x
, where
r=\mathrm{dist}(z,x)
. In the local
construction above,

\Theta_{s,t}^{\mathrm{loc}}(z)=\sum_{\alpha}\rho_{\alpha}(\sigma)\Theta_{\alpha,s,t}(z).

Since
|\nabla\rho_{\alpha}(\sigma)|\leq Cr^{-1}

and the maps
\Theta_{\alpha,s,t}
depend smoothly on
s
and
t
, we
obtain, for
s,t
in the same small subinterval,

(6.3)

|\Theta_{s,t}(z)-I|\leq C|s-t|,\qquad|\nabla\Theta_{s,t}(z)|\leq C|s-t|\,r^{-1}

near every vertex. More generally, for the composed family
\Theta_{t}
, we have

|\nabla\Theta_{t}(z)|\leq C^{\prime}r^{-1}

near each vertex.

By Hardy’s inequality, Lemma
3.17
, multiplication by

r^{-1}
maps
H^{1}
boundedly into
L^{2}
in dimension three. Therefore
multiplication by
\nabla\Theta_{t}
defines a bounded map

\nabla\Theta_{t}\colon H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E)\longrightarrow L^{2}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E).

Using the product rule
\nabla(\Theta_{t}\sigma)=(\nabla\Theta_{t})\sigma+\Theta_{t}\nabla\sigma
,
we conclude that

\Theta_{t}\colon H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E;B_{0})\longrightarrow H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E;B_{t})

is bounded. The same argument applied to
\Theta_{t}^{-1}
shows that

\Theta_{t}
is a bounded isomorphism between these Sobolev spaces.

Moreover, (
6.3
) gives, for
s,t
in the same small
subinterval,

\|(\Theta_{t}-\Theta_{s})\sigma\|_{H^{1}}\leq C|t-s|\,\|\sigma\|_{H^{1}},\qquad\sigma\in H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E;B_{0}).

Since the interval
[0,1]
is covered by finitely many such subintervals, the
family
\Theta_{t}
is continuous in operator norm as a map

H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E;B_{0})\longrightarrow H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E).

Define

\mathcal{D}_{t}=D_{B_{t}}\circ\Theta_{t}\colon H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E;B_{0})\longrightarrow L^{2}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E).

For
\sigma\in H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E;B_{0})
, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{D}_{t}\sigma-\mathcal{D}_{s}\sigma\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=\|D((\Theta_{t}-\Theta_{s})\sigma)\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq C\|(\Theta_{t}-\Theta_{s})\sigma\|_{H^{1}}.

Hence
\mathcal{D}_{t}
is a norm-continuous family of Fredholm operators on the
fixed domain
H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E;B_{0})
. By homotopy invariance of the Fredholm
index,
\textup{Ind}(\mathcal{D}_{t})

is independent of
t
. Since
\Theta_{t}
is an isomorphism,

\textup{Ind}(\mathcal{D}_{t})=\textup{Ind}(D_{B_{t}}).

Therefore

\textup{Ind}(D_{B_{t}})

is constant for
t\in[0,1]
. We may therefore replace the original auxiliary fields

\nu_{k}
by
\mathbf{n}
without changing the index.

Step 2. Metric deformation near vertices.

We next deform the metric near the vertices. Let
h_{0}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}
. For each
vertex
x
of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
, choose a sufficiently small neighborhood of
x
in
the ambient open manifold
X
and choose a flat metric
h^{x}
on that
neighborhood such that

h^{x}(x)=h_{0}(x).

Using a partition of unity, and choosing the vertex neighborhoods pairwise
disjoint, we glue these local flat metrics to
h_{0}
away from the vertices.
This gives a smooth metric
h_{1}
on
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
which is flat in a small
neighborhood of every vertex and agrees with
h_{0}
away from these
neighborhoods. Define

h_{t}=(1-t)h_{0}+th_{1},\qquad 0\leq t\leq 1.

Let
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k,t}
denote the unit inner normal to
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
with
respect to
h_{t}
. During this step, we have the auxiliary vector field
\nu_{k}=\mathbf{n}
. The corresponding
h_{t}
-dependent boundary condition
B^{h_{t}}
on the spinor bundle
E_{h_{t}}=S(T\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{h_{t}}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{3})
is defined by:

\mathscr{E}\,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k,t})c(\mathbf{n})\sigma=-\sigma\qquad\text{on }\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}.

For every adjacent pair of codimension-one faces
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{i}
and

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{j}
, the faces remain transverse for all
t
. Hence their

h_{t}
-unit inner normals remain distinct, and therefore

\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{i,t},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{j,t}\rangle_{h_{t}}<1=\langle\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n}\rangle.

Thus the strict inner-product comparison in
Theorem
3.15
holds for each
t\in[0,1]
. Consequently,

D^{h_{t}}
with boundary condition
B^{h_{t}}
is essentially self-adjoint and
Fredholm for all
t
.

Because
h_{t}
is a smooth family of metrics, there exists a smooth family of bundle isometries
\Phi_{t}\colon E_{h_{t}}\to E_{h_{0}}
covering the identity map on
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
. Define

\widetilde{D}_{t}\coloneqq\Phi_{t}\circ D^{h_{t}}\circ\Phi_{t}^{-1}

acting on sections of
E_{h_{0}}
. Similarly, the boundary condition
B^{h_{t}}
pulls back to a boundary condition
\widetilde{B}_{t}
on
E_{h_{0}}
. Then
\widetilde{B}_{t}
and the coefficients of
\widetilde{D}_{t}
depend continuously on
t
.

Similar to

Step 1
, we construct bundle isomorphisms
\widetilde{\Theta}_{t}\colon E_{h_{0}}\to E_{h_{0}}
mapping the initial boundary condition
B^{h_{0}}
to the pulled-back boundary condition
\widetilde{B}_{t}
. Moreover, the same argument from
Step 1
shows that
\textup{Ind}(D^{h_{t}}_{B^{h_{t}}})
is independent of
t\in[0,1]
.

Step 3. Flattening the codimension one faces near vertices.

The goal of this step is to flatten the codimension-one faces in small neighborhoods of the vertices of
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},h_{1})
. Let
x
be a vertex of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
. We will deform each codimension-one face
\overline{F}_{j}
passing through
x
to its tangent plane at
x
, within a small neighborhood of
x
. To streamline the discussion, we perform this deformation on one codimension-one face at a time. For each individual deformation, we will show that the index of the associated Dirac operator remains constant. Since there are only finitely many faces and vertices,
performing these deformations successively gives a new polyhedral manifold

(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}^{\prime},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{h})
for which the metric
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{h}
and all
codimension-one faces are flat in sufficiently small neighborhoods of the
vertices.

Recall that
h_{1}
is flat near each vertex. We identify a sufficiently small
neighborhood of
x
with a neighborhood of the origin in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. Let

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{0}
be one codimension-one face passing through
x
, and let

\Sigma
be the corresponding smooth surface near the origin. After a
rotation on
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, we may assume that

T_{0}\Sigma=\{z=0\}.

Thus
\Sigma
is the graph of a smooth function
f(x,y)
satisfying

f(0,0)=0,\qquad\nabla f(0,0)=0.

Choose a smooth cutoff function
\rho
supported in
B_{r_{0}}(0)
and equal to

1
on
B_{r_{0}/2}(0)
, where
B_{r_{0}}(0)
is the ball of radius
r_{0}
centered at the origin. The gradient of
\rho
satisfies
\|\nabla\rho(r)\|\leq C/r
for some constant
C>0
. Define a family of surfaces
\Sigma_{t}
,
t\in[0,1]
, by

(6.4)

\Sigma_{t}=\left\{(x,y,z):z=(1-t\rho(x,y))f(x,y)\right\}.

Then
\Sigma_{0}=\Sigma
, the deformation is supported in
B_{r_{0}}(0)
, and

\Sigma_{1}
agrees with the tangent plane
\{z=0\}
on
B_{r_{0}/2}(0)
. Let us denote the corresponding surface at time
t
under this deformation by
\Sigma_{t}
, and the corresponding new codimension-one face of the manifold by
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{0}^{t}
. Let

{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}^{(t)}
denote the space obtained from
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
by
replacing
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{0}
with the corresponding deformed face

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{0}^{(t)}
.

We first check that
{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}^{(t)}
is a polyhedral manifold in the sense of Definition
2.5
.
A normal to
\Sigma_{t}
is

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{N}_{t}=\bigl(-(1-t\rho)\nabla f+tf\nabla\rho,\,1\bigr).

Since
f=O(r^{2}),\nabla f=O(r)

and

|f\nabla\rho|=O(r)

on the support of
\nabla\rho
, we have

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{N}_{t}=(0,0,1)+O(r)

uniformly in
t
. Therefore, after shrinking
r_{0}
if necessary, the normal to

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{0}^{(t)}
remains linearly independent from the normals to all
adjacent fixed faces along their intersections. At the vertex itself, the
normal is fixed and equal to the normal of
T_{0}\Sigma
. It follows that
{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}^{(t)}
satisfies conditions of Definition
2.5
, hence is a
polyhedral manifold, for every
t\in[0,1]
. Moreover, after shrinking
r_{0}
if necessary,
all dihedral angles of
{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}^{(t)}
also remain less than
\pi
.

To show the invariance of the index of the associated Dirac operator, We construct a
polyhedral
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism

\Phi_{t}\colon{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}^{(1)}\longrightarrow{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}^{(t)}

by adapting the constructions in Examples
3.3
and

3.4
.

We first construct local maps near the vertex
x
(which has been identified with the origin of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
). Let

\Sigma_{t}
be the deformed face, and let
\Sigma_{k}
be an adjacent face that
is kept fixed. We choose Euclidean coordinates near the vertex
x
, with
x
identified
with the origin in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, so that

T_{0}\Sigma_{t}=\Pi_{1}=\{z=0\},\qquad T_{0}\Sigma_{k}=\Pi_{k}=\{y\sin\theta-z\cos\theta=0\}.

Let

g_{t,1}(x,y,z)=z-(1-t\rho(x,y))f(x,y)

be a defining function for
\Sigma_{t}
, and let
g_{k}
be a defining function
for
\Sigma_{k}
. We normalize these defining functions so that

\nabla g_{t,1}(0)=(0,0,1),\qquad\nabla g_{k}(0)=(0,\sin\theta,-\cos\theta).

Define

\widetilde{\Phi}_{t,k}(x,y,z)=\left(x,\,\frac{g_{k}(x,y,z)+g_{t,1}(x,y,z)\cos\theta}{\sin\theta},\,g_{t,1}(x,y,z)\right).

Then
\widetilde{\Phi}_{t,k}
maps
\Sigma_{t}
to
\Pi_{1}
, maps
\Sigma_{k}
to

\Pi_{k}
, and satisfies

d\widetilde{\Phi}_{t,k}|_{0}=I.

Consequently,

\Psi_{t,k}=\widetilde{\Phi}_{t,k}^{-1}\circ\widetilde{\Phi}_{1,k}

is a local
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism which maps the flattened face
\Sigma_{1}
to

\Sigma_{t}
, preserves the fixed face
\Sigma_{k}
, satisfies

d\Psi_{t,k}|_{0}=I,

and depends smoothly on
t
.

For each pair of adjacent faces
\Sigma_{i}
and
\Sigma_{j}
through the vertex,
we define the corresponding local
C^{1}
-diffeomorphism as follows:

(1)

if one of the two faces is the deforming face
\Sigma_{1}
and the
other is a fixed face
\Sigma_{k}
, we use the map
\Psi_{t,k};

(2)

if neither face is the deforming face
\Sigma_{1}
, so that both faces are fixed,
we use the identity map.

Using these local
C^{1}
-diffeomorphisms,
the construction in Example
3.4
gives a local

C^{1}
-diffeomorphism
\Phi_{t}^{\mathrm{loc}}
defined in a sufficiently small neighborhood of the vertex, such that

\Phi_{t}^{\mathrm{loc}}(\Sigma_{1})=\Sigma_{t}

for the deforming face, while every fixed face is mapped to itself. Moreover,

d\Phi_{t}^{\mathrm{loc}}|_{0}=I,

and the family
\Phi_{t}^{\mathrm{loc}}
depends continuously on
t
.

Finally, we extend
\Phi_{t}^{\mathrm{loc}}
to a global polyhedral

C^{1}
-diffeomorphism

\Phi_{t}\colon{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}^{(1)}\longrightarrow{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}^{(t)}.

Choose the vertex neighborhood slightly larger than the support of the
surface deformation. Since the deformation of the face is supported in

B_{r_{0}}(0)
, and the faces are fixed outside this ball,

\Phi_{t}^{\mathrm{loc}}
may be chosen to be the identity near the boundary of
a slightly larger ball. We then extend it by the identity outside that ball.

Let
E_{t}=S(T{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}^{(t)}\oplus\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{3}).

Because
\Phi_{t}
is the identity map outside of the flat ball
B_{r_{0}}(0)
, the pullback bundle
\Phi_{t}^{*}E_{t}
and
E_{1}=S(T{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}^{(1)}\oplus\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{3})
are naturally identified. Let
U_{t}
be the induced unitary map

U_{t}\colon L^{2}({\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}^{(t)},E_{t})\longrightarrow L^{2}({\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}^{(1)},E_{1}).

Let
D_{t}
be the Dirac operator on
{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}^{(t)}
, and set

\widetilde{D}_{t}=U_{t}D_{t}U_{t}^{-1}.

Then
\widetilde{D}_{t}
is a first-order elliptic operator on

E_{1}
over
{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}^{(1)}
. Its coefficients are continuous at the vertex,
smooth away from the vertex, and depend continuously on
t
.

It remains to track the boundary condition. Extend the auxiliary unit-length section
\mathbf{n}
with value in
\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
to a smooth unit-length section
\widetilde{\mathbf{n}}
on a tubular
neighborhood of
\partial\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
in the ambient manifold. After shrinking

r_{0}
if necessary, all deformed faces remain in this tubular neighborhood. Define

(6.5)

\mathbf{n}_{t}=\widetilde{\mathbf{n}}|_{\partial{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}^{(t)}}.

Let
B_{t}
be the boundary condition on
{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}^{(t)}
determined by the
unit inner normals to the faces of
{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}^{(t)}
and the auxiliary
field
\mathbf{n}_{t}
. Let
\widetilde{B}_{t}
be its pullback to
{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}^{(1)}

under
\Phi_{t}
. The boundary conditions
\widetilde{B}_{t}
vary continuously in
t
, and the
construction from Theorem
3.5
gives Lipschitz bundle
isomorphisms

\Theta_{t}\colon E_{1}\longrightarrow E_{1}

mapping
B_{1}
to
\widetilde{B}_{t}
. By the same argument from
Step 1
and
Step 2
,
\textup{Ind}((\widetilde{D}_{t})_{\widetilde{B}_{t}})
is independent of
t
; equivalently,
\textup{Ind}((D_{t})_{B_{t}})
is is independent of
t
.

Applying this deformation successively to all codimension-one faces through
all vertices gives a polyhedral manifold
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}^{\prime},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{h})
whose metric
and codimension-one faces are flat near the vertices, and whose associated
Dirac operator has the same Fredholm index as the original one at the
beginning of this step. See Figure
1
in Section
1
.

Step 4: Cutting off small neighborhoods of the vertices.

At this point, we have reduced to the case in which the metric
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}
of
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g})
is
flat in a small neighborhood of each vertex, and all codimension-one faces
meeting a given vertex are flat in that neighborhood.

Let
x
be a vertex of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
, and choose a sufficiently small flat
neighborhood

U_{x}=B_{\delta}(x)\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}.

Using the flat metric on
U_{x}
, we identify

T\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}|_{U_{x}}\simeq U_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}.

Thus the auxiliary trivial bundle
\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
may be identified
with
T\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}|_{U_{x}}
over
U_{x}
. Under this identification, the section
\mathbf{n}
of the auxiliary bundle
\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
can be
equivalently viewed as a tangent vector field on
U_{x}
.

Choose a hypersurface
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
as in
Example
5.5
, contained in

B_{\varepsilon}(x)\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},\qquad 0<\varepsilon<\delta.

Before cutting along
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
, we make a local deformation of the
auxiliary unit-length section
\mathbf{n}
near
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
. We choose this
deformation so that:

(i)

\mathbf{n}
is tangent to
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
along each curve

\Sigma_{\varepsilon}\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
;

(ii)

and
\langle\mathbf{n},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k}\rangle>0

along each flat face
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{H}_{k}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}\cap B_{\delta}(x)
, where
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k}
is the unit inner normal of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
.

This deformation can be chosen through smooth unit-length sections
supported in
U_{x}
. By the same argument as in
Step 1
, this local deformation does not change the Fredholm
index.

We now cut
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
along
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
. This gives two pieces,

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}\cup_{\Sigma_{\varepsilon}}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{2}.

Here
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}
is the piece containing the vertex, while
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{2}
is the truncated remainder.

Let
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{\Sigma}
be the unit normal vector of
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
pointing out of

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}
. Equivalently,
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{\Sigma}
is the unit inner normal vector of

\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
when
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
is viewed as a boundary face of

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{2}
. We use the same vector
\nu_{\Sigma}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{\Sigma}
, under the Euclidean
identification above, as the auxiliary vector of
\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
on
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
.
Because
\mathbf{n}
is tangent to
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
along

\Sigma_{\varepsilon}\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
, we have

\langle\mathbf{n},\nu_{\Sigma}\rangle=0\qquad\text{along }\Sigma_{\varepsilon}\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}.

Thus the hypotheses of the gluing theorem, Theorem
4.1
, are
satisfied. Hence

\textup{Ind}(D_{B}^{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}})=\textup{Ind}(D_{B_{1}}^{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}})+\textup{Ind}(D_{B_{2}}^{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{2}}),

where
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
are the boundary conditions defined by
Theorem
4.1
. In particular, along
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
, the
condition on
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{2}
is the absolute condition, while the condition on

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}
is the complementary condition.

Claim 6.5
.

We have
\textup{Ind}(D_{B_{1}}^{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}})=0.

Proof.

The codimension-one faces of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}
consist of the flat faces

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{H}_{1},\ldots,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{H}_{\ell}

together with
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
. Let
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k}
be the unit inner
normal to
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{H}_{k}
. We also regard
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k}
as a section of the
auxiliary trivial bundle
\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
, and denote this auxiliary section by

\nu_{k}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k}.

By the local deformation arranged above, the auxiliary field
\mathbf{n}
satisfies

\langle\mathbf{n},\nu_{k}\rangle>0

on each flat face
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{H}_{k}
. Therefore Proposition
6.3

applies to the collection
\{\nu_{k}\}
with north pole
\mathbf{n}
. We obtain
smooth unit-length sections
\nu_{k,t}
,
t\in[0,1]
, such that

\nu_{k,0}=\nu_{k},\qquad\nu_{k,1}=\mathbf{n},

and, for
t>0
,

\langle\nu_{i,t},\nu_{j,t}\rangle>\langle\nu_{i},\nu_{j}\rangle=\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{i},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{j}\rangle

along every intersection
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{H}_{i}\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{H}_{j}
.

We keep the auxiliary vector
\nu_{\Sigma}
on
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
fixed during
this deformation. Since
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
meets each flat face

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{H}_{k}
orthogonally, the vector
\nu_{k}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k}
is tangent to
