\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
along
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{H}_{k}
. By construction,

\mathbf{n}
is also tangent to
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
along the same curve. The
explicit formula in Proposition
6.3

therefore shows that
\nu_{k,t}
remains tangent to
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
along

\Sigma_{\varepsilon}\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{H}_{k}
for all
t
. Hence

\langle\nu_{k,t},\nu_{\Sigma}\rangle=0=\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{\Sigma}\rangle

along
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{H}_{k}
.

Let
\widetilde{B}_{t}
be the boundary condition on
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}
determined by
the normals
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k}
, the auxiliary fields
\nu_{k,t}
on the
flat faces, and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{\Sigma}
and
\nu_{\Sigma}
along
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}
. For

t>0
, the strict inner product comparison holds along intersections of flat faces
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{H}_{i}\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{H}_{j}
, while
the equality case holds along intersections
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{H}_{k}
.
Thus Theorem
3.15
applies, and

D_{\widetilde{B}_{t}}^{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}}
is Fredholm for every
t>0
.

Moreover, the same boundary-condition deformation argument used in
Step 1

shows that

\textup{Ind}(D_{\widetilde{B}_{t}}^{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}})

is constant for
t\in(0,1]
.

At
t=0
, the boundary condition
\widetilde{B}_{0}
is the mixed boundary
condition of Lemma
5.6
. Therefore
D_{\widetilde{B}_{0}}^{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}}
is invertible,
and
\widetilde{B}_{0}
is extremal in the sense of
Definition
5.1
. Since

\widetilde{B}_{t}\to\widetilde{B}_{0}
in Lipschitz norm, Corollary
5.3

implies that
D_{\widetilde{B}_{t}}^{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}}
is invertible for all sufficiently
small
t>0
. Hence

\textup{Ind}(D_{\widetilde{B}_{t}}^{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}})=0

for all sufficiently small
t>0
. Since the index is constant on
(0,1]
, we
obtain

\textup{Ind}(D_{B_{1}}^{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}})=\textup{Ind}(D_{\widetilde{B}_{1}}^{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}_{1}})=0.

This proves the claim.
∎

Applying this argument at every vertex of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
, we obtain a truncated
manifold
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}^{\prime}
and a boundary condition
B^{\prime}
such that

\textup{Ind}(D_{B}^{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}})=\textup{Ind}(D_{B^{\prime}}^{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}^{\prime}}).

See Figure
2
and
3
in Section
1
. The codimension-one faces of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}^{\prime}
are of two types:

(1)

If a face
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}^{\prime}
is contained in an original face

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
, then the boundary condition is

\mathscr{E}\,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k})c(\mathbf{n})\sigma=-\sigma,

where
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k}
is the unit inner normal to
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}^{\prime}
.

(2)

If a face is one of the cutting hypersurfaces

\Sigma_{\varepsilon}(x_{j})
, then the boundary condition is

\mathscr{E}\,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{\Sigma})c(\nu_{\Sigma})\sigma=-\sigma,

where, using the local Euclidean identification,

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{\Sigma}=\nu_{\Sigma}

is the unit normal inner of the cutting hypersurface
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}^{\prime}
.

Step 5. Product deformation along the original edges of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
.

Cutting off the vertex neighborhoods in
Step 4
creates new edges and truncates
the original edges of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
. To avoid ambiguity, let

\mathcal{E}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}
denote the collection of edge segments in the truncated
manifold
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}^{\prime}
in
Step 4
that arise from the original edges of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
. The new
edges contained in the cutting hypersurfaces

\Sigma_{\varepsilon}(x_{j})
are not included in
\mathcal{E}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}
.

Let
\Gamma\in\mathcal{E}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}
. We deform a sufficiently small tubular
neighborhood of
\Gamma
into a genuine Riemannian product

I\times\mathbb{G},

where
I
is a compact interval and
\mathbb{G}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
is a flat
sector. At the end of the deformation,
\Gamma
corresponds to

I\times\{0\}
, the two original faces meeting along
\Gamma
correspond to
the two side faces
I\times\partial\mathbb{G}
, and the cutting hypersurfaces
at the endpoints correspond to the end faces

\partial I\times\mathbb{G}
.

Suppose that

\Gamma=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{1}\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{2}

and that its endpoints lie on the cutting hypersurfaces

\Sigma_{\varepsilon}(x_{i})
and
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}(x_{j})
. We perform the
deformation in two stages.

(1)

We first deform the metric in a sufficiently small tubular
neighborhood of
\Gamma
to a flat product metric. The deformation is
chosen to be fixed near the two end faces, where the metric is already
flat. The faces remain transverse throughout the deformation, and the
auxiliary section on both side faces is the same unit-length section
\mathbf{n}
.
The deformation argument from

Step 2
therefore shows that the Fredholm index remains unchanged.

(2)

We next deform the faces
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{1}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{2}
so that
the neighborhood becomes a genuine product
I\times\mathbb{G}
.

By Example
5.5
, the end faces

\Sigma_{\varepsilon}(x_{i})
and
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}(x_{j})
are flat near
their intersections with
\Gamma
and are orthogonal to
\Gamma
. Let

\alpha_{i}
and
\alpha_{j}
denote the dihedral angles between

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{1}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{2}
near the two endpoints of
\Gamma
. If

\alpha_{i}\neq\alpha_{j}
, we first deform one of the side faces in a small
collar of one endpoint so that the two end angles agree. This deformation
may be realized by rotating the corresponding cross-sectional ray about

\Gamma
. It fixes
\Gamma
and the end face, preserves the
orthogonality to the end face, and may be chosen so that the dihedral
angle remains in
(0,\pi)
throughout the deformation.

Once the two end angles agree, we flatten
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{1}
and

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{2}
within a small tubular neighborhood of
\Gamma
by the same procedure
used in
Step 3
. The resulting neighborhood is isometric to

I\times\mathbb{G}
.

During this face deformation, the auxiliary section
\mathbf{n}
is
deformed as in
Step 3
; see (
6.5
).
The deformation may be chosen so that

\mathbf{n}_{t}
remains tangent to

\Sigma_{\varepsilon}(x_{i})
and
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}(x_{j})
along their
intersections with the side faces. Thus, for
a=1,2
and

\ell\in\{i,j\}
,

\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{a,t},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{\Sigma_{\varepsilon}(x_{\ell})}\rangle=0=\langle\mathbf{n}_{t},\nu_{\Sigma_{\varepsilon}(x_{\ell})}\rangle

along

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{a}\cap\Sigma_{\varepsilon}(x_{\ell})
.
Consequently, the strict inner product comparison holds along
\Gamma
, while
the equality case holds along the edges
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{a}\cap\Sigma_{\varepsilon}(x_{\ell})
.
Theorem
3.15
therefore applies throughout the
deformation.

Finally, the same argument from
Step 3
shows that the Fredholm index remains unchanged during this
face-flattening deformation.

Choosing the tubular neighborhoods sufficiently small, and performing the
construction successively for all
\Gamma\in\mathcal{E}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}
, we obtain
a manifold for which a neighborhood of every edge in
\mathcal{E}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}
is a genuine
product
I\times\mathbb{G}
, without changing the Fredholm index.

Step 6. Smoothing the edges of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}

Let

be one of the edges treated in

. A sufficiently

is now a genuine Riemannian product

where

and

is a flat sector. Choose

sufficiently small, and set

K_{\varepsilon}=B_{\varepsilon}(0)\cap\mathbb{G},\qquad R_{\varepsilon}=\partial B_{\varepsilon}(0)\cap\mathbb{G},\qquad C_{\varepsilon}=I\times R_{\varepsilon}.

We will cut along the hypersurface

, following the same

.

Let
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{1}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{2}
be the two side faces meeting along

. Before cutting, we make a local deformation of the auxiliary field

\mathbf{n}
on these two faces. The deformation is supported in small

and is disjoint from the edge

. We arrange that, near these

is deformed to become the unit inner normal
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k}
of

. Moreover, the deformation is chosen relative to the two end faces

so that

remains tangent to these end faces along their intersections
with
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{1}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{2}
. The auxiliary vector
\nu_{\Sigma}
(see item (
2
) at the end of

)on each of the end faces
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}(x_{i})
and
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}(x_{j})
is kept fixed. The same argument from
Step 1
and
Step 2
shows that the index of the associated Dirac operator (subject to the corresponding boundary condition) remains constant throughout the deformation.

\Gamma
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{1}
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{2}
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}(x_{i})
\Sigma_{\varepsilon}(x_{j})
\mathbf{n}
\mathbf{n}
\mathbf{n}
\mathbf{n}
\mathbf{n}
\mathbf{n}

of an edge
\Gamma
.

along

\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{1}
and

.

We now cut
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}^{\prime}
along

. This gives
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}^{\prime}=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}^{\prime}_{1}\cup_{C_{\varepsilon}}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}^{\prime}_{2}
, where

Let
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{R}

. Using the natural identification of the auxiliary trivial bundle

Moreover, the auxiliary vector

on each end face is its geometric inner normal, which is also orthogonal to

. Hence all hypotheses of

are satisfied, and

\textup{Ind}(D_{B^{\prime}}^{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}^{\prime}})=\textup{Ind}(D_{B^{\prime}_{1}}^{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}^{\prime}_{1}})+\textup{Ind}(D_{B^{\prime}_{2}}^{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}^{\prime}_{2}}).

Claim 6.6
.

\textup{Ind}(D^{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}^{\prime}_{1}}_{B^{\prime}_{1}})=0
.

Proof.

The codimension-one faces of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}^{\prime}_{1}=I\times K_{\varepsilon}
are the two
side faces
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{1}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{2}
, the two end faces

\{0\}\times K_{\varepsilon},\qquad\{1\}\times K_{\varepsilon},

and the cylindrical face
C_{\varepsilon}=I\times R_{\varepsilon}
.
Let
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k}
denote the unit inner normal vector of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
. Let
\nu_{k}
also denote the unit inner normal vector of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
, but viewed as a section of the auxiliary trivial bundle
\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
(which has been identified with
T\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}^{\prime}_{1}
). By the arrangement made in
Steps 4
and
5
, and preserved by the preliminary
deformation above, the current auxiliary field
\mathbf{n}
satisfies

\langle\mathbf{n},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k}\rangle>0

on each side face. Proposition
6.3
therefore gives smooth unit vector fields
\nu_{k,t}
,
t\in[0,1]
on
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
such that:

•

\nu_{k,0}=\nu_{k}
and
\nu_{k,1}=\mathbf{n}
,

•

\langle\nu_{k,t},\mathbf{n}\rangle>0
for all
t\in[0,1]
and every
k\in\{1,2\}
, and

•

\langle\nu_{1,t},\nu_{2,t}\rangle>\langle\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\rangle
along
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{1}\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{2}
for all
t\in(0,1]
.

The auxiliary vectors on the end faces
\{0\}\times K_{\varepsilon}
and

\{1\}\times K_{\varepsilon}
, and
C_{\varepsilon}
are kept fixed. By construction, for every
t>0
, the strict inner product comparison holds along
the edge
\Gamma=\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{1}\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{2}
, while the equality case holds along all remaining edges of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}^{\prime}_{1}
. By Theorem
3.15
, the corresponding
operators
D_{\widetilde{B}_{t}}^{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}^{\prime}_{1}}
are essentially self-adjoint and
Fredholm for
t>0
. Now the same argument used in the proof of Claim
6.5
shows that
\textup{Ind}(D^{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}^{\prime}_{1}}_{B^{\prime}_{1}})=0
.
∎

Repeating this construction for every edge segment in

\mathcal{E}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}
gives a manifold
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}^{\prime\prime}
, obtained by deleting the
interiors of the product neighborhoods
I\times K_{\varepsilon}
, with boundary
condition
B^{\prime\prime}
, such that

\textup{Ind}(D_{B^{\prime}}^{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}^{\prime}})=\textup{Ind}(D_{B^{\prime\prime}}^{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}^{\prime\prime}}).

We now fill each newly created cylindrical boundary component
I\times R_{\varepsilon}
as follows. Choose a
smooth convex domain
U\subset\mathbb{G}
whose boundary consists of the circular
arc
R_{\varepsilon}
and a smooth convex curve
\Lambda
. See the lower left picture in Figure
15
or the right picture in Figure
6
. We require that

R_{\varepsilon}
and

\Lambda
meet orthogonally at their endpoints. The curve
\Lambda
replaces
the corner at the origin of
\mathbb{G}
by a smooth convex arc.

Form the product
I\times U
and glue it to
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}^{\prime\prime}
along the common
boundary hypersurface
I\times R_{\varepsilon}
. Let
{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}^{U}
denote the
resulting manifold. Extend the auxiliary field
\mathbf{n}
from the adjacent side faces
to a smooth unit vector field
\widetilde{\mathbf{n}}
on the new face

I\times\Lambda
. We choose this extension so that (see Figure
15
):

(i)

it is tangent to the end faces
\{0\}\times U
and

\{1\}\times U
along
\{0\}\times\Lambda
and
\{1\}\times\Lambda
;

(ii)

it has positive inner product with the unit inner normal of

I\times\Lambda
.

R_{\varepsilon}
\Lambda

Figure 15.
Cross-sections of the cutting and pasting construction.
Upper left: the product sector with cutting arc
R_{\varepsilon}
. Upper right:
the two pieces obtained after cutting. Lower left: the smoothing domain
U
,
bounded by
R_{\varepsilon}
and
\Lambda
, together with the extension

\widetilde{\mathbf{n}}
. Lower right: the cross-section after gluing

I\times U
to the truncated manifold.

The auxiliary vector fields on the end faces are simply chosen to be the unit inner normal of the end faces, by using the natural identification of the auxiliary trivial bundle
\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
with the flat tangent bundle
T(I\times U)
. The gluing formula (Theorem
4.1
) gives

\textup{Ind}(D^{{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}^{U}}_{B})=\textup{Ind}(D^{I\times U}_{B_{U}})+\textup{Ind}(D^{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}^{\prime\prime}}_{B^{\prime\prime}}),

where
B
,
B_{U}
, and
B^{\prime\prime}
are the corresponding boundary conditions.

The same argument from the proof of Claim
6.6
shows that
\textup{Ind}(D^{I\times U}_{B_{U}})=0
. Therefore, we have

\textup{Ind}(D^{{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}^{U}}_{B})=\textup{Ind}(D^{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}^{\prime\prime}}_{B^{\prime\prime}}).

Performing this gluing construction at every cylindrical boundary component
produces a manifold
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}^{\prime\prime\prime}
with boundary condition
B^{\prime\prime\prime}
such that

\textup{Ind}(D_{B^{\prime\prime\prime}}^{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}^{\prime\prime\prime}})=\textup{Ind}(D_{B^{\prime\prime}}^{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}^{\prime\prime}}).

Step 7. Resolving the remaining singularities.

By construction,
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}^{\prime\prime\prime}
has no codimension-three strata. Consequently,
each connected component of its codimension-two faces is a compact
one-dimensional manifold without boundary, and hence is a circle.

Let
\gamma
be one such component. After choosing a trivialization of its
oriented normal two-plane bundle, a sufficiently small tubular neighborhood of

\gamma
may be identified with a sector bundle over
\mathbb{S}^{1}
. We first
apply the metric and face deformations from
Step 5
, with
\mathbb{S}^{1}
in
place of the interval
I
, to deform this neighborhood into a genuine product

\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{G}.

We then repeat the cutting and pasting construction of
Step 6
. The auxiliary
unit-length section
\mathbf{n}
is deformed and extended similarly as in that step.

The same argument as in
Step 6
shows that this construction does not change the Fredholm index. Performing the construction
in pairwise disjoint tubular neighborhoods of all remaining codimension-two
faces produces a manifold
{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}^{s}
with smooth boundary, together with an
auxiliary unit-length field
\mathbf{n}^{s}
, such that

\textup{Ind}(D_{B^{\prime\prime\prime}}^{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}^{\prime\prime\prime}})=\textup{Ind}(D_{B^{s}}^{{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}^{s}}).

Conclusion.

Throughout
Steps 1–7
, the auxiliary vector field
\mathbf{n}
is deformed, extended, and modified in a manner that preserves the degree of the associated boundary map
\mathbf{n}\colon\partial\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}\to\mathbb{S}^{2}
. Consequently,

\deg(\mathbf{n}^{s})=\deg(\mathbf{n}).

Recall that
\mathbf{n}
is the pullback under
f
of the unit vector field defined in (
6.1
), regarded as a section of the trivial bundle

\partial\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}.

By construction,

\deg(-\mathbf{n})=\deg(f).

Hence,

\deg(-\mathbf{n}^{s})=\deg(-\mathbf{n})=\deg(f).

Since
{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}^{s}
has smooth boundary, the classical index theorem for manifolds with smooth boundary gives

\textup{Ind}\bigl(D^{{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}^{s}}_{B^{s}}\bigr)=\deg(-\mathbf{n}^{s}).

Therefore,

\textup{Ind}(D_{B})=\textup{Ind}\bigl(D^{{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}}^{s}}_{B^{s}}\bigr)=\deg(-\mathbf{n}^{s})=\deg(-\mathbf{n})=\deg(f).

This completes the proof of the theorem.
∎

With all the necessary ingredients now established, we are ready to prove Theorem
1.2
.

Theorem 6.7

(Theorem
1.2
)
.

Let
(M,g)
be a convex polyhedron in the Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, where
g
is the Euclidean metric. Let
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g})
be a spin polyhedral manifold and
f\colon\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}\to M
be a polyhedral map with non-zero degree, such that the scalar curvature, mean curvature, and dihedral angles satisfies

\mathrm{Sc}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}}\geq 0,~H_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}}\geq 0,~\theta_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}}\leq f^{*}\theta_{g},

then
\mathrm{Sc}_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}}=0
,
H_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}}=0
, and
\theta_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}}=f^{*}\theta_{g}
. Moreover,
(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g})
is flat.

Proof.

Let
E=S(T\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}\oplus f^{*}TM)=S(T\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}\oplus\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{3})
and let
D
be the Dirac operator associated with
E
. Let
B
be the boundary condition given by

\mathscr{E}\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k})c(\nu_{k})\sigma=-\sigma\quad\textup{on }\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k},

where
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k}
is the unit inner normal vector field of each codimension-one face
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}
of
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
, and
\nu_{k}
is the unit inner normal vector field of the corresponding face
F_{k}
of
M
.

Let
\mathbf{n}
the be auxiliary vector field given in (
6.1
). By Proposition
6.3
, there exists, on each face

F_{k}
, a smooth family of unit-length sections
\nu_{k,t}
taking value in
\underline{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
for
t\in[0,1]
, such
that

\nu_{k,0}=\nu_{k},\qquad\nu_{k,1}=\mathbf{n},

and the pairwise inner products

\langle\nu_{i,t},\nu_{j,t}\rangle

are non-decreasing in
t
. Pulling these sections back by
f
, define the
boundary condition
B_{t}
on
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M}
by

\mathscr{E}\,\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{c}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{k})c(\nu_{k,t})\sigma=-\sigma\qquad\text{on }\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{k}.

The assumption that
\theta_{\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{g}}\leq f^{*}\theta_{g}
implies that

\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{i}(x),\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{j}(x)\rangle\leq\langle\nu_{i}(f(x)),\nu_{j}(f(x))\rangle

at every
x\in\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{i}\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{j}
for each pair of adjacent codimension-one faces
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{i}
and
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{j}
. Proposition
6.3
therefore imply that, for every

t>0
,

\langle\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{i}(x),\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{}_{j}(x)\rangle<\langle\nu_{i,t}(f(x)),\nu_{j,t}(f(x))\rangle

along each edge
\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{i}\cap\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{F}_{j}
.

It follows from Theorem
3.15
that, for every
t>0
, the
operator
D
with boundary condition
B_{t}
is essentially self-adjoint and
Fredholm. Moreover, Theorem
6.4
gives

\textup{Ind}(D_{B_{t}})=\deg(f).

Since
\deg(f)\neq 0
, it follows that
\textup{Ind}(D_{B_{t}})
is non-zero for all
t\in(0,1]
. Therefore, for each
t\in(0,1]
, there exists a non-zero
\sigma_{t}\in H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E;B_{t})
such that
D\sigma_{t}=0
.

The family
B_{t}
converges to
B_{0}=B
in Lipschitz norm as
t\to 0
. Moreover, by Proposition
2.16
, the boundary condition
B=B_{0}
is extremal in the sense of Definition
5.1
. By Lemma
5.2
, there exists a non-zero
\sigma\in H^{1}(\macc@depth\char 1\relax\macc@set@skewchar\macc@nested@a 111{M},E;B)
such that
\nabla\sigma=0
. In particular,
D\sigma=0
. The conclusion of the theorem now follows immediately from Lemma
2.17
.
∎
